\section{Introduction}\label{s:intro}








A Succinct Non-interactive Argument of Knowledge (SNARK) for a relation $\Relation$ is a non-interactive protocol that enables a prover to convince a verifier that, for a given public input $\inp$, they know a witness $\wit$ such that $(\inp,\wit)\in \Relation$, and where communication and verifier runtime are sublinear (i.e.\ the scheme is succinct).

In the vast majority of scenarios, $\Relation$ is defined over one (or more) algebraic object $R$, usually a finite field, and $(\inp,\wit)\in \Relation$ if and only if $(\inp,\wit)$ consists of elements that satisfy some polynomial equations, called  constraints. The choice of $R$ has a crucial impact on the design and end-to-end performance of the proof system, for two reasons: 
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
\item
\label{i:arithmetization}
\textbf{(Non-)Nativity of the computation.} Usually the computations one wants to prove are not native to $R$, so one has to perform a more or less complex emulation of the computation into polynomial equations over $R$. This can affect the size of the input-witness pair $\inp,\wit$ significantly \cite{??}. Additionally, the emulation step can be  technically involved and prone to  security errors \cite{??}.  
\item
\label{i:field:performance}
\textbf{Proof system efficiency.} The efficiency and design of the prover and verifier algorithms are affected by the choice of $R$: for example, modern CPUs are more performant for operations in certain small fields (e.g.\ M31 \cite{cryptoeprint:2023/824,CircleSTARKs}, Babybear \cite{risc0:whitepaper}, Goldilocks \cite{plonky2:repo}) than in arbitrary fields, especially if these are larger. Importantly, another class of fields where CPUs operate exceptionally fast are \emph{binary fields}, i.e.\ fields of order a power of two.
%
\end{enumerate}
\noindent Recently, two independent lines of research have received increased attention:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
    \item Designing proof systems for relations expressed over  the ring of integers $R=\ZZ$, and over other rings that are not fields \cite{zaratan,zinc,zincplus,qaren,limber,more}. The motivation behind this is to address \cref{i:arithmetization} above: in many practical scenarios, the computation to be proved is most succinctly and easily expressed over such rings, see \cite{zinc,zincplus,limber} \albert{more refs?}. 
    \item Designing proof systems for $R=\comfield$ a binary field, for example \cite{stark,cryptoeprint:2022/1542,binius2,hashcaster:repo,flock}. The reason for this is twofold: first, some computations like classic hashes (SHA-256, Blake, etc.) are easier to express over  $\comfield$ than over other structures such as prime fields (however, these hash functions remain relatively un-native to  $\comfield$ due to the hardness of emulating operations modulo $2^{32}$ or $2^{64}$). Second, binary fields are highly performant on modern CPU's, and further enable using a boon of protocol techniques that further benefit perfomance, most importantly \textit{packed} polynomial commitment schemes \cite{binius,binius2}. 
    %and sumcheck optimizations \cite{yuval,more?}.
\end{itemize}


\subsection{Our contributions}


 In this work we bring the  two aforementioned lines of research together. Concretely, we  present \ftwoz, a hash-based Polynomial Commitment Scheme (PCS) that can commit to a witness vector $\bff \in S^\codedim$ over an arbitrary finitely generated ring $S$. For example, $S$ can be any finite field $\FF$, the ring of integers $\ZZ$, a cyclotomic ring (for lattice-based cryptography), the integers modulo $2^{32}$, etc.

 \ftwoz \ can then prove linear constraints on $\bff$, such as Multilinear Extension (MLE) evaluation claims, over $S$, of the form $$\langle \bff, \vv\rangle = \mu \quad \text{in } S$$ for some public $\vv\in S^\codedim, \mu\in S$.
 More generally, given another finitely generated ring $R$ and a ring homomorphism $\psi: S\to R$, \ftwoz\ can prove claims of the form $$\langle \psi(\bff), \vv\rangle = \mu \quad \text{in } R$$ for public $\vv\in R^{\codedim}, \mu\in R$. For example, $S$ could be the ring of integers, $R$ could be a prime field $\FF_q$, and $\psi$ could be reduction modulo $q$. 
 \bigskip
 


We implement \ftwoz\ and use it, among others (see below), to build a SNARK for proving R1CS constraints over the integers, following the fingerprinting paradigm from Campanelli and Hall-Andersen \cite{zaratan}. Concretely, we commit to witnesses over the integers (setting $S=\ZZ$); then the verifier samples a random prime $q$; then the R1CS is projected to $\FF_q$ through the morphism $\pi_q$ consisting in reduction modulo $q$; then a PIOP is run on the resulting R1CS, yielding a linear claim (an MLE claim) on the projected witness $\pi_q(\bff)$ of the form $\langle \pi_q(\bff), \vv\rangle = \mu$; finally, this claim is proved with \ftwoz. The commitment is made before $q$ is sampled, which is required for the soundness of the fingerprinting technique. The PIOP may output its claim over a field extension of $\FF_q$, which \ftwoz\ can prove (\cref{s:to_general_rings}). An example end-to-end SNARK is described in \cref{s:to_snark}. 

We also build a SNARK that can prove constraints simultaneously over the integers and binary fields, and which uses \ftwoz\ as PCS. This SNARK also supports \emph{virtualization} of $\FF_2$ addition (XOR) between integer witness.  We discuss this further in \cref{s:intro_hybrid_proofs}.  We emphasize that \ftwoz\ can also be used to prove more exotic constraints, such as lattice-type operations, see \cref{s:to_general_bitz}. Overall, our \ftwoz\-based SNARKs support highly expressible constraints that allow to  arithmetize many computations of interest  with little technical complexity, and with small circuits. 

The table below shows highlight benchmark results of our end-to-end proving system, which uses \ftwoz as PCS. We compare against the most performant prior SNARKs we could find for each task at hand. Perhaps surprisingly, in our experiments we find that, when proving integer constraints, Binius64 \cite{binius64}, a proof system fully native to binary fields, greatly outperforms other schemes that are specifically designed for the task. 

Note that multiplication modulo $2^n$, say $x \cdot y = z \mod 2^n$, can be arithmetized over the integers as the constraint $x\cdot y = z + 2^n\cdot w $ with $w$ being a new witness element. We discuss proving SHA-256 below.

\noindent \ftwoz\ presents the following properties:
 \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
     \item  \textbf{Pay-per-bit and algebraic independence.} Its performance  depends essentially exclusively on the bit-size of the witness $\bff$. In particular, the choice of ring $S$ (and $R$ and $\psi$ if it applies) has a tiny impact on its performance. 
     \item \textbf{Composability and universality.} Because of its flexibility in its choice of rings, and because of the point above, \ftwoz\  can be used as a PCS in essentially any proof system. This includes standard proof systems based on finite fields, or more exotic ones working over different rings, e.g.\ the integers \cite{zaratan,limber,zinc,zincplus,qaren}, polynomial rings \cite{zincplus}, lattices \cite{?}, etc.
     
     For example, a proof system using a PCS that is not plausibly post-quantum secure, could replace its PCS with \ftwoz \ (which is plausibly post-quantum secure), regardless of the proof system's underlying field. As mentioned above, the underlying rings barely affect \ftwoz's performance. %\albertimportant{Remove?} %Further, a proof system over a large  field  can lift its constraints to $\ZZ$ (or to $\ZZ[X]$) and project them onto a random prime field of $\approx\lambda$ bits before running its PIOP, so that the PIOP runs over a small field and the \ftwoz\ opening pays only for the bit-size of the lifted witness entries, which can be much smaller than $\log|R|$ (\cref{r:to_reducing_ring}). % [AI-drafted sentence (Claude Opus 5), 2026-09-11] \albertimportant{would be nice to give example numbers of doing this}
     \item \textbf{``Free'' range checks.} \ftwoz\ provides range checks ``for free''. For example, when  committing  to integer witnesses, \ftwoz\ exposes a bit-size parameter $B$. Then, \ftwoz\ can be used to commit to integers of that bit-size, and it automatically guarantees that a malicious prover cannot commit to larger integers. This allows to omit many range checks that a standard proof system would need to enforce.
\end{enumerate}



\subsection{\ftwoz\ in a nutshell}\label{s:intro_nutshell}

 \ftwoz \ proves the aforementioned claim $\langle \psi(\bff), \vv \rangle = \mu$ over $R$ as follows. Assume first that $R$ is a finite prime field $\FF=\FF_q$. We explain the general case later.  We  describe first an initial, mostly insufficient, attempt.
 \medskip
     \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,nosep]
         \item  \textbf{Bitification.} First, it transforms the  claim into a claim of the form  $\langle \psi(\bitstext(\bff)), \uu \rangle = \mu$ over $R$, for some $\uu$, and where $\bitstext(\bff)$ denotes a vector  with entries in $\{0,1\}\subseteq \ZZ$\footnote{$\psi(\bitstext(\bff))$ is the natural interpretation of integer bits as the $0$ or $1$ element of $R$.}. Intuitively, $\bitstext(\bff)$ is simply the result of  bit-decomposing the entries of $\bff$ in a natural way. This decomposition is linear, and so an inner product on $\bff$ equals an inner product on $\bitstext(\bff)$, for a different weight vector. For example, if $S=\ZZ$ and $\bff$ is filled with $B$ bit nonnegative integers, then $\uu=  \vv\otimes(1,2,\ldots, 2^{B-1})$.
         \item \textbf{Commitment.} The prover commits to $\bitstext(\bff)$ over a binary field $\comfield$ (by looking at integers bits as elements from $\FF_2$), using a packed commitment scheme such as WHIR \cite{whir} over $\comfield$, with the ring-switch techniques from \cite{binius,binius2}. Here \emph{packed commitment} means that every $\comfieldexp$ bits are grouped together into a single field element of $\comfield$, and that we encode and Merklize the vector of length $|\bitstext(\bff)|/\comfieldexp$ over $\comfield$.
         \item \textbf{Lift to integers.} Assuming $\FF$ is a prime field of size $q$ and $\psi:\ZZ\to \FF$ is reduction modulo $q$, the prover computes and sends $\mu'\in \ZZ$ such that $\langle \pi_q^{-1}(\uu), \bitstext(\bff)\rangle = \mu'$ in $\ZZ$.
         \item \textbf{Proof in the exponent of $\comfield$.} Now take a generator $g$ of the multiplicative subgroup $\comfield^*$, and prove that $g^{\langle \pi_q^{-1}(\uu), \bitstext(\bff)\rangle} = g^{\mu'}$ in $\comfield$. Concretely, we have
         \begin{equation}\label{e:intro}
         g^{\langle \pi_q^{-1}(\uu), \bitstext(\bff)\rangle} = \prod_{i} \left((g^{\pi_q^{-1}(\uu_i)} -1) \cdot \bitstext(\bff)_i + 1 \right) = g^{\mu'} \qquad \text{in } \comfield,
         \end{equation}
         which is a polynomial expression over $\comfield$ on the vector $\bitstext(\bff)$ that we committed over $\comfield$. 
         
         This is a standard setting where there is no ``wrong-field'' arithmetic, hence in principle one can use any proof system over $\comfield$ to prove \eqref{e:intro}.

     \end{enumerate}  

     \begin{remark}Proving constraints ``in the exponent''  is a well-known idea that goes back at least to \cite{binius64,lev}. One of our main contributions is 1) in what context this technique is used, namely inside the PCS to prove MLE evaluation claims (or inner product claims) of the witness, as opposed to proving the initial arithmetized computation, see \cref{r:comparison_grand_product_binius_lev} for more details;  2) the optimizations we design for such a specific ``proof in the exponent'' setting, see \cref{s:to_low_entropy_gkr,s:gkr_low_entropy}; and 3) extending it so it can be used to prove constraints over arbitrary rings, and not just, say, integers or \emph{prime} fields with small  values (to avoid overflowing the multiplicative order of $g$).\end{remark}
     
\noindent There are several issues with the attempt above:
\medskip
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*,nosep]
    \item \textbf{How to lift from general rings?} First, $R$ may not be a prime field, in which case there is no natural lift to integers in Step 3; rather, there is a lift to integer polynomials $\ZZ[X_1, \ldots, X_k]$. 
    \item \textbf{How to lift large values?} Second, even if $R=\FF_q$ is a prime field,  $q=|\FF|$ may be large and the linear expression $\langle \pi_q^{-1}(\uu), \bitstext(\bff)\rangle$ may overflow the order of $g\in \comfield^{*}$, which is $2^{\comfieldexp}-1$. In this case, proving  $\langle \pi_q^{-1}(\uu), \bitstext(\bff)\rangle = \mu'$  ``in the exponent of $g$'' is unsound. 
    \item \textbf{Expensive grand product.} Third, even if neither of these problems hold, \eqref{e:intro} is, in principle, an expensive constraint to prove: indeed, it is a length $|\bitstext(\bff)|$ grand product of, essentially, arbitrary factors from $\comfield$ (typically $\approx$128 bit elements).  Grand products can be expensive, and furthermore the fact that the factors are large prevents us from using known sumcheck optimizations such as univariate skips \cite{gruen} and others from \cite{flock,sumcheck_opt_1,sumcheck_opt_2}.
    \item \textbf{Non-succinct verifier.} Fourth, when proving \eqref{e:intro}, typically one ends up with an MLE evaluation claim on the vector $(g^{\pi_q^{-1}(\uu)_i})_i$ (and another on $\bitstext(\bff)$, which is proved with any PCS over $\comfield$). If the verifier checks the claim itself, then it pays a linear cost. If otherwise the prover has to prove this claim, then it is essentially back to square one.
\end{itemize}





\noindent We solve these issues as follows: 

\bigskip


\noindent \textbf{Issues 1 and 2:} These are solved by lifting the inner product claim to $\ZZ[X_1, \ldots, X_k]$ (after observing that all finitely generated rings are a projection of a ring such as $\ZZ[X_1, \ldots, X_k]$), and then projecting the resulting claim to a new claim over a random prime field. Then we proceed as in Steps 3 and 4 with this new claim over $\FF_{q'}$. 

\begin{comment}
\paragraph{Issues 1 and 2: lift to $\ZZ[X_1, \ldots, X_k]$ and project to a random prime field, then lift again.} If $R$ is a ring with generators $1, \gamma_1, \ldots, \gamma_k$, we define a canonical ring homomorphism $\picanon: \ZZ[X_1, \ldots, X_k] \to R$ as $\picanon(X_i) = \gamma_i$ and $\picanon(1)=1$, and at Step 3 we lift to a claim $\langle\bitstext(\bff), \picanon^{-1}(\uu) \rangle = \mu'$ over $\ZZ[X_1, \ldots, X_k]$ (for a prime field, $k=0$ and this is the integer claim of Step 3). Then the verifier sends a random prime $q'$ of $\approx |\comfield^*|/|\bitstext(\bff)|$ size (small enough to avoid the issue of overflowing in the exponent) and a random morphism $\pirand:\ZZ[X_1, \ldots, X_k] \to \FF_{q'}$ (e.g.\ reduction modulo $q'$ followed by evaluation at a random point), the claim is replaced by its image under $\pirand$, and we re-enter Step 3 with this new claim over $\FF_{q'}$.
\bigskip 
\end{comment}

\bigskip
 
\noindent \emph{\underline{128 bits of security using $\FF_{2^{128}}$, arbitrary rings $R$, and arbitrary sizes of the entries of $\vv$.}} We manage to instantiate \ftwoz\ with 128 bits of security, using $\comfield=\FF_{2^{128}}$, while having essentially no limitation on the size of the ring $R$ we start with.


\paragraph{Issues 3 and 4: batched low entropy grand products} We split  \eqref{e:intro} into $\approx\sqrt{|\bitstext(\bff)|}$ grand products of the same form as \eqref{e:intro}, so that, even though in total there are $|\bitstext(\bff)|$ factors among all of them, these factors can only take $\sqrt{|\bitstext(\bff)|}$ values. More concretely, we assume $\uu=\uu^{(1)} \otimes \uu^{(2)}$ tensor-decomposes into $\approx \sqrt{|\bitstext(\bff)|}$ sized factors. This is typically the case since $\vv$ is usually an ``eq'' vector, and $\uu=L^{\mathsf{T}}\cdot \vv$ for a highly structured matrix $L$\footnote{For example, in our previous example, $\uu= \vv \otimes (1,2,\ldots, 2^{B-1})$. If $\vv$ is an ``eq'' vector, then $\uu$ admits highly customizable tensor decompositions.}. Then, assuming the notation of Step 3 above, we aim to prove  in the exponent $\langle \pi_q^{-1}(\uu^{(1)}), \bitstext(\bff)_{*j}\rangle = \mu_j'$ in $\ZZ$  for all $j\in [|\uu^{(2)}|]$, such that $\sum_{j} \pi_q(\mu_j') \cdot \uu_j^{(2)} = \mu$, where we index   $\bitstext(\bff)$ with indices $i\in [|\uu^{(1)}|], j\in [|\uu^{(2)}|]$. We  set up a grand product for each of these claims, obtaining 
  \begin{equation}\label{e:intro_2}
         g^{\langle \pi_q^{-1}(\uu^{(1)}), \bitstext(\bff)_{*j}\rangle} = \prod_{i} \left((g^{\pi_q^{-1}(\uu_i^{(1)})} -1) \cdot \bitstext(\bff)_{ij} + 1\right) = g^{\mu_j'} \qquad \text{for all } j.
         \end{equation}
There are $|\bitstext(\bff)|$ factors in total, but only $\approx \sqrt{|\bitstext(\bff)|}$ different values these factors can take. 

We say \eqref{e:intro_2} is a \emph{low entropy batched grand product}. To prove \eqref{e:intro_2} we use a batched GKR protocol with the following  optimization, among others: because of the low-entropy phenomenon, we build several small lookup tables of size $O(\sqrt{|\bitstext(\bff)|})$ (of a few MB for $|\uu^{(1)}|=2^{16}$, see \cref{tab:gkr_tables}) which are used during the most expensive sumcheck rounds of the GKR protocol. Concretely, the last sumcheck (the most expensive one) uses several lookup tables to compute the first three messages of the prover; the second last sumcheck does the same for the first two prover messages (with two tables); and the third last sumcheck does the same for the first prover message (with one table).

\bigskip

\noindent \emph{\underline{The key point}:} The key is that these square-root sized tables can be used in each of the grand products from \eqref{e:intro_2}, producing a large amortization, and allowing us to build several tables for different sumcheck rounds, while keeping them in cache, where table reads are cheaper than field multiplications. 

This is enabled by the  decomposition of the initial claim into the subclaims $\langle \pi_q^{-1}(\uu^{(1)}), \bitstext(\bff)_{*j}\rangle = \mu_j'$. This decomposition is possible because we only seek to prove witness MLE evaluations (or inner products) in the exponent. The reason we only need this this is that we arithmetize over a variety of rings and run a PIOP over those rings, which reduced the intiial constraints to a \emph{linear} claim on the witness, which we then prove ``in the exponent''. 

Other proof systems using the ``proof in the exponent'' technique, e.g.\ \cite{binius64}, aim to prove entire constraints in the exponent. Ultimately, this means one has to prove products of the form $y\cdot x$ in the exponent, for $x,y$ two witness elements, while we only prove products $a\cdot x$, for $a$ a  value coming from a public vector admitting a square-root sized tensor decomposition. See \cref{r:comparison_grand_product_binius_lev} for more details. 




\bigskip

\noindent \emph{\underline{Issue 4.}} The above solution also takes care of Issue 4: the batched GKR protocol outputs MLE evaluation claims on $(g^{\pi_q^{-1}(\uu^{(1)})_i})_i$ (and $\bitstext(\bff)$, which is dealt with by the PCS over $\comfield$). This is an $O(\sqrt{|\bitstext(\bff)|})$ vector, and the verifier can check this claim by itself cheaply.


\subsubsection{Using \ftwoz\ to prove constraints with hybrid arithmetics}\label{s:intro_hybrid_proofs}


In our implementation, we use \ftwoz\ as a PCS that can simultaneously prove R1CS constraints over integers and binary fields. We explain how next. We stress again that one can use \ftwoz\ to build all sorts of proof systems for all sorts of constraints. Nothing forces one to use R1CS-like constraints and integers and binary fields. 

 \paragraph{Virtual $\FF_2$-linear transforms, NP-complete dually linear relations, and one arithmetization of SHA-256} At the end of the steps of \cref{s:to_simple_bitz}, we have a standard linear claim on $\bitstext(\bff)$ in $\comfield$, say \begin{equation}\label{e:intro_comfield_linear}\langle \bitstext(\bff), \uu'\rangle = \eta \quad \text{ in } \comfield.\end{equation} Say we would like to add an extra constraint, over $\comfield$, on $\bitstext(\bff)$ of the form $\bitstext(\bff) = M \cdot \bfg$ for some matrix $M$ and vector $\bfg$ with entries in $\FF_2$. Then, we could instead of committing to $\bitstext(\bff)$ in Step 1 of \cref{s:to_simple_bitz}, commit to $\bfg$. The claim  \eqref{e:intro_comfield_linear} becomes
 %
 $$
\langle \bfg, M^{\mathsf{T}}\uu'\rangle = \eta \quad \text{ in } \comfield,
 $$
 where $\mathsf{T}$ denotes transposition. This is a linear claim on $\bfg$, the vector we committed to. We can prove this claim with a standard PCS over $\comfield$ such as \cite{whir}. 
 
 To keep the verifier succinct, we need to ensure that $M\cdot \uu'$ admits a succinct representation. In our applications, the vector $\uu'$ admits such \cref{??}, and so one needs to make sure $M$ carries some succinct structure, such as for example a tensor decomposition, so that $M\cdot \uu'$ does not become a dense vector \albert{review paragraph}.  

 
 This allows \ftwoz\  to prove polynomial constraints over $R$ on witness vectors $\bff$ whose bit-decomposition is a virtual $\FF_2$-linear combination of a committed vector $\bfg\in \FF_2^{\codedim'}$. For example, \ftwoz \ can efficiently prove the following R1CS-like relation: 
%
\begin{align*}
&\Relation_{\mathsf{R1CS-bits-virtual}}(\ZZ)\\ & \qquad\qquad \defeq \left\{ \begin{aligned}
    &\inp= (\xx, A,B,C, M)\in \FF_2^{\inplen} \times (\ZZ^{m\times \codedim})^3 \times \FF_2^{\codedim \times (\inplen+\codedim')}\\
    & \wit = \bfg \in \bits^{\codedim'} \subseteq \FF_2^{\codedim'}
\end{aligned}\  \left| \  \begin{aligned} %&f=\Enc_\cC \circ \pack \circ \pi_2(\bff)\\ 
&(A\cdot \bff) \circ (B\cdot \bff) = C \cdot \bff\ \text{in } \ZZ \\
&\bitstext(\bff)= \left(  M\cdot \bfg\right)    \text{ in } \comfield\end{aligned} \right.\right\},
\end{align*}
%
A particularly interesting subclass of this relation is that obtained by setting $A=B=\mathbf{0}$, in which case the resulting relation, which we call the \emph{$\mathsf{CM}$ relation}, degenerates to linear constraints over $\ZZ$ and over $\FF_2$, i.e.\ a ``dually linear'' constraint. 

In \cref{r:CM_is_NP_complete} we show that the CM relation is NP-complete \albert{Or NP-hard?}. We arithmetize SHA-256 as an instance of the CM relation, with $4.4\times$ smaller witnesses than Binius64 \cite{binius64} and Flock \cite{flock} ($3.6\times$ smaller than the AI-optimized arithmetization), see \cref{tab:sha_arithmetization}.  We remark that despite this small arithmetization, when it comes to proving many classic hash compressions, we observe that binary field native proof systems such as Flock \cite{flock} and Binius64 \cite{binius64} perform better. Because of this, in \cref{s:hybrid_snark} we propose a proof system that can handle simultaneously constraints over integers (or other rings) and binary fields, and which uses \ftwoz\ as its PCS.



\begin{table}[H]
    \centering
    \begin{tabular}{l|c}
        SHA-256 arithmetization type & Witness bit-size per compression  \\ 
        \toprule
        $\mathsf{CM}$ & $7144$ bits \\
        \hline
        R1CS over $\comfield$ &  $31744$ bits
         \\
        \hline
        R1CS over $\comfield$, AI optimized &  $25470$ bits
    \end{tabular}
    \caption{Witness bit-size per SHA-256 compression as per two different arithmetizations. The $\mathsf{CM}$ row indicates the bit-size when arithmetizing SHA-256 via an instance of the $\mathsf{CM}$ relation (see \eqref{e:CM_rel}) using the methods from \cref{e:CM_rel,r:CM_is_NP_complete}, see \url{https://github.com/reilabs/freigen/blob/324935e/Freigen/Circuit/Examples/Sha256.lean} \albertimportant{Change this to avoid deanonymization in the EC submission}. The second row displays the witness bit-size in the  arithmetization from Binius64 \cite{binius64}, which is an R1CS constraint over a binary field. Flock's \cite{flock} arithmetization has 31400 bits per compression. The AI row refers to an optimized arithmetization found by an AI working against a Lean specification in the zkGolf competition \url{https://zk.golf/}.  }
    \label{tab:sha_arithmetization}
\end{table}




\subsection{Related work}\label{s:related_work}

\paragraph{Proof systems over integers and other rings.} In \cite{zaratan}, Campanelli and Hall-Andersen propose proving polynomial constraints over integers, by projecting them onto a random prime field (sampled by the verifier), a technique which the authors call \emph{fingerprinting}. Subsequent works have adopted this idea \cite{zinc,qaren,zincplus,limber}. \cite{zaratan} also proposes a hidden-order group based PCS suitable for their PIOP, similar to the ones in \cite{DARK,BlockDARK}. The PCS is not implemented and so we do not compare against it.

The Zinc \cite{zinc} work adopts \emph{fingerprinting} and designs a hash-based PCS over the rationals $\QQ$, suitable for the fingerprinting approach, providing the first relatively practical proof system over the integers. \cite{zinc} addresses subtle technical issues that arise when aiming to commit to \emph{integer} vectors with error correcting codes (namely, malicious provers can potentially use rational numbers that are very close to being integers of the target magnitude).

The Zinc+ \cite{zincplus} work extends \cite{zinc} in two directions: 1) It proposes a constraint system that includes polynomial constraints simultaneously over many types of rings\footnote{In the terminology of \cite{zincplus}, the constraints are over \emph{polynomial rings with ideal membership constraints}, for certain types of ideals (generated by a monic and non-constant polynomial, or zero). However this is equivalent to saying the constraints are over different types of rings (within a certain class)} and fields, and provides a method for constructing PIOP's for those. This method extends the fingerprinting approach by not only projecting $\ZZ$ onto a random prime field, but projecting all sorts of rings and fields onto a random prime field. 2) It improves the soundness analysis of the PCS in \cite{zinc}, plus it proposes a new linear code over $\ZZ$ or $\QQ$, called Integer Pseudo Reed-Solomon (IPRS) code, obtained by ``lifting'' (in a suitable way) an FFT encoding algorithm over a small finite field, to the integers. \albert{compare \ftwoz \  to Zinc+?}



In \cite{qaren} the authors propose $\QQ$aren, a compiler that takes any PCS over a finite field of sufficiently large characteristic and creates a PCS for integer polynomials (or, more precisely, rational polynomials close to the target integer polynomials, see the overview of \cite{zinc} above). The authors instantiate their compiler to obtain the first transparent and fully succinct PCS over integers/rationals. The prior work \cite{zinc,zincplus} is not succinct on the bit-size of the integers being committed. No implementation or performance reports are provided in \cite{qaren}.

The concurrent work Limber \cite{limber} proposes a new compiler that transforms any PCS over fields of sufficiently large characteristic $q$ (in this case, $q$ is $\Omega(\lambda^2 \log^2 N)$, $N$ being the witness size, while in \cite{qaren} it is $\Omega(2^\lambda)$) into a PCS for integer polynomials. Additionally Limber compiles into strictly integer PCS, and does not present the issue that a malicious prover could use rationals that are ``almost integers''. Limber is instantiated in practice with the Hyrax and the Brakedown PCS. In \cref{s:experiments} we compare \ftwoz \ against Limber.

\paragraph{Proof systems over binary fields.} Hash-based SNARKs over binary fields were pioneered by the line of work of Ben-Sasson et al.~\cite{FRI,stark,linearSizedIOPs,cryptoeprint:2022/1542}, but did not see widespread adoption until the introduction of \textit{packed} polynomial commitment schemes by Diamond and Posen~\cite{binius,binius2}; see also \cite{binius64}. Such schemes allow one to commit to a bit-valued witness over an extension field without incurring embedding overhead, while still providing evaluation proofs for its original ``uncompressed'' representation. The \emph{ring-switching} technique of Diamond and Posen~\cite{binius2} is generic, enabling the use of codes over larger alphabets without introducing representational redundancy.\footnotemark 
A particularly attractive choice is Reed--Solomon codes over $GF(2^k)$ \cite{binius64,hashcaster:repo}, which offer an excellent trade-off between distance and encoding cost, although several works~\cite{Blaze,tensorswitch,Bolt} explore this design space using more efficient codes.
%the binary and packed version of the Basefold PCS.   
\footnotetext{%
Alternative approaches based on Galois conjugates have been proposed thereafter~\cite{lev:hashcaster,FRIBinius:Galois}.
Remarkably, the Galois approach is present in prior work \cite[Appendix F]{stark}, although not explicitly framed in full generality.
}

At the time of writing, two further works push the performance envelope of binary proof systems. Bünz, Rothblum, and Wang~\cite{flock} combine \cite{binius64} with the batched R1CS strategy inspired by~\cite{linearSizedIOPs}, achieving a striking proving throughput of several hundred thousand hashes per second on an M4 Max CPU. BinarySpartan~\cite{binaryspartan}, in turn, incorporates the optimizations of \cite{binius64}-Flock into Spartan, resulting in a proof system with again high hash-proving throughput.
% \ulrich{Not sure about Flock and Binary Spartan, since nothing conceptually new is happening there.
% The main improvement over binius64 stems from batching R1CS instances as in \cite{linearSizedIOPs}, and to be frank I do not know what Spartan is about, since binius64 and Flock are essentially Spartan.
% I have the impression that naming them requires also to provide a more complete list of binary provers.
% }

\paragraph{Commitments in binary fields with openings in rings $R$ of characteristic different than $2$.} To our knowledge this primitive is studied only in Soukhanov's note \cite{lev} and in the present work. The note \cite{lev}  proposes a scheme, called \emph{Fields-Witch} for committing over the binary field $\FF_{2^{127}}$ and then executing a PCS opening over $\FF_{2^{127}-1}$ (a prime field), by running the PCS in the exponent of a generator of   $\FF_{2^{127}}^*$. The approach, substantially different to ours, is limited to $R=\FF_{2^{127}-1}$ (or analogous configurations where $2^n-1$ is prime) and does not seem to be able to leverage our aforementioned ``low-entropy'' based optimizations.  We discuss this further in \cref{r:comparison_grand_product_binius_lev} and compare \ftwoz\ with \cite{lev} in \cref{?}. %Soukhanov has since released an implementation of the note (\texttt{fields-witch}, \url{https://github.com/morgana-proofs/fields-witch}); we compare against it in \cref{s:exp_fields_witch} and discuss the approach further in \cref{r:comparison_grand_product_binius_lev}

 $\QQ$aren and Limber \cite{qaren,limber} provide a related primitive when $R=\ZZ$, but can only support commitments over fields of relatively large characteristic (compared to $2$). 




% especially Limber because it aims at reducing the characteristic

\section{Technical overview}\label{s:tech_overview}

   




 \albertimportant{discuss somewhere (technical overview) the succinctness/non-succinctness of verifier}






In this section we describe the \ftwoz \ PCS, step by step (\cref{s:to_simple_bitz,s:to_general_rings}), and argue its efficiency (\cref{s:to_low_entropy_gkr}) and soundness (\cref{s:to_soundness_prime,s:to_soundness_rings}) at an intuitive level. \Cref{s:to_f2_virtual} describes how to prove claims on virtual $\FF_2$-linear transforms of the committed vector. In \cref{s:to_snark} (in the appendix) we explain how to build a full SNARK for constraints over (multiple) rings using \ftwoz\ as a PCS.


\subsection{The prime field case}\label{s:to_simple_bitz}


We start with a simple setting.  We let $\FF_q$ be a prime field with $q$ elements, and assume the prover knows a vector $\bff\in \FF_q^{\codedim_0}$ for which it claims that
 \begin{equation}\label{e:new_to_0}\langle \vv, \bff\rangle =\mu \qquad \text{in  } \FF_q,
\end{equation}
where $\vv\in \FF_q^{\codedim_0}, \mu\in \FF_q$ are public.  

This is a core setting, in that many finite-field based SNARKs require such primitive. However, on several occasions, they require $\FF_q$ to be a field extension, instead of just a prime field. In \cref{s:to_general_rings} we treat this important case, as well as others.

Additionally, the present setting is required when using the fingerprinting PIOP approach of \cite{zaratan} for proving integer constraints. In that approach, the integer constraint is projected to a random prime field $\FF_q$, and a PIOP is run over $\FF_q$, outputting a claim of the form \eqref{e:new_to_0}. 




We next present a protocol (a simple instantiation of \ftwoz) for proving \eqref{e:new_to_0}. We assume $\vv$ admits a tensor decomposition
%
$$
\vv= \vv^{(1)} \otimes \vv^{(2)},
$$
where we assume there is freedom to choose the lengths of $\vv^{(1)},\vv^{(2)}$ (whose product is $\codedim_0$).  This is typically the case since $\vv$ is usually an ``eq'' vector, i.e.\ $\vv=(\eq(\bb,\rr))_{\bb\in \bits^{\log \codedim_0}}$ for some $\rr\in \FF_q^{\log\codedim_0}$. Tensor decompositions play a crucial role in the efficiency of \ftwoz, as we discuss in \cref{s:why_the_tensor_split}.

 \paragraph{Step 1: Commitment in $\comfield$.} Let $B\defeq \lceil \log q\rceil$ and let $\codedim\defeq B\cdot \codedim_0$. The prover creates a vector $\bitstext(\bff) \in \bits^{\codedim} \subseteq \ZZ^{\codedim}$ of integer bits, obtained by bit-decomposing each entry of $\bff$ (a field element) into a vector of $B$ bits, and concatenating all such $B$-length vectors. 

     Next, the prover looks at $\bitstext(\bff)$ as a vector from $\FF_2^{\codedim}$, packs it as a vector from $\comfield^{\codedim/\comfieldexp}$ (see below), and encodes the packed vector with a Reed-Solomon code $\cC$ (or any other code) over $\comfield$, and sends a Merkle root of the resulting codeword to the verifier.   To \emph{pack} $\bitstext(\bff)$, every consecutive $\comfieldexp$ bits $b_1, \ldots, b_\comfieldexp$ of $\bitstext(\bff)$ are joined together as a single field element $\sum_{i\in [\comfieldexp]} X^{i-1}\cdot b_i$ from $\comfield$ (one can use a different $\FF_2$-base than the monomial base $(X^i)_i$). We denote the encoded word by $f\in \comfield^{\codelength}$, with $\codelength$ being the length of the code.
     
    From here on, the prover and verifier set out to prove an instance of the following relation, which we call LinBitsRings, and which takes a more general form in \cref{s:to_general_rings}:
%
    \begin{align*}
 &\LinModFieldBin(\FF_q) = \left\{ \left(\begin{aligned} &\inp=(\mu, \vv)\in \FF_q  \times \FF_q^{ \codedim_0}, \\
 &\oracle = f \in \comfield^{\codelength}, \\ &\wit= \bff\in \FF_q^{\codedim_0}\end{aligned}\right) \;\middle|\;  \begin{aligned}  
 &f = \Enc_{\cC}\circ \pack\circ\pi_2(\bitstext(\bff)),\\
 &\langle \bff, \vv \rangle = \mu  \qquad  \text{in } \FF_q
 \end{aligned} \right\},
\end{align*}
    %
    where, $\Enc_\cC$ denotes encoding with the code $\cC$; $\pack$ is the packing operation; and $\pi_2$ is reduction modulo $2$ (so that $\pi_2(\bitstext(\bff))$ means that we look at the bits in $\bitstext(\bff)$ as elements from $\FF_2$). When proving such relation, the verifier receives $\inp$ and oracle access the $\oracle$, and the prover receives additionally $\ww$.

    If using a proximity parameter beyond the unique decoding radius, the prover and verifier perform an out of domain sample, see Round 0 of \cref{c:core_iop}.


\paragraph{Step 2: Bitification of the linear claim.}  Observe that $$\langle \vv,\bff\rangle =  \sum_{i\in [\codedim_0]} \sum_{j\in [B]}\vv_i \cdot 2^{j-1}\cdot \pi_q(\bitstext(\bff)_{ij}) = \langle \uu, \pi_q(\bitstext(\bff))\rangle \qquad \text{in } \FF_q,$$ for $\uu\defeq \vv\otimes(1,2,\ldots, 2^{B-1})$, where we index the entries of $\bitstext(\bff)$ with tuples $(i,j)\in [\codedim_0]\times[B]$.  Accordingly, the claim \eqref{e:new_to_0} is replaced by \begin{equation}\label{e:intro_eq_2}\langle \uu, \pi_q(\bitstext(\bff))\rangle =\mu \qquad \text{in  } \FF_q.
\end{equation} 
%
If $\vv$ is an ``eq vector'' or a vector admitting flexible tensor decompositions, then $\uu$ can be tensor decomposed as $\uu= \uu^{(1)} \otimes \uu^{(2)} \in \FF_q^{\codedim_1 \cdot \codedim_2}$ with  $\codedim_1\approx \codedim_2\approx \sqrt{\codedim}$ (our instantiation takes $\codedim_1\in [\codedim^{0.5},\codedim^{0.6}]$, see \cref{s:instantiation}). 

%Other choices of decompositions may lead to better efficiency later, especially if $\bff$ contains entries with heterogeneous bit-sizes.  % assume for the sake of efficiency later on that $\uu$ also admits a tensor decomposition $\uu=\uu_1\otimes \uu_2 \in \FF_q^{\codedim_1 \cdot \codedim_2}$. This is in general a free assumption, see \cref{r:tensor_dec_u} \albert{add a remark not in the main body?}.
 %\bigskip



 \paragraph{Step 3 (Optional):  Resampling of $\FF_q$.}  For soundness, we need that $|\FF_q|< \fieldboundsizeexponent$ (because we will prove a collection of integer linear claims ``in the exponent'' of a generator of $\comfield^*$). If this condition is not met, we replace $\FF_q$ by a smaller prime field via a randomized procedure. For now, we assume the condition holds, and we explain this field resampling step below in \cref{r:large_prime_field_in_BitZ_PCS}. 


 \paragraph{Step 4: Transformation of \eqref{e:intro_eq_2} into a batch of low-magnitude integer claims.} The prover organizes $\bitstext(\bff)$ into a $\codedim_1\times \codedim_2$ matrix with columns $\bitstext(\bff)_{*j}\in \bits^{\codedim_1}$ ($j\in [\codedim_2]$). Then it publishes   $\mu_1,\ldots, \mu_{\codedim_2}\in  \ZZ$ defined as 
         $\mu_j=\langle \pi^{-1}(\uu^{(1)}), \bitstext(\bff)_{*j}\rangle$  in $\ZZ$, for all $j\in [\codedim_2]$.  
         Then the verifier checks that $\mu_j\in \range{0}{(q-1)\cdot \codedim_1}$ for all $j\in [\codedim_2]$, and that 
         %
         $$
        \sum_{j\in [\codedim_2]} \pi(\mu_j) \cdot \uu_j^{(2)} = \mu \qquad \text{in  } \FF_q,
         $$
         and \eqref{e:intro_eq_2} is replaced by the integer claim \begin{equation}\label{e:intro_eq_3}\langle \pi^{-1}(\uu^{(1)}), \bitstext(\bff)_{*j}\rangle =\mu_j \qquad \text{in  } \ZZ \qquad \text{for all } j\in [\codedim_2],
        \end{equation} 
        where $\pi^{-1}$ is the canonical representation of elements from $\FF_q$ as integers in the range $\range{0}{q-1}$.
        
        
        
        \paragraph{Step 5: Exponentiation in $\comfield$ and grand product in $\comfield$.}  Since we assumed $|\FF_q|< \fieldboundsizeexponent$, and since both sides of \eqref{e:intro_eq_3} lie in $\range{0}{(q-1)\cdot \codedim_1}$ (recall that $\bitstext(\bff)$ has bit entries, and that the verifier checked the range of each $\mu_j$), \eqref{e:intro_eq_3} is true if and only if 
          \begin{equation}\label{e:intro_eq_4}
          \begin{aligned}
          g^{\mu_j}  &= g^{\langle \pi^{-1}(\uu^{(1)}), \bitstext(\bff)_{*j}\rangle}\\ &= g^{\sum_{i\in[\codedim_1]} \pi^{-1}(\uu_i^{(1)}) \cdot \bitstext(\bff)_{ij} } = \prod_{i\in [\codedim_1]} g^{ \pi^{-1}(\uu_i^{(1)}) \cdot \bitstext(\bff)_{ij} } 
          \\ &= \prod_{i\in [\codedim_1]} \left(\left(g^{\pi^{-1}(\uu_i^{(1)})} -1\right)\cdot \bitstext(\bff)_{ij}  +1\right) \qquad \text{in  } \comfield \qquad \text{for all } j\in [\codedim_2],
          \end{aligned}
        \end{equation} 
        where $g$ is a generator of the multiplicative subgroup of $\comfield$, and in the last expression, $\bitstext(\bff)_{ij}$ denotes the $i,j$-th entry (a bit) in $\bitstext(\bff)$ seen as an element of $\comfield$.

        The prover and verifier run  a batched grand product PIOP over the binary field $\comfield$ for the claim  \eqref{e:intro_eq_4} (see \cref{s:to_low_entropy_gkr}), obtaining as a result a new claim of the form
        \begin{equation}\label{e:intro_eq_5} \langle \zz, \bitstext(\bff)_{*j} \rangle = c_j \qquad \text{in  } \comfield \qquad \text{for all } j\in [\codedim_2],
        \end{equation} 
        for some $\zz\in \comfield^{\codedim_1}$ and $c_j\in \comfield$. In general, these claims can be batched to \begin{equation} \label{e:intro_eq_5_batched}\langle \zz', \bitstext(\bff) \rangle = c \qquad \text{in  } \comfield\end{equation} for  $\zz'\in \comfield^{\codedim}, c\in \comfield$ with, for example, $\zz'=(\alpha_1,\ldots, \alpha_{\codedim_2}) \otimes \zz$ for $\alpha_1, \ldots, \alpha_{\codedim_2}$ verifier's randomness, and $c=\sum_{j\in [\codedim_2]} \alpha_j\cdot c_j$. 
        However, in most instantiations this step may not be necessary, especially if using an interleaved code to commit to $\bitstext(\bff)$ \albert{In the GKR grand product section of the main body I may need to mention batching?}. 

        


        This is by far the most expensive step of \ftwoz. Our grand product PIOP is a simple GKR protocol for grand products, for which we design novel \albert{to our knowledge} prover optimizations, see \cref{s:to_low_entropy_gkr} below and \cref{s:gkr_low_entropy}. These optimizations exploit the ``low entropy'' of the factors $(g^{\pi^{-1}(\uu^{(1)}_i)}-1)\cdot \bitstext(\bff)_{ij} +1$ across $(i,j)\in [\codedim_1]\times [\codedim_2]$, in the sense that across $\codedim=\codedim_1\cdot \codedim_2$ factors, there are only $\codedim_1+1=O(\sqrt{\codedim})$ possible values taken by these factors.  This, among others, is why it is important to have the vector $\uu$ admit a tensor split into $\approx$square-root sized factors. % See \cref{?,s:gkr_low_entropy} for more details.
\begin{comment}
\begin{remark}[Other usages of the ``prove-in-the-exponent'' approach]
       There are other projects (e.g.\ Binius64 \cite{binius64} and Soukhanov's note \cite{lev}) that use exponentiation to prove integer (or mod $|\comfield|-1$) claims in binary fields, none of these (to our knowledge) is used or is usable in a context that results in the low entropy grand products of ours, where our optimizations apply.  We discuss this further in \cref{r:comparison_grand_product_binius_lev}.
      \end{remark}  
      \end{comment}
 \paragraph{Step 6: Proving linear claims on $\bitstext(\bff)$ over $\comfield$.} At this point, since $\bitstext(\bff)$ was committed over $\comfield$ (in packed form), one can prove \eqref{e:intro_eq_5} (or the batched version of it) via standard methods such as in \cite{binius,binius2,flock}. Concretely, we use the so-called ring switch from \cite{binius,binius2} to turn \eqref{e:intro_eq_5} into linear claims about the packed binary field elements we encoded, and then use WHIR/Ligerito \cite{whir,ligerito} over $\comfield$ to prove these claims. 


\bigskip       

 
    In \cref{s:instantiation} we discuss how to efficiently instantiate \ftwoz \ with $\comfield=\FF_{2^{128}}$, and attain 128 bits of security.





\begin{remark}[Description of Step 3]\label{r:large_prime_field_in_BitZ_PCS}
    Assume the condition $|\FF_q|< \fieldboundsizeexponent$ does not hold. Then, in Step 3, the prover sends an integer $\mu'\in \ZZ$ such that
    %
    $$\langle  \pi^{-1}(\uu^{(1)})\otimes \pi^{-1}(\uu^{(2)}), \bitstext(\bff)\rangle = \mu' \qquad \text{in } \ZZ,$$    
    The verifier checks that  $\pi(\mu')=\mu$, and responds with a random prime $q'$ from a public set $\cP$ of primes, all smaller than $\fieldboundsizeexponent$. Then, using the fingerprinting technique \cite{zaratan},  the prover and verifier project the above claim  to the field $\FF_{q'}$, so that it becomes
        $$\langle  \pi_{q'}\circ \pi^{-1}(\uu^{(1)})\otimes  \pi_{q'}\circ\pi^{-1}(\uu^{(2)}),  \pi_{q'}(\bitstext(\bff))\rangle = \pi_{q'}(\mu') \qquad \text{in } \FF_{q'}.$$  
        Then the prover and verifier proceed to Step 4 after updating $\FF_{q} \gets \FF_{q'}$, $\pi\gets \pi_{q'}$, $\mu \gets \pi_{q'}(\mu')$, and $\uu\gets \pi_{q'}\circ \pi^{-1}(\uu^{(1)}) \otimes \pi_{q'}\circ \pi^{-1}(\uu^{(2)})$, where $\pi_{q'}$ denotes reduction modulo $q'$.
        
    To ensure soundness of the projection step, the verifier checks that $\mu'\in \range{0}{\codedim\cdot (q-1)^2}$  (see \cref{s:to_soundness_rings}).
\end{remark}


\begin{remark}[Succinctness of the verifier and the proof size]
Throughout the above construction, the verifier handles only vectors of size $\codedim_1$ and $\codedim_2$, hence, by setting $\codedim_1$ and $\codedim_2$ approximately of size $\sqrt{\codedim}$, we manage to keep the verifier and proof size succinct. We leave as open problem (\cref{?}) making these asymptotically polylogarithmic, instead of square root sized. 
\end{remark}
%\paragraph{Costs.} Concrete costs are provided in \cref{?}. In short, the prover operations are comprised of: encoding  the vector $\pack\circ \pi_2(\bitstext(\bff))\in \comfield^{\codedim/\comfieldexp}$, and computing a Merkle tree for the resulting word (Step 1); the operations required in the GKR grand product protocol and its subsequent possible batching steps (which we optimize in \cref{s:gkr_low_entropy}) (half Step 5); the operations used by the ring-switch \cite{binius2} and the PCS, WHIR or Ligerito in our instantiation (Step 6); and the operations from Steps 2 to half of Step 5. The latter involve a linear amount of operations over $\ZZ$ and over $\comfield$.

\subsubsection{Why the tensor split}\label{s:why_the_tensor_split} The claim $\langle \uu, \pi_q(\bitstext(\bff))\rangle = \mu$ (cf.\  \eqref{e:intro_eq_2}) over $\FF_q$ is split, in Step 4, into $\codedim_2$ claims over $\ZZ$ of the form 
$
\langle \pi^{-1}(\uu^{(1)}), \bitstext(\bff)_{*j}\rangle =\mu_j$ for all  $j\in [\codedim_2]$ (cf.\ \eqref{e:intro_eq_3}).  This pays off in three ways:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*,nosep]
    \item All $\codedim_2$ grand products in \eqref{e:intro_eq_4} share the same $\codedim_1$ public values $g^{\pi^{-1}(\uu_i^{(1)})}$. In total, there are $\codedim$ factors, but at most $\codedim_1+1\approx \sqrt{\codedim}$ distinct values. This is crucial for performance when proving the grand products (see \cref{s:to_low_entropy_gkr}).
    \item The verifier only handles vectors of length $\codedim_1 \approx \sqrt{\codedim}$ and $\codedim_2\approx \sqrt{\codedim}$, instead of vectors of length $\codedim$, allowing for succinctness. 
    \item Each $\mu_j$ belongs to the range $\range{0}{(q-1)\cdot \codedim_1}$ instead of $\range{0}{(q-1)\cdot \codedim}$. Hence prime fields of size up to $(|\comfield|-1)/\codedim_1$, instead of $(|\comfield|-1)/\codedim$, fit in the exponent of $g$ without Step 3. This difference becomes important in practice when seeking to instantiate \ftwoz\ at 128 bits of security while using $\comfield$, and not paying too much on grinding, cf.\ \cref{s:instantiation}.  \albert{Could be more detailed}
\end{itemize}

In \cref{t:core_iop_complexity} we record the concrete costs of this protocol (in a more general form).


\subsubsection{ Round-by-round knowledge soundness}\label{s:to_soundness_prime}

\albertimportant{Review}

In \cref{s:soundness_core_iop} we prove (\cref{t:thm_core_IOPP}) that the IOP $\Pi$ of \cref{s:to_simple_bitz} is perfectly complete and round-by-round knowledge sound. We do so by viewing $\Pi$ as the composition of three Interactive Oracle Reductions (IORs, cf.\ \cite{warp,BCFW25} and \cref{s:IORs}). The first one, $\Pi_1$ (Steps 2, 3, 4 and the first half of Step 5), reduces the initial claim, namely knowledge of a witness for $(\inp,\oracle)=((\mu,\vv),f)\in \LANG(\LinBitsDual(\FF_q))$, to the grand product claim \eqref{e:intro_eq_4}, namely knowledge of $\bfg\in \FF_2^{\codedim_1\times \codedim_2}$ with $f=\Enc_\cC\circ \pack(\bfg)$ and $$\prod_{i\in [\codedim_1]}\bigl((g^{\pi_q^{-1}(\uu^{(1)}_i)}-1)\cdot \bfg_{ij}+1\bigr)=g^{\mu_j}$$ for all $j\in [\codedim_2]$ (an instance of the relation $\PESAT$ of \cref{d:pesat}, see \cref{s:interleaved_setting}). The second one, $\Pi_2$ (second half of Step 5), is a standard instantiation\footnote{Our optimizations from \cref{c:gkr_grand_product} for the grand product GKR apply at the implementation level: they  keep the transcript invariant, and so they require no soundness analysis.} of the GKR protocol for these $\codedim_2$ grand products  which reduces them to the linear claims over $\comfield$  for the witness vectors $\bitstext(\bff)_{*j}$ \eqref{e:intro_eq_5} (the relation $\LIN(\comfield,\cC,\pack)$ of \cref{s:interleaved_setting}). The third one, $\Pi_3$ (Step 6), proves the latter with the ring-switching technique of \cite{binius2} followed by WHIR/Ligerito \cite{whir,ligerito}, exactly as in Flock \cite{flock} \albert{and binius?}, possibly with a batched sumcheck before that, to reduce the claims \eqref{e:intro_eq_5} into a single MLE evaluation of $\bitstext(\bff)$ over $\comfield$. It is well known that $\Pi_2$ and $\Pi_3$ are perfectly complete and round-by-round knowledge sound\footnote{For $\Pi_3$ this holds in a relaxed form, in the sense that a malicious prover may convince the verifier with an oracle $f$ which is not exactly $\Enc_\cC\circ \pack(\bfg)$ for a valid witness $\bfg$, but is \emph{close} to it in Hamming distance. This is a well-studied technicality \cite{stark,BCFW25,whir,rbr_fri,zook}, which we treat in \cref{s:IORs,s:soundness_core_iop} using the formalism of round-by-round knowledge soundness \emph{with relaxation} from \cite{zook}. We do not discuss this further in this overview.}, and that the composition of round-by-round knowledge sound IORs is again round-by-round knowledge sound (\cref{r:ior_compositoin}). Hence in this overview we only discuss $\Pi_1$. \albertimportant{formalization around UDR/Johnson and proximity could be clearer}

\bigskip

\noindent \emph{\underline{Round-by-round knowledge soundness of $\Pi_1$}}. The (possibly malicious) prover and verifier start with the public input $(\vv,\mu)$ and the oracle $f$.  Assume first that Step 3 does not trigger, so that $|\FF_q|<(|\comfield|-1)/\codedim_1$. We discuss the case where this is otherwise later. 
 
  \emph{Step 2:} After Step 2, the claim is that the prover knows $\bfg\in \bits^{\codedim}\subseteq \ZZ^\codedim$ such that
%
\begin{equation}\label{e:to_soundness_1}
\langle \uu, \pi_q(\bfg)\rangle = \mu \quad \text{in } \FF_q, \qquad \text{and} \qquad f= \Enc_\cC \circ \pack\circ \pi_2(\bfg).
\end{equation}
%
This step has  no  knowledge soundness loss, in the sense that there exists an efficient algorithm that given a valid witness $\bfg$ for  \eqref{e:to_soundness_1}, outputs a  witness $\bff\in \FF_q^{\codedim_0}$ such that $((\vv,\mu), f, \bff)\in \LinBitsDual(\FF_q)$. Indeed, for any such $\bfg\in  \bits^{\codedim}\subseteq \ZZ^\codedim$ it suffices to take $\bff=\sum_{j\in [B]} 2^{j-1}\cdot \pi_q(\bfg_{ij})$, where these operations are over the field $\FF_q$. 

\emph{Step 4:} At the end of Step 4 the  claim has become that the prover knows $\bfg\in \bits^{\codedim}\subseteq \ZZ^\codedim$ such that
%
\begin{equation}\label{e:to_soundness_2}
\langle \pi_q^{-1}(\uu^{(1)}), \bfg_{*j}\rangle =\mu_j \quad \text{in  } \ZZ \quad \text{for all } j\in [\codedim_2], \qquad \text{and} \qquad f= \Enc_\cC \circ \pack\circ \pi_2(\bfg).
\end{equation}
%
Again, we justify that given a valid witness $\bfg$ for \eqref{e:to_soundness_2}, the exact same witness $\bfg$ satisfies \eqref{e:to_soundness_1}, and hence this step also incurs no knowledge soundness loss. Indeed, 
 if \eqref{e:to_soundness_2} holds and the verifier's checks pass, then 
$$
\langle \uu,\pi_q(\bfg)\rangle = \sum_{j\in [\codedim_2]} \pi_q\bigl(\langle \pi_q^{-1}(\uu^{(1)}), \bfg_{*j}\rangle\bigr)\cdot \uu^{(2)}_j = \sum_{j\in [\codedim_2]} \pi_q(\mu_j)\cdot \uu_j^{(2)}=\mu,
$$
%
Hence \eqref{e:to_soundness_1} holds for the same  witness $\bfg$.


\emph{First half of Step 5:} Finally, the first half of Step 5 replaces \eqref{e:to_soundness_2} by the grand product claim \eqref{e:intro_eq_4}. If the latter holds for some $\bfg\in \bits^{\codedim}\subseteq \ZZ^\codedim$, then $\langle \pi_q^{-1}(\uu^{(1)}), \bfg_{*j}\rangle \equiv \mu_j \pmod{\mathsf{ord}(g)}$, where $\mathsf{ord}(g)=|\comfield|-1$. Since $\bfg$ has bit entries and $\mu_j$ passed the range check of the verifier, both sides lie in $\range{0}{(q-1)\cdot \codedim_1}$. Further,  $(q-1)\cdot \codedim_1<|\comfield|-1$ by our assumption on $q$, so the congruence is an equality over $\ZZ$ and so \eqref{e:to_soundness_2} holds. 

Hence, if Step 3 does not trigger, then $\Pi_1$ is a one-round IOR with  round-by-round knowledge soundness error $0$ (the verifier sends no message during this round, hence the round can be glued together with the next one in \cref{s:soundness_core_iop}).

\emph{Step 3.} Assume Step 3 triggers (\cref{r:large_prime_field_in_BitZ_PCS}). Then the protocol has one extra round with a nonzero round-by-round knowledge soundness error. The prover sends $\mu'\in \range{0}{\codedim\cdot (q-1)^2}$ with $\pi_q(\mu')=\mu$, the verifier samples a random prime $q'\in\cP$.

In this case, at the end of Step 3, the claim \eqref{e:to_soundness_1} is replaced by a similar claim over a randomly sampled prime field $\FF_{q'}$, namely the prover claims knowledge of $\bfg\in \bits^{\codedim} \subseteq \ZZ^\codedim$  such that $\langle \pi_{q'}\circ\pi_q^{-1}(\uu^{(1)})\otimes \pi_{q'}\circ\pi_q^{-1}(\uu^{(2)}), \pi_{q'}(\bfg)\rangle=\pi_{q'}(\mu')$ in $\FF_{q'}$. Let $\cE$ be the event that this   holds for some $\bfg$  but \eqref{e:to_soundness_1} does not. Then the integer $h\defeq \langle \pi_q^{-1}(\uu^{(1)})\otimes \pi_q^{-1}(\uu^{(2)}), \bfg\rangle - \mu' \in \ZZ$ satisfies $\pi_q(h)=\langle \uu, \pi_q(\bfg)\rangle-\mu\neq 0$. Hence $h\neq 0$. Moreover, we have $|h|\leq 2\cdot \codedim\cdot (q-1)^2$, because $\bfg$ has bit entries and $\mu'$ passed the verifier's range check. Additionally,  $\pi_{q'}(h)=0$, and so $q'$ divides $h$. Such a nonzero and bounded integer $h$ has at most $\log(2\codedim(q-1)^2)/\lfloor\log q_{\min}\rfloor$ prime divisors in a set of primes $\cP$, where $q_{\min}$ is the smallest prime in $\cP$. Hence the probability of $\cE$ is at most 
% 
\begin{equation}\label{e:to_kappa_0}
\kappa_0 \defeq \frac{\log\left(2\cdot \codedim\cdot (q-1)^2\right)}{\lfloor\log q_{\min}\rfloor\cdot |\cP|}
\end{equation}
%
over the choice of $q'$. 


After Step 3, we are exactly in the same scenario as at the beginning of Step 4 in the case that Step 3 did not trigger. Hence $\Pi_1$ is round-by-round knowledge sound with errors $(\kappa_0, 0)$ (or with error $0$ if Step 3 is skipped). 

Overall,  $\Pi$ has errors $\kappa_0$ followed by those of the GKR protocol and of the binary-field PCS (with prior possible batching sumcheck, followed by  ring switch).


\subsection{Generalization to pairs of arbitrary finitely generated rings}\label{s:to_general_rings}




Let $S$ be a finitely generated ring. For example, $S$ could be a finite field extension, or the ring of integers $\ZZ$, or a cyclotomic ring.

That $S$ is finitely generated means that  there exist elements $1, \eta_1, \ldots, \eta_k\in S$ such that every element $s\in S$ can be written as a polynomial expression $s =  \sum_{\aa} s_\aa \cdot \eta_1^{a_1}\cdots \eta_k^{a_k}$ in $\eta_1,\ldots,\eta_k$, for some elements $s_\aa \in \range{0}{\Char(S)-1}_S$ (for some integers $s_\aa$ if $\Char(S)=0$), where $\Char(S)$ is the characteristic of $S$\footnote{The characteristic of a ring with multiplicative unity $1$  is the minimum $n$ such that $1$ added together $n$ times equals $0$; or $\Char(S)=0$ if such $n$ does not exist.}.   For example, if $S$ is a field extension of size $q^e$ with $e> 1$, then $k_S=1$ and $\eta_1 = X$ (where we look at elements from the extension field as polynomials). If $S$ is a prime field or the integers $\ZZ$, then $k_S=0$ and the only generator is $1$. 

Let $R$ be another finitely generated ring, and let $$\psi: S\to R$$ be a ring homomorphism. For example, we could simply have $S=R$ be a finite field, and $\psi$ be the identity (this is the setting in the previous \cref{s:to_simple_bitz}).  Or, in the fingerprinting approach to proving integer constraints, one would take $S=\ZZ$, $R$ a prime field, and $\psi$ reduction modulo $q$. In \cite{zincplus}, $S$ is a polynomial ring, $R$ is a prime field, and $\psi$ is a certain projection. 

Assume now that the prover knows a vector $\bff\in S^{\codedim_0}$ for which it claims that
 \begin{equation}\label{e:new_to_0_rings}\langle \vv, \psi(\bff)\rangle =\mu \qquad \text{in  } R,
\end{equation}
where $\vv\in R^{\codedim_0}, \mu\in R$ are public.  



\paragraph{Size bounds.} For soundness reasons\footnote{There are also formalization difficulties when allowing arbitrary witness elements in an infinite ring. For example, no polynomial time extractor can output an exponentially sized witness.}, when $S$ is infinite, we cannot allow a prover to use arbitrarily large witnesses, and we  restrict the set of allowable witnesses to a publicly specified subset of $S$ (this is standard practice, see e.g.\ \cite{zaratan,zinc,qaren,limber,zincplus}).  

Let $B,D>0$ be public size parameters.  Intuitively, we want the prover to prove knowledge of a witness that can be written as polynomial expressions on $\eta_1, \ldots, \eta_{k_S}$ of total degree less than $D$, with coefficients in $\range{0}{2^B-1}_S$, where $\range{0}{2^B-1}_S$ is the set of elements from $S$ that can be obtained by adding $1$ together less than $2^B$ times (and zero). We denote the set of such elements by $S_{\leq B}^{<D}$. Formally,
 %
 $$
S_{\leq B}^{<D}\defeq \left\{\left.\sum_{\substack{\aa\in \ZZ_{\geq 0}^k,\\  a_1+\ldots+a_k<D} } s_{\aa}\cdot \eta_1^{a_1}\cdots\eta_k^{a_k} \ \right| \ s_{\aa}\in \range{0}{2^B-1}_S\right\}
 $$
 % 
  If $S$ is finite to begin with, we can simply take $B,D$ so that $S_{\leq B}^{<D}=S$, or we may prefer to  restrict witnesses to a subset of $S$ anyway.


 \paragraph{Step 1: Commitment in $\comfield$.}  As before, the prover creates a vector $\bitstext(\bff) \in \bits^{\codedim} \subseteq \ZZ^{\codedim}$ of integer bits, for some $\codedim$, with $\bitstext(\bff)$ being linearly related to $\bff$ in a manner we explain next. Given $s\in S_{\leq B}^{<D}$ with $s = \sum_{\aa} s_\aa \cdot \eta_1^{a_1}\cdots \eta_{k}^{a_k}$, where the sum runs over the set $I$ of tuples $\aa\in \ZZ_{\geq 0}^k$ with $a_1+\ldots+a_k<D$, write
%
$$
s= \sum_{\aa\in I} \sum_{i\in [B]} s_{\aa,i}\cdot 2^{i-1}\cdot  \eta_1^{a_1}\cdots \eta_{k}^{a_k}, \qquad \text{for } s_{\aa,i}\in \{0,1\}.
$$
%
Then define $\bitstext(s)=(s_{\aa,i})_{i \in [B], \aa\in I}$. Then $\bitstext(\bff)$ consists in applying $\bitstext$ entry-wise to $\bff$.  Note that 
%
\begin{equation}\label{e:new_to_linear_bits_to_f}
\bff = L \cdot \bitstext(\bff)
\end{equation}
for certain matrix $L\in S^{\codedim_0 \times \codedim}$. Concretely, $L= \mathsf{Id}_{\codedim_0} \otimes (1, 2, \ldots, 2^{B-1}) \otimes (\eta_1^{a_1}\cdots \eta_k^{a_k})_{\aa\in I}$.

We remark that this is not the only way one can transform $\bff$ into a vector of bits. Especially if $\bff$ contains elements with heterogeneous bit-sizes, a different bit-representation strategy is preferable, to avoid $\bitstext(\bff)$ being unnecessarily large, see \cref{r: non_uniform_bit_decomposition} for more comments on this bitification step.
 


\bigskip

     \noindent Next, the prover commits to $\bitstext(\bff)$ in packed form exactly as in Step 1 of \cref{s:to_simple_bitz}. From here on, the prover and verifier set out to prove an instance of the following relation, which we call LinBitsRings, and which takes a more general form in \cref{s:general_LinBitsDual}:
%
    \begin{align*}
 &\LinModFieldBin(S, R, \psi)\\ &\qquad\qquad\qquad = \left\{ \left(\begin{aligned} &\inp=(\mu, \vv)\in R  \times R^{ \codedim_0}, \\
 &\oracle = f \in \comfield^{\codelength}, \\ &\wit= \bff\in (S_{\leq B}^{<D})^{\codedim_0}\end{aligned}\right) \;\middle|\;  \begin{aligned}  
 &f = \Enc_{\cC}\circ \pack\circ\pi_2(\bitstext(\bff)),\\
 &\langle \psi(\bff), \vv \rangle = \mu  \qquad  \text{in } R
 \end{aligned} \right\}.
\end{align*}

\paragraph{Step 2: Bitification of the linear claim.} Similarly as in Step 2 of \cref{s:to_simple_bitz}, the claim \eqref{e:new_to_0_rings} is replaced by \begin{equation}\label{e:intro_eq_2_rings}\langle \uu, \psi(\bitstext(\bff))\rangle =\mu \qquad \text{in  } R,
\end{equation} 
for a new $\uu\in R^{\codedim}$. This transformation is done using the linear expression from \eqref{e:new_to_linear_bits_to_f}. Concretely, we have $$\begin{aligned}\langle \vv, \psi(\bff)\rangle &= \langle \vv, \psi( L \cdot \bitstext(\bff)) \rangle = \langle \psi(L)^{\mathsf{T}}\cdot \vv, \psi(\bitstext(\bff)) \rangle = \langle \uu, \psi(\bitstext(\bff))\rangle, \\ \uu&\defeq \psi(L)^{\mathsf{T}}\cdot \vv.\end{aligned}$$ 
As before, it is important that $L$ is such that the new vector $\uu$ still admits a tensor decomposition $\uu=\uu^{(1)} \otimes \uu^{(2)}$ with, say, $\uu^{(1)}\in R^{\codedim_1}$ and $\uu^{(2)}\in R^{\codedim_2}$ and $\codedim_1\approx \sqrt{\codedim}$, $\codedim_2\approx \sqrt{\codedim}$. This is the case for the example $L$ given above. For such matrix $L$ we have
%
$$\begin{aligned} \uu&=\vv\otimes (1, 2, \ldots, 2^{B-1})\otimes (\psi(\eta_1^{a_1}\cdots \eta_k^{a_k}))_{\aa\in I},\end{aligned}$$ 
%
which admits flexible tensor decompositions if $\vv$ does.
%


 \paragraph{Step 3 (Optional):  Replacing $R$ by a random ``small'' field $\FF_q$.}  Our next goal is to lift the claim \eqref{e:intro_eq_2_rings}, i.e.\ $\langle \uu, \psi(\bitstext(\bff))\rangle =\mu$ over $R$, to a claim ``in the exponent'' of a generator $g$ of $\comfield^*$. This time, however, there is a priori no natural lifting procedure, since, in intuitive terms, the exponent of $g$ can only accommodate integers or integers modulo $|\comfield|-1$ (the order of $g$), while $\langle \uu, \psi(\bitstext(\bff))\rangle$ is a sum of elements from an arbitrary ring $R$.

 Because of this, the prover and verifier undergo a randomization step where the ring $R$ is replaced by a random ``small'' prime field $\FF_q$. This step can be skipped if:
 \begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
     \item $R=\FF_q$ is a prime field with $q<\fieldboundsizeexponent$, i.e.\ $R$ is a ``small'' field,
     \item $R=\ZZ$ and all entries of $\uu^{(1)}$ are nonnegative and smaller than $\fieldboundsizeexponent$ (cf.\ \cref{r:skip_round_1_integers}),
 \end{itemize}
since in that case there is already a natural and sound lift of the claim $\langle \uu, \psi(\bitstext(\bff))\rangle =\mu$ to the exponent of $g$. The bound $\fieldboundsizeexponent$ ensures that the integer claims of Step 4 of \cref{s:to_simple_bitz} cannot overflow $|\comfield|-1$, even if the claim is false (see \cref{s:to_soundness_prime}).

\bigskip 

\noindent The step proceeds as follows: 
suppose $R$ is generated by $1, \gamma_1, \ldots, \gamma_{k'}$. The prover computes and sends $\mu' \in \ZZ[X_1, \ldots, X_{k'}]$ (the ring of polynomials with integer coefficients and on variables $X_1,\ldots, X_{k'}$) such that
%
\begin{equation}\label{e:new_to_lifted_polring_claim}
\langle \picanon^{-1}(\uu^{(1)}) \otimes \picanon^{-1}(\uu^{(2)}) , \bitstext(\bff) \rangle = \mu' \qquad \text{in } \ZZ[X_1, \ldots, X_{k'}].
\end{equation}
where $\picanon: \ZZ[X_1, \ldots, X_{k'}] \to R$ is a canonical ring homomorphism sending $X_i$ to $\gamma_i$ for all $i\in [k']$, and sending $1\in \ZZ$ to the multiplicative identity of $R$ (also denoted $1$). The inverse $\picanon^{-1}$ is a natural inverse of $\picanon$ (there are several ways to choose it), see \cref{e:presentations}.


%of an element $r\in R$ is obtained as follows: write $r= p(\gamma_1,\ldots, \gamma_{k'})$ for some polynomial $p(X_1,\ldots, X_{k'})$ with coefficients in $\range{0}{\Char(R)-1}$. Then $\picanon^{-1}(r)= \pi^{-1}(p)(X_1,\ldots, X_{k'})$, where $\pi^{-1}(p)$ is obtained by lifting all coefficients of $p$ to the integers. 

%
The verifier checks that $\mu'$ has total degree and bit-size below  certain bounds $D_\ZZ, B_\ZZ$, respectively. These bounds depend on $\codedim, \Char(R)$ and $k'$.  Then the verifier checks that $\picanon(\mu')=\mu$.


Now the idea is that the claim \eqref{e:new_to_lifted_polring_claim} implies the claim \eqref{e:intro_eq_2_rings}, since $\picanon(\mu')=\mu$. Hence, the prover and the verifier  proceed to certify  \eqref{e:new_to_lifted_polring_claim}. The constraint \eqref{e:new_to_lifted_polring_claim} is a polynomial constraint (in fact, a linear constraint) over the ring $\ZZ[X_1,\ldots, X_{k'}]$. We now generalize the fingerprinting technique for polynomial rings from Zinc+ \cite{zincplus} (which in turn generalizes that of \cite{zaratan}), so as to project \eqref{e:new_to_lifted_polring_claim} to a random ``small'' prime field.  

Concretely,  the verifier samples a random prime $q$ and a random morphism
%
$
\pirand : \ZZ[X_1, \ldots, X_{k'}] \to \FF_q
$. The protocol specifies a public set $\cP$ of samplable primes, and for each $q\in \cP$ a public set of samplable morphisms $\cM_q$. We require that all samplable primes $q$ satisfy $q< \fieldboundsizeexponent$.

Now, the claim \eqref{e:new_to_lifted_polring_claim}  is replaced by the claim
%
\begin{equation}\label{e:new_to_projected_lifted_polring_claim}
\langle \pirand\circ \picanon^{-1}(\uu^{(1)}) \otimes \pirand\circ \picanon^{-1}(\uu^{(2)}) , \pirand(\bitstext(\bff)) \rangle = \pirand(\mu') \qquad \text{in } \FF_q,
\end{equation}
(note that since $\bitstext(\bff)$ is full of bits, $\pirand(\bitstext(\bff))$ is simply an interpretation of these bits as the elements $0,1$ of $\FF_q$). 

Now the prover and verifier set out to prove \eqref{e:new_to_projected_lifted_polring_claim}. Recall that the prover has also sent a commitment to $\Enc_\cC\circ \pack \circ \pi_2(\bitstext(\bff))$. Hence we are exactly in the same setting as in Step 4 of \cref{s:to_simple_bitz}. Accordingly, at this point, the prover and verifier apply Steps 4, 5 and 6 of \cref{s:to_simple_bitz}, taking 
\begin{align*}
\uu^{(1)}{} &\gets \pirand\circ \picanon^{-1}\left(\uu^{(1)}\right)\in \FF_{q}^{\codedim_1},\\
\uu^{(2)}{} &\gets \pirand\circ \picanon^{-1}\left(\uu^{(2)}\right)\in \FF_{q}^{\codedim_2},\\
\mu&\gets \pirand(\mu')\in \FF_q.
\end{align*}


\subsubsection{Round-by-round knowledge soundness}\label{s:to_soundness_rings}
\albertimportant{Review}

As in \cref{s:to_soundness_prime}, we view the protocol as a composition $\Pi_1\circ \Pi_2\circ \Pi_3$, where now $\Pi_1$ consists of Steps 2 and 3 of \cref{s:to_general_rings} followed by Step 4 and the first half of Step 5 of \cref{s:to_simple_bitz}. After Step 3, the prover and verifier are exactly in the same spot as in Step 4 of \cref{s:to_simple_bitz}. Hence the analysis of Steps 4 and 5 in \cref{s:to_soundness_prime} applies word by word. The protocols $\Pi_2$ and $\Pi_3$ are exactly the same as in \cref{s:to_simple_bitz}. Because of this, below we only discuss Steps 1, 2 and 3. The full soundness statement of \cref{s:to_general_rings} can be found in \cref{t:thm_LinModFieldBits}, and is proved in \cref{a:LinModFieldBin}.

\bigskip

\noindent\emph{\underline{Steps 1 and 2 (commitment and bitification)}.} The prover commits to a bit vector $\bfg=\bitstext(\bff)$ rather than to $\bff$, and after Step 2 the claim is that the prover knows $\bfg\in \bits^{\codedim}\subseteq \ZZ^\codedim$ such that
%
\begin{equation}\label{e:to_soundness_1_rings}
\langle \uu, \psi(\bfg)\rangle = \mu \quad \text{in } R, \qquad \text{and} \qquad f= \Enc_\cC \circ \pack\circ \pi_2(\bfg).
\end{equation}
%
As in \cref{s:to_soundness_prime}, these steps incur no knowledge soundness loss: given any such $\bfg$, the vector $\bff\defeq L\cdot \bfg\in S^{\codedim_0}$ is a valid witness for $((\mu,\vv),f)$, since $\bfg$ is a bitification of $\bff$ and $\langle \vv, \psi(\bff)\rangle = \langle \uu, \psi(\bfg)\rangle=\mu$ by the computation in Step 2. The only new point is that $\bff$ automatically lies in $(S_{\leq B}^{<D})^{\codedim_0}$, because every entry of $L\cdot \bfg$ is a polynomial in $\eta_1,\ldots,\eta_k$ of degree less than $D$ with coefficients in $\range{0}{2^B-1}_S$: committing to bits enforces the size bounds on the witness, and no separate range proof is needed.

\bigskip 

\noindent\emph{\underline{Step 3}.} If Step 3 is skipped, \eqref{e:to_soundness_1_rings} is already a claim of the form \eqref{e:to_soundness_1}. Otherwise, Step 3 is the only source of soundness error in $\Pi_1$, and its analysis generalizes that of \cref{s:to_soundness_prime} from integers to polynomials, following Zinc+ \cite{zincplus}. Let $\cE$ be the event that the projected claim \eqref{e:new_to_projected_lifted_polring_claim} holds for some $\bfg\in \bits^\codedim$ with $f= \Enc_\cC \circ \pack\circ \pi_2(\bfg)$ (note that $\bfg$ is fixed by $f$ before $q,\pirand$ are sampled), but \eqref{e:to_soundness_1_rings} does not. Consider the polynomial
%
$$
h\defeq \langle \picanon^{-1}(\uu^{(1)}) \otimes \picanon^{-1}(\uu^{(2)}) , \bfg \rangle-\mu' \ \in \ \ZZ[X_1,\ldots, X_{k'}].
$$
%
Since $\picanon$ is a ring homomorphism with $\picanon\circ\picanon^{-1}=\mathsf{id}$ and $\picanon(\mu')=\mu$, we have $\picanon(h)=\langle \uu, \psi(\bfg)\rangle-\mu\neq 0$, so $h\neq 0$. Since $\bfg$ has bit entries, the inner product above satisfies the same degree and bit-size bounds as an honest $\mu'$, and so does $\mu'$ by the verifier's checks; hence $h$ has total degree at most $D_\ZZ$ and coefficients of absolute value at most $2^{B_\ZZ+1}$. Finally, since $\pirand$ is a ring homomorphism restricting to $\pi_q$ on $\ZZ$, the projected claim states precisely that $\pirand(h)=0$. If, for example, $\cM_q$ consists of the maps $h\mapsto \pi_q(h)(\bm{\alpha})$ for $\bm{\alpha}\in \FF_q^{k'}$ (reduce the coefficients modulo $q$, then evaluate at $\bm{\alpha}$), then either $q$ divides every coefficient of $h$, which as in \cref{s:to_soundness_prime} happens with probability at most $(B_\ZZ+1)/(\lfloor \log q_{\min}\rfloor\cdot |\cP|)$, or $\pi_q(h)$ is a nonzero polynomial of total degree at most $D_\ZZ$, which vanishes at a random $\bm{\alpha}$ with probability at most $D_\ZZ/q_{\min}$ by the Schwartz--Zippel lemma. Altogether,
%
\begin{equation}\label{e:to_kappa_0_rings}
\Pr[\cE]\leq \kappa_0\defeq \frac{D_\ZZ}{q_{\min}} + \frac{B_\ZZ+1}{\lfloor\log q_{\min}\rfloor\cdot |\cP|},
\end{equation}
%
cf.\ \eqref{e:to_kappa_0} for the case $k'=0$, and \cref{l:examples_morphisms} for the concrete bounds $D_\ZZ, B_\ZZ$ of \cref{c:core_iop}. Both checks on $\mu'$ are essential: without the bit-size bound the prover could choose $\mu'$ so that every coefficient of $h$ is a multiple of $\prod_{q\in\cP} q$, and without the degree bound so that $h$ is a multiple of $\prod_{q\in \cP}(X_1^{q}-X_1)$, which vanishes on $\FF_q^{k'}$ for every $q\in \cP$. As in \cref{s:to_soundness_prime}, $\Pi_1$ is thus round-by-round knowledge sound with errors $(\kappa_0,0)$, and the errors of the whole protocol are $\kappa_0$ followed by those of $\Pi_2$ and $\Pi_3$.





\subsection{GKR for low-entropy batched grand products}\label{s:to_low_entropy_gkr}
  In Step 5 of \ftwoz \ (\cref{s:to_simple_bitz}) the prover must prove that
                    \begin{equation}\label{e:intro_eq_4_repeated}g^{\langle \pi^{-1}(\uu^{(1)}), \bitstext(\bff)_{*j}\rangle} = \prod_{i\in [\codedim_1]} \left(\left(g^{\pi^{-1}(\uu^{(1)}_i)} -1\right)\cdot \bitstext(\bff)_{ij}  +1\right) =g^{\mu_j} \qquad \text{in  } \comfield \qquad \text{for all } j\in [\codedim_2].
        \end{equation} 
        %
        As we mentioned, this is by far the most expensive step in \ftwoz. We next explain how we prove \eqref{e:intro_eq_4_repeated} in an efficient manner. We make the crucial  assumption that  both $\codedim_1$ and $\codedim_2$ are $O(\sqrt{\codedim})$ (see \cref{s:instantiation} for exact parameter instantiations). This assumption is relatively simple to attain, as we have discussed throughout \cref{s:intro,s:tech_overview}. At a protocol level, we simply prove \eqref{e:intro_eq_4_repeated} with a standard GKR protocol for grand products \cite{thaler13}. Concretely, let $\codedim_1=2^{r_1}$ and $\codedim_2=2^{r_2}$. We set up multilinear polynomials $L_{0}, L_1,\ldots, L_{r_1}$ on $r_2 + i$ variables for $i\in \range{0}{r_1}$, respectively, such that 
        %
                $$
        L_{r_1}(\bb,\cc) = \left(g^{\pi^{-1}(\uu^{(1)}_{\mathsf{int}(\bb)})} -1\right)\cdot \bitstext(\bff)_{\mathsf{int}(\bb),\mathsf{int}(\cc)}  +1  \quad  \text{ for all } (\bb,\cc) \in \bits^{r_1} \times \bits^{r_2},
        $$
        and, for $i=0,\ldots, r_1-1$
      \begin{equation}\label{e:to_gkr_polys}
  L_{i}(\bb,\cc)  =  L_{i+1}(\bb,0,\cc) \cdot L_{i+1}(\bb,1, \cc)  \quad \text{for all } (\bb,\cc)\in \bits^{i} \times \bits^{r_2}.
    \end{equation}
    We then use a series of sumchecks to certify \cref{e:to_gkr_polys} for all $i=0,\ldots, r_1-1$, and $L_{0}(\cc) = g^{\mu_{\mathsf{int}(\cc)}}$ for all $\cc\in \bits^{r_2}$.  
    
    
    We emphasize again that, at a protocol level, this GKR scheme is completely standard. See \cref{s:gkr_low_entropy} for all the details. 
    
    
    At the end of the GKR grand product, the prover and verifier are left with MLE evaluation claims of the form:
    %
    \begin{equation}\label{e:intro_grand_product_eq_1}
    \mle{g^{\pi^{-1}(\uu^{(1)})}}(\rr) = a,\qquad \mle{\bitstext(\bff)_{*j}}(\rr)=a_j \qquad \text{for all } j \in [\codedim_2]
    \end{equation}
    for some $\rr\in \comfield^{r_1}$, where $\mle{*}$ denotes MLE extension, and $g^{\pi^{-1}(\uu^{(1)})}$ is the vector with entries $g^{\pi^{-1}(\uu^{(1)}_i)}$. 
    
    Since $\uu$ is public, the verifier checks the first claim of \eqref{e:intro_grand_product_eq_1} on its own in time $O(\sqrt{\codedim})$. This is one among several reasons why the tensor split of $\uu=\uu^{(1)} \otimes \uu^{(2)}$ and splitting the original claim into a batch of $\codedim_2$ claims  pay off (see \cref{s:why_the_tensor_split}). 


    
    We design novel (to our knowledge \albert{check}) prover optimizations for the GKR grand product (without altering the protocol itself), see \cref{s:gkr_low_entropy} for details. Namely, we exploit the following property, which is enabled, again, by the tensor split of $\uu=\uu^{(1)} \otimes \uu^{(2)}$ and splitting the original claim into a batch of $\codedim_2$ claims:
    \medskip

    \begin{quote}\emph{\textbf{Low entropy grand products:}} There are at most $\codedim_1=O(\sqrt{\codedim})$ values that $L_{r_1}(\bb,\cc)$ can take on the hypercube of size $\codedim_1\cdot \codedim_2 = \codedim$. \end{quote}

     

    This property trickles down to different stages of the GKR protocol. For example, the above property implies that $L_{r_1-1}(\bb',\cc)$ as well can take only an $O(\sqrt{\codedim})$ amount of values (with worse constants now) across all $(\bb',\cc)\in \bits^{r_1-1}\times \bits^{r_2}$. Similarly, the coefficients of the first messages sent by the prover at the last stages of the GKR protocol (which involve the most expensive sumchecks), also benefit from a low entropy property. 

      This allows the  prover to compute small lookup tables that are kept on cache and reused across all values of $\cc\in [\codedim_2]$. We build different lookup tables used at different stages of the GKR protocol.  For example, we use 4 lookup tables in the first round of the last sumcheck of GKR (the most expensive sumcheck, being on $r_1+r_2$ variables). These tables are used to compute the first prover message of such sumcheck. We then construct 5 more tables to compute the second prover message, and finally we use one last table to compute the third prover message (see \cref{tab:gkr_tables}). Similarly, we build lookup tables for the first rounds of the second and third last sumchecks of the GKR protocol.
      




\begin{comment}


    
    \paragraph{Comparison with the grand products of \cref{lev,binius64}} \albertimportant{do we really need this part if we already have \cref{r:comparison_grand_product_binius_lev}}

    Binius64 \cite{?}, a proof system for polynomial constraints over $\comfield$,  multiplication of integers modulo $m=|\comfield|-1$, say $x\cdot y = z \mod m$,  by proving $g^{x\cdot y} = g^z$. More concretely, given vectors $\ww_1,\ww_2,\ww_3\in \ZZ^n$, Binius64 proves $\ww_{3i}= \ww_{1i}\cdot \ww_{2i} \mod m$ for all $i\in [n]$ by proving \begin{equation}\label{e:to_binius_product}g^{\ww_{1i}\cdot \ww_{2i}} = g^{\ww_{3i}} \qquad \text{for all } i\in [n].\end{equation} Using $\comfield=\FF_{2^{128}}$, Binius64  is then able to prove \emph{integer} multiplication (without modular reduction) of $64$-bit integers, by mixing in a simple small constraint over $\comfield$. 

    Intuitively, the main qualitative difference between Binius64 and our setting is that the former has elements of the form ``witness $\cdot$ witness'' in the exponent of $g$. On the other hand, our exponents contain linear combinations of elements of the form ``(public value) $\cdot$ witness``, and the public values split into a tensor product. This ultimately allows us to create $O(\sqrt{\codedim})$ grand products where the constant elements $g^{\uu_i}$ are publicly known and the same across all $O(\sqrt{\codedim})$ grand products, yielding the  low entropy property that our optimizations exploit. 
    
    In the Binius64 setting, on the other hand, this split is not available because the witness vector, in general, does not admit a tensor split \albert{ is it only because of that? or are there more reasons?}.

    Binius64 reduces proving \eqref{e:to_binius_product} to proving an MLE evaluation claim for the vector $(g^{\ww_{1i}\cdot \ww_{2i}})_i$. To do this, it uses a combination of LogUp* \cite{?}, a grand product PIOP, and the Frobenius automorphism. Concretely,  letting $\aa \defeq ( g^{\ww_{1i}})_i$ and writing $\ww_2=\sum_{j\in [64]} 2^{j-1} \cdot \ww_2^{(j)}$ with $\ww_2^{(j)}$ consisting of bits, 
    \begin{equation}\label{e:to_binius_product}\aa_i^{2^{j-1}\cdot \ww_{2i}^{(j)}} = g^{\ww_{3i}} \qquad \text{for all } i\in [n], \ j\in [64].\end{equation}
    
    \eqref{e:to_binius_product} is replaced by 
    \begin{equation}\label{e:to_binius_product}g^{\ww_{1i}\cdot \ww_{2i}} = g^{\ww_{3i}} \qquad \text{for all } i\in [n].\end{equation}
