\subsubsection{Efficiency and comparison to other ``prove-in-the-exponent'' usages}\label{r:comparison_grand_product_binius_lev}

We next discuss other existing approaches to proving foreign field statements in the exponent of a generator of $\comfield^*$ (the multiplicative subgroup of a binary field), in the context of hash-based SNARKs. Notice that elliptic curve-based PCS's usually perform commitments and opening proofs in the exponent of a generator of a large elliptic curve subgroup.  

   
        \paragraph{Binius64} For example, \cite{binius64}  multiplies pairs of 64-bit integer witness elements in the exponent of $g$. This is substantially different from our setting where, when binary fields come into play, we are only interested in proving an integer claim about a linear combination of elements of the form ``(public values)$\cdot$ (witness elements)'', i.e.\ $\langle \vv, \ww\rangle = \mu$ in $\ZZ$, with the public values $\vv$ admitting a tensor split $\vv=\vv^{(1)} \otimes \vv^{(2)}$ (also \ftwoz \ can prove multiplication of all sorts of ring elements, not just 64-bit integers).   
        This simpler setting is what allows us to use the low entropy optimizations designed in this work.  Crucially, \textbf{the reason why we only need to address this setting is because we start off with constraints and a PIOP over integers/fields/rings (Steps 2 to 4 inclusive), and only ever move to work on binary fields when we need to prove an MLE evaluation of the witness (a linear claim) over a ``small'' prime field (Step 5)}. 

        
        
        %The tensor split allows us to replace the claim into $\codedim_2=|\vv^{(2)}|$ claims $\langle \vv^{(1)}, \ww_{*j}\rangle = \eta_j$ in $\ZZ$ for all $j\in [\codedim_2]$. These claims are then each proved ``in the exponent''. Since $\vv^{(1)}$ is common across all claims, and $\ww$ is filled with bits, the resulting grand products are low entropy. 
        
      
        \cite{binius64} on the other hand multiplies 64-bit integer witness in the exponent directly. More precisely, \cite{binius64} proves the identity $g^{\ww_{1i}\cdot \ww_{2i}} = g^{2^{64}\cdot \ww_{3i}} \cdot g^{\ww_{4i}}$ for $\ww_1, \ldots, \ww_4$ vectors of 64-bit integers (together with a small additional constraint to address possible overflow). This is done through a combination of LogUp* \cite{logupstar}, a GKR grand product (for relatively small products), and the Frobenius automorphism trick. It does not seem possible to follow a similar optimization strategy to ours.
       

        We  emphasize again that \ftwoz \ supports proving operations of all kinds of ring elements (small and large integers, field elements, lattice ring elements, extension field elements), as opposed to \cite{binius64} where multiplication is limited to 64-bit integers.

        In \cref{s:experiments} we compare the performance of \ftwoz \ and Binius64 when multiplying integers.  






        \paragraph{The note \cite{lev}.} In the note \cite{lev}, Soukhanov proposes a scheme for committing to a vector of bits $\ww$ over the binary field $\FF_{2^{127}}$, and proving MLE evaluation claims over the prime field  $\FF_{2^{127}-1}$, for a vector of elements from   $\FF_{2^{127}-1}$ obtained by packing bits from $\ww$ together. To do so, at a high level, the MLE evaluation claims are proved ``in the exponent'' in the binary field $\FF_{2^{127}}$, leveraging that  the multiplicative subgroup of  $\FF_{2^{127}}$ has prime order $|\FF_{2^{127}}^*| = 2^{127}-1$. The proposed approach is substantially different from ours: \cite{lev} essentially takes a polylogarithmic PCS over the prime field $\FF_{2^{127}-1}$, such as Basefold \cite{basefold}, WHIR \cite{whir}, etc.,  and executes it ``in the exponent of $\FF_{2^{127}}$''. To do so, it relies on the lookup argument LogUp* \cite{logupstar}, grand product arguments (for small products), the Frobenius trick, and additional commitments, for each of the $\log\ell$ rounds.  
        
        In its execution,  \cite{lev} and Binius64 \cite{binius64} seem to us to be more similar than any of these two to \ftwoz. Indeed, they both use, mostly, the same collection of subprotocols, in a similar manner.  \cref{tab:fields-witch} in \cref{s:exp_fields_witch} compares Soukhanov's implementation of \cite{lev} with \ftwoz\ on the same machine. In \cref{s:experiments} we perform extensive comparisons between \ftwoz\ and Binius64.  % Because of this we expect both approaches to have comparable performance profiles, though we do not validate this hypothesis due to \cite{lev} not reporting performance nor providing an implementation.
        
        Conceptually, \cite{lev} shares with \ftwoz \ the aim of committing to bits in a binary field, while proving MLE claims of those bits in some other structure $R$. Note that \cite{lev} is limited to $R=\FF_{2^{127}-1}$, while \ftwoz \ can handle essentially all rings and fields $R$ of practical interest, achieving 128 bits of security and using $\FF_{2^{128}}$ as binary field. Additionally it is not clear whether \cite{lev} can leverage any of our low-entropy oriented optimizations without substantially modifying the approach, incorporating some of our ideas. 

        \albertimportant{The comparison of Lev and \ftwoz\ right now has several unbacked claims (which I believe are true, but can't really prove them, especially without an implementation). So probably it's better to not push it, remove the unbacked claims, and leave it as it is}
        
\subsection{Virtual $\FF_2$-linear transforms, NP-complete multi-domain linear relations, and hybrid proof systems}\label{s:to_f2_virtual}



Observe that, at the end of the day, Step 5 of \cref{s:to_simple_bitz} outputs a linear claim of the form\footnote{We start with the batched version of the claim, but the ideas in this section can be adapted to \eqref{e:intro_eq_5} as well.}
\begin{equation}\label{e:intro_eq_5_repeated} \langle \zz, \bitstext(\ww) \rangle = c \qquad \text{in  } \comfield,
\end{equation} 
for some $\zz\in \comfield^{\codedim}$ and $c\in \comfield$. This is a standard linear claim over $\comfield$ for the vector $\bitstext(\ww)$ which we committed to previously (in this subsection we denote the witness by $\ww$, to free the letter $\bff$). 


Now, say that we know that $\bitstext(\ww)$ is a linear transformation of a vector $\bff\in  \FF_2^{\codedim'} \subseteq  \comfield^{\codedim'}$, so that
%
$$
\bitstext(\ww) = M \cdot \bff \qquad \text{ in } \comfield,
$$
for some matrix $M\in \FF_2^{\codedim\times \codedim'}$.
Then 
%
\begin{equation}\label{e:intro_eq_5_virtual}
 \langle \zz, \bitstext(\ww) \rangle  =  \langle M^{\mathsf{T}}\cdot \zz, \bff \rangle = c \qquad \text{in  } \comfield.
\end{equation}
Hence, in this scenario, one can commit to $\bff$ instead of $\bitstext(\ww)$ (again, in packed form if necessary),  replace the claim \eqref{e:intro_eq_5_repeated} by \eqref{e:intro_eq_5_virtual}, and prove the latter in Step 6 of \cref{s:to_simple_bitz}. 



Thus, \ftwoz\ can be expanded so as to prove claims where we are given a public vector $\inp=\xx\in R^\inplen$, and the prover claims  it knows $\wit=\bff\in \FF_2^{\codedim'}$ such that 

\begin{equation}\label{e:intro_eq_0_virtual}
\begin{cases}
&\pp_i(\xx,\ww)=0 \qquad \text{in  } R \qquad \text{ for all } i\in [m], \\ &\bitstext(\ww)=\pi_2^{-1}(M\cdot \bff)
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
%
where  $\pp=(\pp_i)_{i\in [m]}$ is a collection of polynomials over $R$; and $\pi_2$ is reduction modulo $2$ \albert{I may have been loose about when $\bitstext(\ww)$ is in $\FF_2$ and when in integers during the technical overview, review}.  

A particularly interesting instantiation of this relation is the following R1CS-like relation over the integers, where $\ww$ is a vector of bits (hence $\ww=\bitstext(\ww)$) and $\ww= \pi_2^{-1}(M\cdot \bff)$:


    
%
\begin{align*}
&\Relation_{\mathsf{R1CS-bits-virtual}}(\ZZ)\\ &\qquad \qquad\qquad \defeq \left\{ \begin{aligned}
    &\inp= (\xx, A,B,C, M)\in \FF_2^{\inplen} \times (\ZZ^{m\times \codedim})^3 \times \FF_2^{\codedim \times (\inplen+\codedim')}\\
    & \wit = \bff \in \bits^{\codedim'} \subseteq \FF_2^{\codedim'}
\end{aligned}\  \left| \  \begin{aligned} %&f=\Enc_\cC \circ \pack \circ \pi_2(\bff)\\ 
&(A\cdot \ww) \circ (B\cdot \ww) = C \cdot \ww\ \text{in } \ZZ \\
&\ww= \pi_2^{-1}\left(  M\cdot (\xx, \bff)\right)   \end{aligned} \right.\right\}
\end{align*}


An interesting subset  of $\Relation_{\mathsf{R1CS-bits-virtual}}(\ZZ)$
 is that where $A$ and $B$ are zero matrices. We call the resulting relation the \emph{CM} relation because of the matrices $C$ and $M$:
 \begin{equation}\label{e:CM_rel}
\Relation_{\mathsf{CM}}(\ZZ) \defeq \left\{ \begin{aligned}
    &\inp= (\xx, C, M)\in \FF_2^{\inplen} \times \ZZ^{m\times \codedim} \times \FF_2^{\codedim \times (\inplen+\codedim')}\\
    & \wit = \bff \in \bits^{\codedim'} \subseteq \FF_2^{\codedim'}
\end{aligned}\  \left| \  \begin{aligned} %&f=\Enc_\cC \circ \pack \circ \pi_2(\bff)\\ 
&C \cdot \ww = 0\ \text{in } \ZZ \\
&\ww= \pi_2^{-1}\left(  M\cdot (\xx, \bff)\right) \end{aligned} \right.\right\}
\end{equation}
%
In a sense, the CM relation places only linear constraints over its witness (with some constraints happening in $\comfield$ and others in $\ZZ$). Yet, the CM relation is NP-complete, as shown below in \cref{r:CM_is_NP_complete}. 



Indeed, \textbf{we arithmetize SHA-256 as a CM instance}. Our arithmetization leads to witnesses of $7144$ bits per compression, $4.4\times$ smaller than those of Binius64 \cite{binius64} and Flock \cite{flock} (see \cref{tab:sha_arithmetization}).

\begin{remark}[The $\RELCM$ relation is NP-complete]\label{r:CM_is_NP_complete}
Any Boolean circuit can be arithmetized as an instance of $\RELCM$. Indeed, since any boolean circuit can be written as a system of equations using the operations XOR and AND, it suffices to prove that, given $x,y,z\in \bits$, we can express both of the following constraints as an instance of $\RELCM$:
%
$$    x \xor y = z,\qquad \qquad 
    x \band y = z.
$$
Indeed, $x \xor y = z$ if and only if $\pi_2^{-1}(\pi_2(x) + \pi_2(y))=z$, where here $+$ means addition modulo $2$. Further, $x \band y = z$  if and only if  
%
$$
2\cdot z =  x+y - \pi_2^{-1}(\pi_2(x) + \pi_2(y)).
$$    
One can verify this last equivalence for example  by directly inspecting that it holds for the four possible combinations of $x,y\in \bits$.
\end{remark}





%\subsection{The $\mathsf{CM}$ constraint system: composition of  $\ZZ$-linear  with $\FF_2$-linear constraints}

%\albertimportant{Not sure this is needed depending on how we write the technical overview.} 

%\albertimportant{Maybe we should have general R1CS here, not just linear R1CS. And then describe the CM relation a subset of the more general R1CS relation}


%\begin{remark}
%
%    $\RELCM$ is related to PIMSAT and $\LinBitsDual^{\mathsf{virtual}}$ in the sense that $\RELCM$ is the result of taking  $\PIMSAT(\bits, \pp_{\mathsf{CM}}, \bot)$ (i.e.\ the PIMSAT relation with $R=\ZZ, \moduledegree=0, B=1$ and no ideals) and replacing the vector input-witness vector in PIMSAT by a vector of the form $\mathbf{h}= \pi_2^{-1}\left(\Mmatrix\cdot \pi_2(\bff)\right)$.
    %
%\end{remark}

\subsubsection{\ftwoz \ for fully hybrid constraints over $\ZZ$ and $\comfield$.}\label{s:hybrid_snark}



Say the computation involves both  $\FF_2$ operations and other more integer-like operations, like integer multiplication or multiplication mod $2^{32}$. This is the case, for example, with Virtual Machines running RISC-V instructions. To prove these computations we use an arithmetization with constraints both in $\ZZ$ and in $\FF_2$. We build a SNARK for such constraints as follows. Consider a witness $(\bff_\ZZ, \bff_{\FF_2})$ split into an integer vector $\bff_\ZZ\in \ZZ^{\codedim_\ZZ}$ used in the integer constraints, and an $\FF_2$ vector $\bff_{\FF_2}\in \FF_2^{\codedim_{\FF_2}}$ used in the $\FF_2$ constraints. We can also assume that the $\FF_2$ constraints  use some entries from $\bff_{\ZZ}$ which are integer bits (looking at those integer bits as elements from $\FF_2$), otherwise we would have two unrelated constraints. Accordingly we have $\bff_\ZZ=(\bff_{\ZZ}^{(1)}, \bff_{\ZZ}^{(2)})$, where $\bff_{\ZZ}^{(1)}$ is a vector of arbitrary integers, and $\bff_{\ZZ}^{(2)}$ is a vector of bits.

We want to prove that $(\inp_\ZZ, \bff_\ZZ)\in \Relation_\ZZ$ and $(\inp_{\FF_2}, \pi_2(\bff_{\ZZ}^{(2)}), \bff_{\FF_2})\in \Relation_{\FF_2}$, where $\Relation_\ZZ$ and $\Relation_{\FF_2}$ are relations over $\ZZ$ and $\FF_2$, respectively, and where $\pi_2(\bff_{\ZZ}^{(2)})$ denotes the vector of bits $\bff_{\ZZ}^{(2)}$ seen in $\FF_2$. The overall proof system proceeds as follows:  
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,nosep]
        \item Commit to the concatenation of $\bitstext(\bff_{\ZZ})=(\bitstext(\bff_{\ZZ}^{(1)}, \bff_{\ZZ}^{(2)})$ and $\bff_{\FF_2}$ over a binary field $\comfield$ (in packed form).
        \item  Partially run the previously described \ftwoz-SNARK for the statement $(\inp_\ZZ, \bff_\ZZ)\in \Relation_\ZZ$. Run it until we have an inner product claim $\langle\ww, \bitstext(\bff_\ZZ)\rangle= \eta$  over $\comfield$. 
        \item Then partially run any SNARK for binary fields such as \cite{binius64}, for the claim $(\inp_{\FF_2}, \pi_2(\bff_{\ZZ}^{(2)}), \bff_{\FF_2})\in \Relation_{\FF_2}$. Run it until we have an inner product claim $\langle\ww', (\pi_2(\bff_{\ZZ}^{(2)}), \bff_{\FF_2})\rangle= \eta'$  over $\comfield$.
        \item At this point we have two inner product claims over $\comfield$ for $\pi_2(\bitstext(\bff_\ZZ))$ and $(\pi_2(\bff_{\ZZ}^{(2)}), \bff_{\FF_2})$, two vectors that we committed to. Now we prove both in a batched manner using WHIR \cite{whir} or any other similar PCS over $\comfield$. 
        
        If necessary the inner product claims can be reduced to MLE evaluation claims before, via a batched sumcheck.
    \end{enumerate}
\bigskip
\noindent We demonstrate the practicality of this approach by proving a large amount of chained SHA-256 compressions, together with a large amount of multiplications modulo $2^{32}$. The results can be found in \cref{tab:intro_table} and in \cref{s:experiments_sha_modmul}. \albert{Comment there's no link witness but it's irrelvant for performance}




\subsection{Further work and open problems}
 \ftwoz \ is not a zero-knowledge scheme as of now. Enabling zero-knowledge for it is ongoing work of ours. 
 
 \ftwoz's verifier and proof sizes have asymptotic square root costs. Making them polylogarithmic is an intriguing problem which we leave open.



\section{Preliminaries}

\paragraph{General notation} Given an integer $n\geq 1$, we write $[n]$ to denote the set $\{1,\ldots, n\}$. Given integers $m\leq n$, we let $\range{m}{n}$ denote the set of integers $\{m, m+1,. \ldots, n\}$.

We use $\circ$ to denote composition of maps, so that for maps $g: S_1\to S_2$ and $f:S_2\to S_3$, we have $f\circ g: S_1 \to S_3$ defined as $f\circ g(x) = f(g(x))$. 

Any map $f:S_1 \to S_2$ applies  entry-wise to vectors from $S_1^n$, for any $n$, and similarly for matrices. A map $f$ also applies coefficient-wise to polynomials with coefficients in $S_1$. A multiplication of a vector  or a matrix by a scalar means that we multiply each entry of the vector or matrix by that scalar. 

We denote vectors with boldface letters, e.g.\ $\uu,\vv$. By $\uu_i$ we denote the $i$-th position of $\uu$. Sometimes we index the entries of a vector $\uu$ with elements of a boolean hypercube $\bits^{t}$. 

We look at vectors both as row vectors and column vectors. When performing matrix-vector multiplications, it will always be clear which one we intend. Given a ring $R$, a  matrix $M\in R^{n\times m}$ and  vectors $\uu\in R^m$, $\vv\in R^n$, one has
%
\begin{equation}\label{e:matrix_transpose_inner_product}
\langle M\cdot \uu, \vv \rangle = \langle \uu, M^{\mathsf{T}} \cdot \vv\rangle,
\end{equation}
%
where $M^{\mathsf{T}}$ denotes transposition and $\langle \cdot, \cdot \rangle$ denotes inner product.

\subsection{Algebra}\label{s:prelims_algebra}
\paragraph{Concrete fields and rings} We write $\FF_q$ for the finite field with $q$ elements (where $q$ is a prime or a prime power), $\QQ$ for the field of rational numbers, and $\ZZ$ for the ring of integers. For an integer $n\geq 1$, $\ZZ_n$ denotes the ring of integers modulo $n$, with the convention $\ZZ_0\defeq \ZZ$. When $n$ is prime, $\ZZ_n=\FF_n$. We let $$\pi_n :\ZZ\to \ZZ_n$$ denote reduction modulo $n$. For $n\geq 1$, we fix the inverse $\pi_n^{-1} : \ZZ_n \to \range{0}{n-1}\subseteq \ZZ$ mapping each element $a$ in $\ZZ_n$ to the integer in the range  $\range{0}{n-1}$ representing $a$. 



\paragraph{Rings in general} All rings in this work are associative, commutative, have a multiplicative identity. Given a ring $R$, we write $R[X_1, \ldots, X_n]$ for the ring of polynomials with coefficients in $R$ and variables $X_1, \ldots, X_n$ (not necessarily multilinear). 

 The \emph{characteristic} of a ring $\ring$, denoted $\Char(R)$, is the smallest $c\geq 1$, if it exists, such that $1 + \overset{c}{\ldots} + 1 = 0$ in $\ring$. Otherwise, if $1 + \overset{c}{\ldots} + 1 \neq 0$ for all $c$, the characteristic is zero.
 By $\range{0}{n}_\ring$ we denote all elements in $\ring$ of the form $1 + \overset{i}{\ldots} + 1$ for $i=0,\ldots, n$. For every ring $R$ we have a canonical embedding 
 %
 $$
    \ZZ_{\Char(R)} \subseteq R
 $$
of the ring of integers modulo $\Char(R)$ into $R$. Namely, the set $\{n \cdot 1 \mid n\in \ZZ\} \subseteq R$ for $1$ the identity element of $R$, forms the ring  $\ZZ_{\Char(R)}$. Further, $\pi_{\Char(R)}$ induces a natural homomorphism (not necessarily surjective)
%
\begin{equation}\label{e:Z_to_ring}
\pi_{\Char(R)} : \ZZ \to R
\end{equation}
sending $n\in \ZZ$ to $n\cdot 1$ for $1$ the identity of $R$. 

Given a set or vector of integers $\ww\in \ZZ^m$, we define \begin{equation}\label{e:integers_subscript_proj}\ww_R= \pi_{\Char(R)}(\ww).\end{equation}
 
 \paragraph{Finitely generated rings} Further, all rings in this work are \emph{finitely generated} as rings. Equivalently, every ring $R$ in this work is isomorphic to a quotient $$R=\ZZ[X_1,\ldots, X_k]/I$$ of a polynomial ring over $\ZZ$ by an ideal $I$. This condition is satisfied by, for example:
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,nosep]
\item All finite fields and the rings $\ZZ_n$; 
\item $\ZZ$, and the polynomial rings $\ZZ[X]$ and $\FF_q[X]$
\item Arbitrary quotients of the previous rings, such as $\ZZ_n[X]/(h(X))$ for an arbitrary polynomial $h(X)$ and an arbitrary integer $n\geq 0$, which includes  lattice rings. 
\end{enumerate}
It is not satisfied by, for example, $\QQ$ and $\QQ[X]$, which are not finitely generated as rings. Some constructions in this work  impose further requirements on the rings they use. We state these when  needed.


Given a finitely generated ring $R$ with fixed generators $1,\eta_1,\ldots,\eta_k$ (we always list $1$ among the generators, in particular $k=0$ for $\ZZ_n$) and bounds $B, D\geq 1$, we let $R_{\leq B}^{<D}$ denote the set of elements of $R$ of the form $$h=\sum_{\aa\in \ZZ_{\geq 0}^k,\ a_1+\ldots+a_k<D} h_{\aa}\cdot \eta_1^{a_1}\cdots\eta_k^{a_k}, \qquad h_{\aa}\in \range{0}{2^B-1}_R,$$ i.e.\  $R_{\leq B}^{<D}$ is formed by the elements of $R$ that can be written as a polynomial of total degree less than $D$ in $\eta_1,\ldots, \eta_k$, with coefficients obtained by adding $1$ together (in $R$) less than $2^B$ times. 


%--

%(One could bound the degree in each generator separately instead; nothing in this work depends on this choice.) We write $\gamma_1=1,\gamma_2,\ldots,\gamma_{\moduledegree}$, where $\moduledegree=\binom{D-1+k}{k}$, for the monomials $\eta_1^{a_1}\cdots\eta_k^{a_k}$ appearing above (in some fixed order), so that $R_{\leq B}^{<D}=\{\sum_{i\in[\moduledegree]} h_i\cdot\gamma_i \mid h_i\in\range{0}{2^B-1}_R\}$. For example, $\ZZ_{\leq B}^{<D}=\range{0}{2^B-1}$ for every $D$; for $R=\FF_q[X]/(h(X))$ or $R=\ZZ_n[X]/(h(X))$ with generator $x$ and $D=\deg h$, the $\gamma_i$ form the power basis $1,x,\ldots,x^{D-1}$ (this covers all finite fields); and for $R=\ZZ[X]$ with generator $X$ we obtain the polynomials of degree less than $D$ with coefficients in $\range{0}{2^B-1}$.



 
 %An ideal $I$ of a ring $R$ generated by a set $S\subseteq R$ is denoted $(S)$.

$$
R_{\leq B}[X_1, \ldots, X_n]
$$
%
as the subset of $R[X_1, \ldots, X_n]$ formed by polynomials all whose coefficients are in the set $\range{0}{2^B-1}_R$.

We let $$\pi_{\FF}: \ZZ[X] \to \FF$$ be the  canonical homomorphism from $\ZZ[X]$ to $\FF$ obtained by reducing modulo the ideal $(q,a(X))$, i.e.\ by reducing coefficients modulo $q$ and then reducing the resulting polynomial modulo $a(X)$.

\begin{lemma}[Generalized Alon--F{\"u}redi Theorem~\cite{BCPS18}]
\label{lem:sz_exceptional_sets}
Let $R$ be a ring and let $S\subseteq R$ be a finite exceptional subset of $R$. Let $f(\YY)\in R[\YY]$ be a nonzero multilinear polynomial in $\mu$ variables. Then
$\Pr_{\rr\getsr S^\mu}[f(\rr)=0] \le \mu/|S|$.
\end{lemma}

\subsection{Linear codes and relations}\label{s:prelims_codes}

\paragraph{Linear codes}

Let $\cC \subseteq \FF^{\codelength}$ be a linear code over $\FF$ of dimension $\codedim$ and blocklength $\codelength$. For $h\geq 1$, the \emph{$h$-interleaving} of $\cC$ is the code $\cC^{\equiv h} \subseteq \left(\FF^{h}\right)^{\codelength}$ of dimension $\codedim\cdot h$ and blocklength $\codelength\cdot h$, whose codewords have the form $$\left((c_{i1},\ldots, c_{ih})\right)_{i\in [\codelength]} \qquad \text{for } (c_{1j},\ldots, c_{\codelength j})\in \cC \qquad \text{for all } j\in [h].$$

Given two words $w_1, w_2$ of length $n$, the relative Hamming distance between $w_1$ and $w_2$ (the fraction of positions in which they differ) is denoted 
%
$$\hamming(w_1,w_2).$$
The \emph{relative minimum distance} of $\cC$, denoted $\hamming(\cC)$, is the minimum of $\hamming(c_1,c_2)$ over distinct codewords $c_1,c_2\in \cC$. Given $\delta\in (0,1)$ and an integer $L\geq 1$, we say $\cC$ is \emph{$(\delta, L)$-list-decodable} if for every word $w\in \FF^{\codelength}$ there are at most $L$ codewords $c\in \cC$ with $\hamming(w,c)<\delta$. Every code is $(\delta,1)$-list-decodable for $\delta\leq \hamming(\cC)/2$.  The value $\hamming(\cC)/2$ is called the \emph{unique decoding radius} of $\cC$.

\paragraph{Relations}
A \emph{relation} $\Relation$ consists of triples  $(\inp,\oracle;\wit)$, where $\inp$ is the explicit instance, $\oracle$ the implicit instance or oracle, and $\wit$ the witness.  The corresponding language  is 
\(
  \LANG(\Relation)\ \defeq\ \bigl\{(\inp,\oracle)\ \bigm|\ \exists\,\wit \text{ such that } (\inp,\oracle;\wit)\in\Relation\bigr\}.
\)

\paragraph{Polynomial Equation Satisfiability (PESAT) over finite fields} We recall a relation from \cite{BCFW25} \albert{Linear time accumulation paper, or earlier?} over finite fields, which captures popular constraints such as R1CS, CCS, AIR (cf.\ \cite{BCFW25}), and lookup and permutation constraints (cf.\ \cite{zinc}).

Fix a finite field $\FF$, a witness length $\codedim$, an input length $\inplen$, a number of rows $n$, a collection of $n$ polynomials $\pp=(\mathbf{p}_{1}, \ldots, \pp_\numrows)$ on $\inplen+\codedim$ variables. 

\begin{definition}\label{d:pesat} The PESAT relation with respect to these parameters is defined as
$$
\PESAT(\FF,\pp) = \left\{\left. 
\begin{aligned}
    \inp &= \xx \in \FF^{\inplen},\\
    \oracle &= \bot ,\\
    \wit &=\bff \in \FF^\codedim
\end{aligned} 
\ \right| \ 
\begin{aligned}
    \mathbf{p}_{i}(\xx, \bff) = 0  \text{ for all } i\in [\numrows].
\end{aligned}
\right\}
$$
\end{definition}
%
Let $\cC\subseteq \FF^{\codelength}$ be a linear code of dimension $\codedim'$ and blocklength $\codelength$ over $\FF$.    Let $W\subseteq \FF^{\codedim}$ be a subset and let $T:W \to \FF^{\codedim'}$ be an arbitrary map (looking ahead, for us $T$ will be a packing map that packs elements from $\FF_2$ into elements of a larger binary field, so that $W=\FF_2^{\codedim}\subseteq \FF^{\codedim}$).  We define the \emph{Encoded PESAT  relation} as: %\albert{No reason to expose the interleaving this early}
%
$$
\PESAT(\FF, \pp, \cC, T) \defeq \left\{ \left(\begin{aligned} &\inp=\xx\in \FF^\inplen, \\&\oracle = f \in \FF^{\codelength}, \\ &\wit= \bff\in W\end{aligned}\right) \;\middle|\;  \begin{aligned}
&\Enc_{\cC}(T(\bff)) = f\\ \wedge \  &\pp_i(\xx, \bff) = 0  \text{ for all } i\in [\numrows]\end{aligned}\right\}
$$
We will drop the reference to $T$ whenever it is understood from the context. When $\numrows=1$ and $\xx$ are such that $\xx=(\vv,\mu)\in \FF^{\codedim}\times \FF$; $\pp_1(\xx,\ff)= \langle \ff, \vv\rangle -\mu$ (so that $\inplen=\codedim+1$), the relation $\PESAT(\FF,\pp, \cC, T)$ is called a \emph{constrained code} \cite{whir} \albert{WHIR?}.  We denote it $$\LIN(\FF,\cC,T).$$
%
\begin{definition}[Relaxation of $\PESAT$ and $\LIN$]Given $\delta \in (0,1)$, the \emph{relaxation} of $\PESAT$ and $\LIN$, denoted
%
$$
\PESAT^\delta, \qquad \qquad \LIN^{\delta}
$$
%
are the relations defined exactly as $\PESAT$ and $\LIN$, respectively, except that the constraint $\Enc_{\cC}(T(\bff))=f$ is replaced by
%
$$
\hamming(\Enc_{\cC}(T(\bff)), f) < \delta.
$$
\end{definition}

\paragraph{Multilinear extensions.} Given a field $\FF$ and $\uu\in \FF^{2^r}$ indexed by $\bits^r$, we denote by $\mle{\uu}\in \FF[X_1,\ldots,X_r]$  the unique multilinear polynomial with $\mle{\uu}(\bb)=\uu_\bb$ for all $\bb\in\bits^r$. For $\bm\alpha,\bm\beta\in\FF^r$ we let $\eq(\bm\alpha;\bm\beta)\defeq \prod_{i\in[r]}\bigl(\alpha_i\cdot \beta_i+(1-\alpha_i)\cdot (1-\beta_i)\bigr)$, so that $\mle{\uu}(\bm\alpha)=\sum_{\bb\in\bits^r}\uu_\bb\cdot \eq(\bb;\bm\alpha)$. We write $\eq(\bb,\cc;\bm\alpha)$ for $\eq((\bb,\cc);\bm\alpha)$.


\subsection{Interactive Oracle Reductions (IOR) of proximity}\label{s:IORs}

A public-coin \emph{Interactive Oracle Reduction (IOR) of proximity} $\Pi=(\prover,\verifier)$ from a relation $\Relation$ to a relation $\Relation'$ is specified as follows. The prover $\prover$ receives $(\inp, \oracle,\wit)\in \Relation$. The verifier $\verifier$ receives $\inp$ and query access to $\oracle$. They interact for $k$ rounds. In round $j\in[k]$, the prover sends an oracle $\provermsg_j$ and the verifier sends a uniformly random message $\verifiermsg_j\in\{0,1\}^{r_j}$. After the $k$ rounds, the verifier makes  queries to $\oracle$ and to $\provermsg_1,\ldots,\provermsg_k$. Then:
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item $\verifier$ either rejects or outputs a new  pair $(\inp', \oracle')$, where $\oracle'$ is specified implicitly in terms of the oracles $\oracle$ and $\provermsg_1,\ldots,\provermsg_k$.
  \item $\prover$ outputs a witness $\wit'$ such that $(\inp', \oracle', \wit')\in \Relation'$.
\end{itemize}
We write $ \langle \prover, \verifier\rangle(\inp,\oracle,\wit)$ for the interaction of prover and verifier during the IOR.

\begin{definition}[Completeness]
\label{def:ior-completeness}
$\Pi$ has \emph{(perfect) completeness}  if for every $(\inp, \oracle,\wit)\in\Relation$,
\[
  \Pr_{\verifiermsg_1,\dots,\verifiermsg_k}\bigl[\,(\inp',\oracle';\wit')\in \Relation'\, \mid \, (\inp',\oracle';\wit') \gets \langle \prover, \verifier\rangle(\inp,\oracle,\wit)\bigr]=1.
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
    An IOP for a relation $\Relation$ is an IOR from $\Relation$ to the trivial relation $\Relation_{\mathsf{triv}}=\{(\inp=\bot,\oracle=\bot, \wit=\bot)\}$.
\end{remark}

We proceed to define round-by-round knowledge soundness of an IOR, following \cite{BCFW25} in the formulation with relaxed relations of \cite{zook}.



\begin{definition}[Knowledge state function with relaxation]
\label{def:knowledge-state-function}
Let $\Pi = (\prover, \verifier)$ be an IOR from a relation $\Relation$ to $\Relation'$. A \emph{knowledge state function with relaxation $(\widetilde{\Relation}, \widetilde{\Relation'})$} for $\Pi$ is a (possibly inefficient) function $\kstate$ that, on input  $(\inp, \oracle)$, interaction transcript $\tr$, and knowledge state witness $\kswit$, outputs a bit and has the following properties.
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*,nosep]
  \item \textbf{Empty transcript.} If $\tr = \emptyset$ is the empty transcript, then $\kstate(\inp, \oracle, \tr, \kswit) = 1$ if and only if $(\inp, \oracle; \kswit) \in \widetilde{\Relation}$.
  \item \textbf{Prover moves.} If $\tr$ is a transcript where the prover is about to move and $\kstate(\inp, \oracle, \tr, \kswit) = 0$, then $\kstate(\inp, \oracle, \tr \Vert \provermsg, \kswit) = 0$ for every prover message $\provermsg$.
  \item \textbf{Full transcript.} If $\tr = (\provermsg_1, \verifiermsg_1, \ldots, \provermsg_k, \verifiermsg_k)$ is a full transcript, then $\kstate(\inp, \oracle, \tr, \kswit) = 1$ if and only if $\verifier$ on $\tr$ outputs $(\inp', \oracle')$ such that $(\inp', \oracle'; \kswit) \in \widetilde{\Relation'}$.
\end{itemize}
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Round-by-round knowledge soundness]
\label{def:rbr-knowledge-new}
A $k$-round IOR $\Pi$ has \emph{round-by-round knowledge soundness with relaxation} with errors $(\knowledgeerror_1,\ldots,\knowledgeerror_k)$ if there exists a knowledge state function $\kstate$ as in \cref{def:knowledge-state-function} and a deterministic extractor $\rbrextractor$ such that for every $(\inp,\oracle)$, every $j\in[k]$, and every transcript $\tr$ where the verifier is about to send $\verifiermsg_j$,
\[
  \Pr_{\verifiermsg_j}\!\left[
  \exists\,\kswit \ \text{such that} \ \begin{array}{l}
  \wedge\ \kstate\bigl(\inp,\oracle,\tr,\rbrextractor(\inp,\oracle,\tr\Vert\verifiermsg_j,\kswit)\bigr)=0\\
  \wedge\ \kstate(\inp,\oracle,\tr\Vert\verifiermsg_j,\kswit)=1
  \end{array}
 \right]
  \ \le\ \knowledgeerror_j(\inp,\oracle).
\]
If $(\widetilde{\Relation}, \widetilde{\Relation'})=(\Relation, \Relation')$ then we say the round-by-round knowledge soundness of $\Pi$ is \emph{without relaxation}.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Composition of IORs {\cite[Theorem 4.5]{zook}}]\label{r:ior_compositoin}
Let $\Pi_1$ be a perfectly complete $k_1$-round IOR from $\Relation_1$ to $\Relation_2$ with round-by-round knowledge soundness with relaxation $(\widetilde{\Relation_1},\widetilde{\Relation_2})$ and errors $\kappa_{11},\ldots,\kappa_{1k_1}$, and let $\Pi_2$ be a perfectly complete $k_2$-round IOR from $\Relation_2$ to $\Relation_3$ with round-by-round knowledge soundness with relaxation $(\widetilde{\Relation_2},\widetilde{\Relation_3})$ and errors $\kappa_{21},\ldots,\kappa_{2k_2}$. Then the IOR from $\Relation_1$ to $\Relation_3$ that runs $\Pi_1$ and then $\Pi_2$ on the output of $\Pi_1$ is perfectly complete, has $k_1+k_2$ rounds, and has round-by-round knowledge soundness with relaxation $(\widetilde{\Relation_1},\widetilde{\Relation_3})$ and errors $\kappa_{11},\ldots,\kappa_{1k_1},\kappa_{21},\ldots,\kappa_{2k_2}$.
\end{lemma}


\paragraph{Grinding}\albert{Review}
 Grinding \cite{?} is a technique where, given an IOP $\Pi$ for a relation $\Relation$ with relaxation $\widetilde{\Relation}$ with round-by-round knowledge errors $\kappa_1,\ldots, \kappa_k$,  produces a new IOP $\Pi'$ for $\Relation$ with   round-by-round knowledge with relaxation $\widetilde{\Relation}$ with errors $$2^{-\alpha_1}\cdot \kappa_1,\ldots, 2^{-\alpha_k}\cdot \kappa_k.$$ The new IOP $\Pi'$ works exactly as $\Pi$, except that, at each round $i\in [k]$, the verifier samples a random string of $\alpha_i$ bits, called \emph{grinding string}, which it sends alongside its message, and rejects unless all bits are $0$. 

 Clearly $\Pi'$ is not  necessarily complete, even if $\Pi$ is. The goal is to use the BCS compilation of IOPs, and not the IOPs themselves (i.e.\ the compilation where oracles are replaced by Merkle roots and where interactiveness is removed through the Fiat-Shamir transform). To this end, one chooses $\alpha_1, \ldots, \alpha_k$ small enough that the prover of the BCS transform of $\Pi'$ can \emph{grind} transcripts until all of the grinding strings in the transcript are the zero string. This results in the prover having to compute  per round $i\in [k]$, an average of $2^{\alpha_i}$  hashes of its $i$-th message followed by a small string of bits (in practice, one has the prover send a small string of bits next to its message, so that during grinding, it does not need to produce fresh Merkle roots at each grinding iteration). In \cref{tab:grinding-cost}, we provide example grinding times for different values of $\alpha_i$.
 
 The round-by-round knowledge errors of $\Pi'$ are obtained by defining a state function which is exactly as the state function of $\Pi$, except that the function outputs zero if some of the aforementioned strings of bits are not the zero string. The extractor for $\Pi'$ is defined as the extractor for $\Pi$, ignoring the additional strings of bits in the transcript.

\section{\ftwoz \ IOP for LinBitsRings}

In this section we describe the \ftwoz\ IOP and the main relations it proves.  We begin with a summary of the notation we use throughout the paper. Most of the notation will be introduced subsequently, but nevertheless we record it here for convenience of the reader.

\subsection{The core LinBitsRings relation}\label{s:linmodfieldbin}




\renewcommand{\ringtwo}{\FF_q}
\renewcommand{\ringmorphism}{\pi_q}
In this section we define the main linear relation we work with. We start by fixing notation:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
    \item Let $\comfield$ be a field with $2^{\comfieldexp}$ elements. Let
%
$
\beta_1, \ldots, \beta_\comfieldexp \in \comfield
$
%
be an $\FF_2$-basis of $\comfield$ seen as an $\FF_2$-vector space of dimension $\comfieldexp$. Given a vector $\uu\in \FF_2^\codedim$, with $\comfieldexp$ dividing $\codedim$, we define
%
$$
\pack(\uu)\in \comfield^{\codedim/\comfieldexp}
$$
as the vector whose $j$-th entry is
%
$$
\pack(\uu)_{j} = \uu_{(j-1)\cdot \comfieldexp +1 }\cdot \beta_1 + \ldots + \uu_{(j-1)\cdot \comfieldexp +\comfieldexp-1 }\cdot \beta_{\comfieldexp-1}+\uu_{j \cdot \comfieldexp} \cdot \beta_\comfieldexp \in \comfield.
$$
%
\item 
 Let $\codedim\geq 1$ be a dimension parameter divisible by $\comfieldexp$, $\cC$ a code over $\comfield$ of dimension $\codedim/\comfieldexp$ and blocklength $\codelength$.  
 \item  Let $R$ be a finitely generated ring (typically $R$ is $\ZZ$, a finite field, not necessarily a prime field, or a quotient of a polynomial ring $\ZZ[X]$ or $\FF_q[X]$) and let $\pi:\ZZ \to R$  be the  homomorphism mapping an integer $a$ to $a\cdot 1\in R$ (i.e.\ reduction modulo the characteristic of $R$).
\end{itemize}

 We begin by defining  a particular subclass of our target relation.

\begin{definition} The \emph{Core LinBitsRings} relation  is defined as
%
$$
\LinBitsDual(\bits,\pi, R,\cC) \defeq \left\{ \left(\begin{aligned} &\inp=(\mu, \vv)\in R \times R^{\codedim}, \\&\oracle = f \in \comfield^\codelength, \\ &\wit= \bff\in \{0,1\}^\codedim \subseteq \ZZ^\codedim\end{aligned}\right) \;\middle|\;  \begin{aligned} &f=\Enc_{\cC}\circ \pack \circ \picomfield(\bff) \\    & \langle \pi(\bff), \vv\rangle  = \mu \quad \text{in} R  \end{aligned} \right\}
$$
%
\end{definition}
%
%Again, when there is no risk of confusion, we write $\LinBitsDual(\bits,\ringmorphism, \moduletwo,\cC)$ simply as $\LinBitsDual$.
%
%
%
For $\delta\in (0,1)$, we let $\LinBitsDual^\delta$  be the relation $\LinBitsDual$  after replacing the constraint $f=\Enc_{\cC}\circ \pack\circ\pi_2(\bff)$ by
%
$$
\hamming(f,\Enc_{\cC}\circ \pack\circ\pi_2(\bff))<\delta.
$$
%
%
%
%
%

\renewcommand{\moduleone}{M}
\renewcommand{\moduleonebound}{M_{\leq B}}
\renewcommand{\moduletwo}{R}
 %For example, if $\ringtwo=\ZZ$, $\modulemorphism$ can be $\mathsf{id}$, and if $\ringtwo=\FF$, then $\modulemorphism$ can be $\pi_q$, i.e.\ reduction modulo $q$.


%When $Q$ and $\yy$ are such that $\yy=(\rr,\mu)\in \FF^{\log\codedim}\times \FF$ and $$Q(\yy,\ff)= \mle{\bff}(\rr)-\mu,$$ we denote $\Relation_{\cC, \FF}^{Q}$  by $\Relation_{\cC, \FF}^{\mathsf{mle}}$. 


%Next let $\comfield$ be a finite field of characteristic $2$,  and let $\moduletwo$ be either the field of rationals $\QQ$ or a finite field of characteristic $q$, possibly an extension field. For intuition, $\comfield$ is the field over which we will commit our witness, and $\moduletwo$ is the field over which we prove linear relations on the witness. If $\moduletwo=\QQ$ then we define $\degevalfield=0$, and if $\moduletwo$ is a finite field of characteristic $q$, then $\degevalfield$ denotes the degree extension of $\moduletwo$ over $\FF_q$.

%Let 
%
%$$
%\pievalfield: \ZZ[X] \to \moduletwo
%$$
%
%Rbe the natural projection of $\ZZ[X]$ onto $\moduletwo$. Concretely, if $\moduletwo=\QQ$,  then  $\pievalfield$ is reduction modulo the ideal $(X)$ (i.e.\ $\pievalfield(\sum_{i=0}^{k} a_i\cdot X^i)  = a_0$);  if $\moduletwo$ is a prime field $\FF_q$, then $\pievalfield$ is reduction modulo $q$ and modulo the ideal $(X)$; and if $\moduletwo$ is an extension field, so that $\moduletwo=\FF_q[X]/(h(X))$ for some irreducible polynomial $h(X)\in \FF_q[X]$, then $\pievalfield$ is reduction modulo $q$ and modulo the ideal $(h(X))$.




\renewcommand{\modulemorphism}{\psi}
\renewcommand{\moduletwo}{R}


\subsection{Tensor decomposition and products of constrained codes}\label{s:interleaved_setting}
%
\renewcommand{\ringmorphism}{\pi}
%\renewcommand{\ringtwo}{}

As discussed in \cref{s:to_simple_bitz} \albert{TO}, \ftwoz \ crucially demands and uses a tensor decomposition of $\vv$. We next establish notation and basic definitions around it.

 Let $(\inp,\oracle,\wit)=((\mu,\vv), f, \bff) \in \LinBitsDual(\bits,\ringmorphism, R,\cC)$. We fix a tensor decomposition of the vector $\vv\in \moduletwo^\codedim$ as $$\vv = \vv^{(1)} \otimes \vv^{(2)} = \left(\vv_i^{(1)}\cdot \vv_j^{(2)}\right)_{i\in [\codedim_1], j\in [\codedim_2]}$$ for some $\vv^{(1)}\in \moduletwo^{\codedim_1}, \vv^{(2)}\in \moduletwo^{\codedim_2}$, with $\codedim_1 \cdot \codedim_2=\codedim$. Note that the trivial tensor decomposition is admitted, i.e.\ we may effectively not decompose $\vv$ by choosing $\codedim_1=\codedim$, $\codedim_2=1$, $\vv^{(1)}=\vv$, and $\vv^{(2)} = (1)$.

%We assume $\cC = \cC_1^{\equiv \codedim_2}$ is the $\codedim_2$-dimensional interleaving of a code $\cC_1\subseteq \comfield^{\codelength/\codedim_2}$ of dimension $\codedim_1/2^\comfieldexp$ and blocklength $\codelength/\codedim_2$.

\renewcommand{\modulemorphism}{\ringmorphism}

We  index the entries of $\bff$ as $\bff=(\bff_{ij})_{i\in [\codedim_1], j\in[\codedim_2]}$. Note that
%
$$
\langle \modulemorphism(\bff), \vv \rangle = \sum_{j\in [\codedim_2]}\left(\sum_{i\in [\codedim_1]} \modulemorphism(\bff_{ij}) \cdot \vv_i^{(1)}\right) \cdot \vv_j^{(2)} = \sum_{j\in [\codedim_2]} \langle \modulemorphism(\bff_{*j}) , \vv^{(1)} \rangle \cdot \vv_j^{(2)},
$$
where $\bff_{*j} = (\bff_{ij})_{i\in[\codedim_1]}$. 
%
% We use $0$ and $1$ indistinctly to denote the zero and one integer, and zero and one field elements. 
%
Consider the relation  (cf.\ \cref{d:pesat}) $\PESAT(\comfield, Q_{\mathbb{B}\text{it}\ZZ},\cC, \pack)$ for $\ell_2$ constraint polynomials $Q_{\mathbb{B}\text{it}\ZZ}$ so that
%
\begin{align*}
&\PESAT(\comfield, Q_{\mathbb{B}\text{it}\ZZ},\cC, \pack)\\ &\qquad = \left\{ \left(\begin{aligned} &\inp=(\yy,\bm{\mu})\in \comfield^{\codedim_1}\times \comfield^{\codedim_2}, \\&\oracle = f\in \comfield^{\codelength}, \\ &\wit=\bff= (\bff_{*j})_{j\in [\codedim_2]}\in \FF_2^{\codedim_1 \times \codedim_2}\end{aligned}\right) \;\middle|\;  \begin{aligned} &f= \Enc_{\cC}\circ\pack(\bff) \\ \wedge \  &\prod_{i=1}^{\codedim_1} \left((\yy_i-1)\cdot \bff_{ij} +1\right) = \bm{\mu}_j  \text{ for all } j\in [\codedim_2]\end{aligned}  \right\}.
\end{align*}
%
%We omit an explicit description of the polynomials $Q_{\mathbb{B}\text{it}\ZZ}$, since the fact that the above relation is a particular instance of the PESAT relation is not relevant in this paper.
%
Here $Q_{\mathbb{B}\text{it}\ZZ}$ consists of the $\codedim_2$ polynomials $\prod_{i=1}^{\codedim_1}((\yy_i-1)\cdot \bff_{ij}+1)-\bm{\mu}_j$, $j\in[\codedim_2]$.


\begin{comment}
\begin{remark}[Using interleaved codes] \albert{What is this remark for?} \label{r:interleaved_codes} In practice, one may use as code $\cC$ an $\codedim_2$ interleaving $\cC_1^{\equiv \codedim_2}$ of a code $\cC_1$ of dimension $\codedim_1/\comfieldexp$. Then, if $\comfieldexp$ divides $\codedim_1$ \albert{add as requirement somewhere},  then $$\pack\circ \picomfield(\ff)=(\pack\circ \picomfield(\ff_{*j}))_{j\in [\codedim_2]}$$
%
and 
 $$f = \Enc_{\cC_1^{\equiv \codedim_2}}\circ\pack\circ \picomfield(\bff) =  (\Enc_{\cC_1}\circ \pack\circ \picomfield(\bff_{*j}))_{j\in [\codedim_2]}\in \comfield^{\codelength}.$$
%
 %\albert{If we do interleacing, the reason why we work with an interleaved $\PESAT(\comfield, Q_{\mathbb{B}\text{it}\ZZ},\cC_1^{\equiv \codedim_2}, \pack)$ rather than $\codedim_2$ copies of a non-interleaved PESAT is rather technical. It has to do with keeping the same distance parameter when doing the reduction from $\LinBitsDual$ to $\LIN$. If we simply considered separate encodings of $\pack(\bff_{*j})$, the proximity parameter in from $\LIN$ to $\LinBitsDual$ would grow by a factor of $\codedim_2$.}
%
\end{remark}
\end{comment}
   

%
We assume prover and verifier have access to an IOR $\Pi_{\mathsf{red}}$  that reduces 
%
$
\PESAT(\comfield, Q_{\mathbb{B}\text{it}\ZZ},\cC, \pack)$ to $\LIN(\comfield, \cC, \pack)$, where, recall, this latter relation has predicates of the form $\langle \bfg, \mathbf{z}\rangle = c$ with $\bfg\in \FF_2^{\codedim}$, $\mathbf{z}$ a vector over $\comfield$, $c\in \comfield$, and $g=\Enc_{\cC}\circ \pack(\bfg)$.   In our instantiation $\Pi_{\mathsf{red}}$ is an optimized GKR protocol for  grand products (cf.\ \cref{s:gkr_low_entropy}), which reduces the initial grand product claims to MLE evaluation claims of $\yy$ and $\bff$ over $\comfield$.

%
%
We assume $\Pi_{\mathsf{red}}$ works so that the verifier never queries $\oracle$; and that, given an input
%
$$\left(\inp=(\yy,\bm{\mu}),\  \oracle= f, \ \wit=\picomfield(\bff)\right)\in \PESAT(\comfield, Q_{\mathbb{B}\text{it}\ZZ},\cC, \pack),$$
%
$\Pi_{\mathsf{red}}$ outputs
%
$$
\left((\mathbf{z}, c), \ f, \ \picomfield(\bff)\right) \in \LIN( \comfield,\cC,\pack)$$ for some vector $\mathbf{z}\in \comfield^{\codedim}$ and field element $c\in \comfield$. In particular, $\Pi_{\red}$ outputs the same oracle $f$ and witness $\pi_2(\bff)$ as the oracle and witness it is given as inputs. 


%This is indeed the case with GKR: GKR reduces the initial circuit evaluation claim with a witness input, into an MLE evaluation claim of the same witness, without querying any oracle in-between. 




This is indeed the case with GKR: GKR reduces the initial circuit evaluation claim with a witness input, into an MLE evaluation claim of the same witness, without querying any oracle in-between. Consequently an IOR between the versions of these relations with oracle $\bot$ (such as \cref{c:gkr_grand_product}, cf.\ \cref{l:gkr_grand_product})  can be turned, in a straightforward manner, into an IOR from $\PESAT(\comfield, Q_{\mathbb{B}\text{it}\ZZ},\cC, \pack)$ to $\LIN(\comfield, \cC, \pack)$ with round-by-round knowledge soundness with relaxation $(\PESAT^\delta,\LIN^\delta)$ and the same errors, for every $\delta$. The input to the IOR now includes an oracle $f$, and this same oracle is output at the end of the IOR. The knowledge state function adds the condition $\hamming(f,\Enc_\cC\circ\pack(\ww))<\delta)$. 


%
%\john{I actually don't like this name. There's too many things in the protocol that is linear and it's too vague}





\subsection{The {\ftwoz} IOP for the core LinBitsRings relation}\label{s:core_iop}


\paragraph{Algebraic set-up}


 Recall (\cref{s:prelims_algebra}) that all rings in this work are finitely generated as rings. We fix generators $1, \eta_1, \ldots, \eta_{k}\in R$ \albert{I added a $1$ here (one can assume without loss that $1$ is part of the generating set). Let AI check that nothing breaks} and we let
%
$$
\picanon^{R} : \ZZ[X_1,\ldots, X_{k}] \to R
$$
be the surjective ring homomorphism mapping each integer $a$ to $a\cdot 1\in R$, and  $X_i$ to $\eta_{i}$ for $i\in [k]$. The choice of $\eta_1,\ldots, \eta_k$ is far from unique, see \cref{e:presentations} below. 

We also fix an inverse map  $\picanon^{R}{}^{-1}: R \to \ZZ[X_1, \ldots, X_{k}]$ for $\picanon^{R}$, so that $\picanon^{R}\circ \picanon^{R}{}^{-1}=\mathsf{id}$. We refer to $\picanon^{R}{}^{-1}(a)$ as the \emph{lift} of $a\in R$. Note that, given $\picanon^R$, there may be many possible choices for $\picanon^{R}{}^{-1}$. In  \cref{e:presentations}  we provide example inverse maps.  

Recall (see \cref{s:prelims_algebra}) that $\pi_n$ denotes reduction modulo $n$ and that $\pi_n^{-1}$ maps each element from $\ZZ_n$ to its integer representative in $\range{0}{n-1}$.



%We also fix an inverse map $\picanon^{R}{}^{-1}: R \to \ZZ[X_1, \ldots, X_{\dim(R)-1}]$ of $\picanon^{R}$, defined as follows. Given $a\in R$, write $a=\sum_{i\in [\dim(R)]} a_i \cdot \gamma_i$ with $a_i\in \ZZ_{\Char(R)}$ (cf.\ \cref{s:prelims_algebra}), then $\picanon^{R}{}^{-1}(a) = \pi_{\Char}^{-1}(a_1) + \sum_{i=2}^{\dim(R)} \pi_{\Char}^{-1}(a_i)\cdot X_{i-1}$.


\begin{example}\label{e:presentations}
We provide some examples of rings $R$ and choices for  projections $\picanon^R$ and their inverses: 
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*,nosep]
    \item \textbf{Prime fields.} If  $R=\FF_q$ is a prime field with $q$ elements, then we can take $k=0$, so that $\picanon^{\FF_q}=\pi_q:\ZZ \to \FF_q$ is simply reduction modulo $q$, with its inverse being $\pi_q^{-1}$ (see \cref{s:prelims_algebra}). 
    \item \textbf{Integers.} Similarly, for $R=\ZZ$ we can take $k=0$, and then $\picanon^{\ZZ}$ and its lifts are the identity map on $\ZZ$.
\item \textbf{Field extensions.} If $R$ is a field extension of a prime field $\FF_q$ of degree $d>1$, then we can  take $k=1$ and $\eta_1 = x$, where $x$ is a root of an irreducible polynomial $h(X_1)\in \FF_{q}[X_1]$ of degree $d$ (so that $R=\FF_q(x)$). Then $\picanon^R:\ZZ[X_1]\to R$ consists in reducing coefficients modulo $q$, and then sending $X_1$ to $x$. Writing $a\in R$ as $a=\sum_{i=0}^{d-1} a_i\cdot x^{i}$ with $a_i\in \FF_q$, one can then define a natural lift of $a$ as $\sum_{i=0}^{d-1} \pi_q^{-1}(a_i)\cdot X_1^{i}$.
\item \textbf{Univariate integer polynomials.} If $R=\ZZ[X]$, then we can take $k=1$ and $\eta_1=X$, and then $\picanon^{\ZZ[X]}:\ZZ[X_1]\to \ZZ[X]$ is an isomorphism, with lifts given by its inverse.
\item \textbf{Lattices, Galois rings, etc.} More generally, if $R$ is finitely generated as a $\ZZ_{\Char(R)}$-module and it is free as a $\ZZ_{\Char(R)}$-module, with  $\ZZ_{\Char(R)}$-module-basis $\beta_1=1, \beta_2, \ldots, \beta_m$ (a condition satisfied by, e.g., all finite fields, $\ZZ$, and $\ZZ_n[X]/(h(X))$ for any monic $h(X)$ and any integer $n\geq 0$), then we may take $k=m-1$ and $\eta_i=\beta_{i+1}$ for $i\in [k]$. Then given $a\in R$ and writing $a=\sum_{i\in [m]} a_i\cdot \beta_i$ with $a_i\in \ZZ_{\Char(R)}$, we can define the lift of $a$ as $\pi_{\Char}^{-1}(a_1) + \sum_{i=2}^{m}\pi_{\Char}^{-1}(a_i)\cdot X_{i-1}$.
\end{itemize}
\end{example}


We assume without further comment that it is efficient to compute  values $\picanon^R(h)$  for $h\in \ZZ[X_1,\ldots, X_k]$, and to check whether  $\picanon^R(h)=0$.   This is the case in all examples above.


\paragraph{The \ftwoz\ IOP.} We provide an IOP for $\LinBitsDual$ next. We assume the notation from the previous two \cref{s:linmodfieldbin,s:interleaved_setting}, and from the preliminary \cref{s:prelims_algebra}, together with:
\renewcommand{\evalfield}{R}
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
%\item Given a finite field $\KK$ which is an extension of a prime field $\FF_p$ of degree $\degevalfield$, we let $\picanon^\KK: \ZZ[X] \to \KK$ be the canonical homomorphism from $\ZZ[X]$ to $\KK$: namely, if $\KK=\ZZ[X]/(p,h(X))$  for a polynomial $h(X)$ irreducible over $\FF_p[X]$, then $\picanon^\KK$ consists in modding $p$ out, and taking the quotient by $(h(X))$ (if $\KK$ is the prime field $\FF_p$, then $\picanon^\KK$ is just $\pi_p$). We fix an inverse for $\picanon^{\KK}$ of the form $$\picanon^{\KK}{}^{-1}: \KK \to \range{0}{p-1}^{<\degevalfield}[X] \subseteq \ZZ[X].
%$$ In words, $\picanon^{\KK}{}^{-1}$ simply takes an element from $\KK$ represented as a degree $< \deg(h(X))$ polynomial over $\FF_p$, and applies $\pi_{p}^{-1}$ to it. We remark that one could choose any other such ``inverse'' maps, e.g.\ for $\pi_{p}^{-1}$ one may prefer to use centered representatives. 





%\item Define $\picanon^\ZZ: \ZZ\to\ZZ$ as the identity map.  We define $\deg(\ZZ)=1$.



\item Let $\Pi_{\iopp}$ be an IOP for $\LIN(\comfield,\cC, \pack)$. In our instantiation, $\Pi_{\iopp}$ is the WHIR/Ligerito protocol \cite{whir,ligerito} over binary fields as implemented in Flock \cite{flock}, with ring-switching \cite{binius} and a preliminary sumcheck over $\comfield$ that transforms arbitrary inner products to MLE evaluation claims (since the ring switch of \cite{flock} does not support arbitrary inner product claims). We set the proximity parameter  to the Johnson bound when targeting $100$ bits of security, and to the unique decoding radius for $128$ bits of security, see \cref{s:instantiation} for more details.   
\item 
Let $\codedim =\codedim_1\cdot \codedim_2$ be dimension parameters,  and $R$ a ring with generators $1,\eta_1,\ldots, \eta_k$,  as in the previous sections. We let $\cP$ be a set of primes with $\max\cP< \fieldboundsizeexponent$. Given the tensor decomposition $\vv=\vv^{(1)}\otimes \vv^{(2)}$ from \cref{s:interleaved_setting}, we define \albert{Why a subscript $R$ in $N_R$ and not in the others?}
\begin{equation}\label{e:ring_theta_params}
\begin{aligned}
\uu &\defeq (\vv^{(1)},\vv^{(2)})\in R^{\codedim_1+ \codedim_2}\\
D &\defeq \max_{i\in [|\uu|]} \deg\left(\picanon^{R}{}^{-1}(\uu_i)\right), \qquad\qquad N_R \defeq \max_{i\in [|\uu|]}\ \left\lVert \picanon^{R}{}^{-1}(\uu_i)\right\rVert_\infty,
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
%
where $\deg$ denotes total degree, and $\lVert \cdot \rVert_\infty$ denotes the maximum absolute value of the coefficients. We also define a quantity  $T$  as follows. $T$ is the maximum, over monomials $m$ in variables $X_1,\ldots, X_k$, of the number of pairs $(m_1, m_2)$ of monomials of total degree at most $D$ each such that $m_1\cdot m_2 = m$. 

For example, $T\leq D+1$ if $k=1$, and $T\leq 2$ if $D\leq 1$. In general we have $T\leq (D+1)^{k}$.  %In practice, for us, the bound $T\leq (D+1)^{k}$ is good enough.
%
%$$
%\Char^*(R) \defeq \begin{cases} \Char(R)-1 & \text{if } \Char(R)>0,\\ \max\left(\lVert \vv^{(1)}\rVert_\infty,\ \lVert \vv^{(2)}\rVert_\infty\right) & \text{if } R=\ZZ. \end{cases}
%$$

\item For every prime $q\in \cP$, let $\cM_q$ be a set of ring homomorphisms $$\pirand:\ZZ[X_1,\ldots, X_{k}] \to \FF_q,$$ and let $\cM\defeq (\cM_q)_{q\in \cP}$. The verifier will sample primes and morphisms from $\cP$ and $\cM_q$. We let $\theta(\cP,\cM,\codedim)$ be the maximum probability that $\pirand(h)=0$, over random $q\gets \cP$, $\pirand\gets \cM_q$, and where the maximum is taken over nonzero polynomials $h\in \ZZ[X_1, \ldots, X_{k}]$ of total degree at most $2\cdot D$, with $\lVert h\rVert_{\infty} \leq 2\cdot T\cdot \codedim\cdot N_R^2$, and such that $\picanon^{R}(h)\neq 0$. Precisely:
%
$$
\theta(\cP,\cM,\codedim) \defeq  \max_{h}\left\{\Pr_{q\gets \cP, \pirand\gets \cM_q}[ \pirand(h)=0] \right\},
$$
%
where $h$ ranges over the polynomials just described. See \cref{l:examples_morphisms} for an example. The quantities $D$, $N_R$, $T$ and  $\theta(\cP,\cM,\codedim)$ depend on $R$ and on the instance vector $\vv$. We omit this dependence from the notation.
\end{itemize}


\begin{lemma}[Example definition of  $\cM_q$ and the corresponding $\theta(\cP,\cM,\codedim)$]\label{l:examples_morphisms}
    Let $\cP$ be a set of primes. For each $q\in \cP$ let $\cM_q$  be the set of all maps $\pi: \ZZ[X_1,\ldots, X_{k}]\to \FF_q$ of the form
     $$\pi(h(X_1, \ldots, X_{k})) = \pi_q(h)(\alpha_1, \ldots, \alpha_{k}), \qquad \text{for } h\in \ZZ[X_1,\ldots, X_{k}],$$
%
for some $\alpha_1,\ldots, \alpha_{k}\in \FF_q$, and where $\pi_q(h)$ is $h$ after reducing its coefficients modulo $q$. Then
         $$
    \theta(\cP,\cM,\codedim) \leq \frac{2\cdot D}{q_{\min}} + \frac{\log\left(2\cdot T\cdot \codedim\cdot N_R^2\right)}{|\cP|\cdot \lfloor\log(q_{\min})\rfloor}.
    $$
    where $q_{\min}$ is the smallest prime in $\cP$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    We defer the proof to later in \cref{s:soundness_core_iop}.
\end{proof}


\renewcommand{\degevalfield}{\deg(\evalfield)}
%\renewcommand{\picanon}{{\pi_{\mathsf{canon}}^R}}


%\albertimportant{This construction is missing OOD sample when we work beyond the UDR. Formalizing OOD sampling is a bit of a pain: we need to define a relation $\LinBitsDual^{\rr,y}$ which is the same as $\LinBitsDual$ but adds the constraint $\mle{\pack\circ\pi_2(\mathsf{\bits}(\bff))}(\rr) =y$ in $\comfield$. We do the same for $\UPESAT$. Then we present \cref{c:core_iop}  as a protocol for $\LinBitsDual^{\rr,y}$, and in \cref{c:LinModFieldBin} present an IOP for the general $\LinBitsDual$ relation where we first do an OOD sample, reducing to $\LinBitsDual^{\rr,y}$. In \cref{c:iop_pimsat} we also add an initial reduction from $\UPESAT$ to $\UPESAT^{\rr, y}$. All this is standard, so I am considering to write a short  section  glossing this over. The question is how to state the formal soundness results}


\begin{construction}[IOP for the core {$\LinBitsDual$ relation}]\label{c:core_iop}
\noindent \textbf{Parameters.} $(\codedim, \codedim_1,\codedim_2, \Pi_\iopp, \Pi_\red, \cP, (\cM_q)_{q\in \cP})$ comprised of: a witness vector length $\codedim$; tensor decomposition dimensions $\codedim_1, \codedim_2$  satisfying $\codedim_1\cdot \codedim_2 = \codedim$; the IOP $\Pi_\iopp$ described above, the IOR $\Pi_\red$ from \cref{s:interleaved_setting}; and the sets $\cP, \cM_q$ from above. 
\\

\noindent \textbf{Inputs.} The prover and verifier receive $\inp=(\mu, \vv)\in \evalfield \times \evalfield^{\codedim}$ and  $\oracle= f \in \comfield^\codelength$. The prover receives $\wit= \bff\in \bits^\codedim \subseteq \ZZ^\codedim$ such that $(\inp,\oracle, \wit) \in \LinBitsDual(\bits,\ringmorphism, \evalfield,\cC)$.  We assume the tensor split from the previous \cref{s:interleaved_setting}, and adopt its notation. 


Let $\basegenerator\in \comfield$ be a fixed generator of the multiplicative group $\comfield^*$. Denote $\picanon^R$ simply as $\picanon$.  %Let $q\in \ZZ$ denote the characteristic of $R$ if $R$ has nonzero characteristic, or if $R$ has zero characteristic, then \albertimportant{Review that if $\vv$ has negative entries, things are fine}
%\begin{equation}\label{e:q_definition_if_R_is_Z}
%q = \codedim_1 \cdot \lVert \vv \rVert_\infty +1.
%\end{equation}



\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*] 
\item \textbf{Round 0 (optional): Out Of Domain (OOD) sample.}  This step can be skipped if the proximity parameter $\delta$ is  set within the unique decoding radius of $\cC$ (cf.\ \cref{s:prelims_codes}, and \cref{t:thm_core_IOPP}). Otherwise the step is executed. In that case, the verifier samples and sends a random $\zeta\in \comfield$, and the prover responds with $$y=\mle{\pack\circ\pi_2(\bff)}(\bm{\zeta}), \qquad \text{where } \bm{\zeta}\defeq \left(\zeta^{2^0}, \zeta^{2^1}, \ldots, \zeta^{2^{\log (\codedim/\comfieldexp) -1}}\right).$$
\item \textbf{Round 1 (optional): replacing $R$ by a ``small'' prime field $\FF_q$.}
This step can be skipped (i.e.\ prover and verifier move to Round 2 directly) if $R$ is a prime field $\FF_p$ and $p < \fieldboundsizeexponent$; in that case we set $q\defeq p$ in Round 2 and Phase 3. Otherwise the step is executed. %, or if $R=\ZZ$ and $\lVert \vv \rVert < \fieldboundsizeexponent$.

The prover computes and sends $\mu' \in \ZZ[X_1, \ldots, X_{k}]$ such that
%
$$
\langle \picanon^{-1}(\vv^{(1)}) \otimes \picanon^{-1}(\vv^{(2)}) , \bff \rangle = \mu' \qquad \text{in } \ZZ[X_1, \ldots, X_{k}].
$$
%
The verifier checks that $\mu'$ has total degree at most $2\cdot D$ and $\lVert \mu'\rVert_\infty \leq T\cdot \codedim\cdot N_R^2$, then it checks that $\picanon(\mu')=\mu$, and replies with a random prime $q\in \cP$ and a random morphism
%
$
\pirand : \ZZ[X_1, \ldots, X_{k}] \to \FF_q
$ from $\cM_q$.
 The prover and verifier update:
\begin{align*}
\vv^{(1)}{} &\gets \pirand\circ \picanon^{-1}\left(\vv^{(1)}\right)\in \FF_{q}^{\codedim_1},\\
\vv^{(2)}{} &\gets \pirand\circ \picanon^{-1}\left(\vv^{(2)}\right)\in \FF_{q}^{\codedim_2},\\
\mu&\gets \pirand(\mu')\in \FF_q,
\end{align*}
The prover and verifier now set to prove the updated claim 
%
\begin{equation}\label{e:core_bitz_eq_new_0}
\langle \vv^{(1)} \otimes \vv^{(2)}, \pi_q(\bff)\rangle = \mu \qquad \text{over } \FF_q.
\end{equation}

\begin{remark}[Skipping Round 1 when $R=\ZZ$ and $\lVert \vv\rVert_{\infty}$ is ``small'']\label{r:skip_round_1_integers}
\albert{Make sure our completeness and soundness theorem proves this case as well}
    This step can also be skipped when $R=\ZZ$ and all entries of $\vv^{(1)}$ are nonnegative and less than $\fieldboundsizeexponent$. In that case the claim to prove at the start of Round 2 is $\langle \vv^{(1)} \otimes \vv^{(2)}, \bff\rangle = \mu $ over $\ZZ$. In this case the map $\pi_q$ in Round 2 is simply replaced by the identity map, $\FF_q$ is replaced by $\ZZ$, and $q$ in the range checks of Round 2 denotes $\max_{i\in [\codedim_1]} \vv^{(1)}_i+1$. In Phase 3, $\bm{\gamma}$ is  $\vv^{(1)}$. 
\end{remark}
\item \textbf{Round 2: transforming \eqref{e:core_bitz_eq_new_0} to $\codedim_2$ integer constraints with bounded integers.}
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
    \item The prover computes and sends integers $\mu_1, \ldots, \mu_{\codedim_2}\in \range{0}{(q-1)\cdot \codedim_1}$ such that, over $\ZZ$, \begin{equation}\label{e:tensored_equality}\langle \bff_{*j}, \pi_q^{-1}(\vv^{(1)})\rangle = \mu_j \qquad \text{for all } j\in [\codedim_2].\end{equation} 
\item The verifier checks that $\mu_j\in \range{0}{(q-1)\cdot \codedim_1}$ for all $j\in [\codedim_2]$, and that \begin{equation}\label{e:tensor_decomposition_reconstruction}\sum_{j\in [\codedim_2]} \pi_q(\mu_j)\cdot \vv_j^{(2)}  = \mu\qquad \text{over } \FF_q.\end{equation} 
Next, prover and verifier will focus on certifying that \eqref{e:tensored_equality} holds for all $j\in [\codedim_2]$.

%In \cref{r:core_iop_optional_sumcheck} we describe a variation in which, instead of sending the elements $\mu_1, \ldots, \mu_{\codedim_2}$, the prover commits to their bit-decomposition over $\comfield$, and proves \eqref{e:tensor_decomposition_reconstruction} with \cref{c:LinModFieldBin}. This saves on proof size and verifier time when $\codedim$ is large.

%    \item $\degevalfield=1$ and $q\geq (|\comfield|-1)/(\codedim_1+1)$.  \albert{Review if I need the $+1$ in the denominator}
%\end{itemize} The verifier uniformly samples and sends a  prime $q'$ from $\cP$, and, if $\degevalfield>1$,  an element $\alpha\in \FF_{q'}$. Now update
%
%\begin{align*}
%\vv^{(1)}{} &\gets \pi_{q',\alpha}\circ \picanon^{-1}\left(\vv^{(1)}\right)\in \FF_{q'}^{\codedim_1},\\
%\mu_j &\gets \pi_{q'}^{-1}\circ \pi_{q',\alpha}(\mu_j) \in \range{0}{q'-1}\subseteq \ZZ, \qquad j\in [\codedim_2],\\
%q&\gets q',
%\end{align*}
%where, recall, $\pi_{q',\alpha}:\ZZ[X]\to \FF_{q'}$ is defined as coefficient-wise reduction modulo $q'$ and then evaluation of $X$ at $\alpha$.
%\end{itemize}
%
%\begin{remark}
%[Using any module homomorphism instead of $\pi_{q',\alpha}$]\label{r:otheSodule_morphisms}
%Instead of sampling $\pi_{q',\alpha}$, the verifier could sample other $\ZZ$-module homomorphisms from $\ZZ^{<\mathsf{deg}(R)}[X]$ to a prime field. For example it could sample a prime $q'$ and $\mathsf{deg}(R)$ random elements $\alpha_1,\ldots, \alpha_{\mathsf{deg}(R)}\in \FF_{q'}$, and then prover and verifier use the map $\pi_{q',\alpha_1,\ldots, \alpha_{\mathsf{deg}(R)}}(f(X)) = \sum_{i\in [\mathsf{deg}(R)]}\pi_{q'}(f_{i}) \cdot \alpha_i$, where $f(X)=\sum_{i\in [\mathsf{deg}(R)]} f_{i}\cdot X^{i-1}$. 
%$$

%
%For ease of readability onwards, we use the same symbols $\vv^{(1)}$ and $q$ for the pre- and post-update vectors and primes in this step.
\end{itemize}
\item \textbf{Phase 3: Transforming the claim into a PESAT claim over {$\comfield$}, and invoking the IOR {$\Pi_{\mathsf{red}}$}.}
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
\item 
Prover and verifier compute and define $$\bm{\gamma} = \pi_{q}^{-1}(\vv^{(1)}) \in \range{0}{q-1}^{\codedim_1}\subseteq \ZZ^{\codedim_1},$$
%
and set to prove the following claim:
%
$$
\prod_{i \in [\codedim_1]} g^{\bm{\gamma}_i\cdot \bff_{ij}}  = g^{\mu_j} \in \comfield, \qquad \text{for all } j\in [\codedim_2].
$$
This claim is equivalent to
$$
\prod_{i \in [\codedim_1]} \left(\left(g^{\bm{\gamma}_i}-1\right)\cdot \pi_2(\bff_{ij}) +1\right)= g^{\mu_j}  \quad  \in \comfield, \qquad \text{for all } j\in [\codedim_2].
$$
Notice that \begin{equation}\label{e:Q_F2Z_intermediate}\left(\inp=((g^{\bm{\gamma}_i})_{i \in [\codedim_1]},\ (g^{\mu_j})_{j\in [\codedim_2]}),\  \oracle= f, \ \wit=\picomfield(\bff)\right)\in \PESAT(\comfield, Q_{\mathbb{B}\text{it}\ZZ},\cC, \pack).
\end{equation}
Let  $\bm{\mu}\defeq (\mu_1,\ldots, \mu_{\codedim_2})$, $g^{\bm{\gamma}}\defeq (g^{\bm{\gamma}_i})_{i \in [\codedim_1]}$ and $g^{\bm{\mu}}\defeq (g^{\mu_j})_{j\in [\codedim_2]}$.
% \ulrich{%
% Suggestion: 
% Maybe it is more clear, if we write in the above two equalities explicitly a $\bmod\: q$, or, add $\in \mathbb F_q$?
% }%
% \albert{See now. It's actually in $\comfield$}
\item To prove \eqref{e:Q_F2Z_intermediate}, prover and verifier engage in the IOR $\Pi_{\mathsf{red}}$\footnote{Recall that, in our instantiation, $\Pi_{\mathsf{red}}$ is the GKR protocol applied to multiple grand products, cf.\ \cref{s:gkr_low_entropy}.}   with input $$\left(\inp=(g^{\bm{\gamma}}, g^{\bm{\mu}}),\  \oracle= f, \ \wit=\picomfield(\bff)\right).$$
%
%
 At the end of this IOR, they are left with the following claim to prove: 
%
\begin{equation}\label{e:iopp_mle_claims}
\left((\mathbf{z}, c), \ f, \ \picomfield(\bff)\right) \in \LIN( \comfield,\cC,\pack)= \left\{ \left(\begin{aligned} &\inp=(\uu,\mu)\in \comfield^{\codedim}\times \comfield, \\&\oracle = g \in \comfield^{\codelength}, \\ &\wit= \bfg\in \FF_2^\codedim\end{aligned}\right) \;\middle|\;  \begin{aligned} 
&g=\Enc_{\cC}\circ \pack (\bfg) \\    &\langle \uu, \bfg\rangle = \mu  \end{aligned}\right\}
\end{equation} for some vector $\mathbf{z}\in \comfield^{\codedim}$ and field element $c\in \comfield$ output during the IOR $\Pi_{\mathsf{red}}$. 
\end{itemize}
\item \textbf{Phase 4: Using an IOP over {$\comfield$}.} Prover and  verifier engage in the IOP $\Pi_{\iopp}$  to certify the claim \eqref{e:iopp_mle_claims}.  If Round 0 (Out Of Domain sampling) triggered, they also use $\Pi_{\iopp}$ to certify the evaluation claim from Round 0.
\end{enumerate}
\end{construction}


In \cref{t:core_iop_complexity} we summarize the main costs of \cref{c:core_iop}.


\paragraph{Costs ($k\leq 1$).}  In \Cref{t:core_iop_complexity} we summarize the costs of \cref{c:core_iop} beyond those of $\Pi_\red$ and $\Pi_\iopp$ (cf.\ \cref{s:gkr_low_entropy,s:instantiation}), for the specific case $k\leq 1$, corresponding to $R$ being of the form $\ZZ_n$ or $\ZZ_n[X]/(h(X))$ for some $n\geq 0$ and some $h(X)$. This is the setting used in our instantiation \cref{s:instantiation,s:experiments} \albert{Do we do any Babybear extension? If not, we only use $k=0$}. The number of rounds is  between $t_\red +t_\iopp$ and $2+t_\red +t_\iopp$ , depending on whether Rounds 0 and 1 execute or not, where $t_\red, t_\iopp$ denote the number of rounds of $\Pi_\iopp,\Pi_\red$.

Rounds 0, 1 and 2 send only non-oracle messages, so the oracles and queries of \cref{c:core_iop} are exclusively those of $\Pi_\red$ and $\Pi_\iopp$. If Round 1 is skipped, $q$ denotes $|R|$ when $R=\FF_q$, and $\max_i \vv^{(1)}_i+1$ when $R=\ZZ$ (cf.\ \cref{r:skip_round_1_integers}). The exponentiations of Phase 3, when computing $(g^{\bm{\gamma_i}})_{i\in [\codedim_1]}$ and $(g^{\mu_j})_{j\in [\codedim_2]}$, take $\lceil \log(q\cdot\codedim_1)/w\rceil \leq \comfieldexp/w$ multiplications each using a fixed-base table with $w$-bit windows ($w=8$ in our implementation).


In our instantiation, we set $\codedim_1\approx \sqrt{\codedim}$, $\codedim_2\approx \sqrt{\codedim}$, making the verifier succinct.  We assume implicitly that the instance is given to the verifier as $(\mu,\vv^{(1)},\vv^{(2)})$, so that it does not read $\vv$ in full.

When $k=0$ (so that $R=\ZZ_n$ for some $n$), we have $D=0$ and $T=1$.  When $k=1$ and $R=\ZZ_n[X]/(h(X))$ for some monic $h(X)$, then $D\leq \deg(h(X))-1$ and $T\leq D
+1$, cf.\ \eqref{e:ring_theta_params} for a definition of these quantities. If $n>0$, then $N_R\leq |\ZZ_n|=n$. Otherwise if $n=0$, $N_R \leq \lVert (\vv^{(1)}, \vv^{(2)})\rVert_\infty$.



\begin{table}[H]
  \centering
  \begin{tabular}{l|l|l}
    \toprule
    Round 0 & Prover & $O(\codedim/\comfieldexp)\cdot \comfield$ \\
     & Verifier & -- \\
     & Proof (bits) & $\comfieldexp$ \\
    \hline
    Round 1 & Prover & $O(\codedim\cdot (D+1))$ additions of $\log(\codedim_1 \cdot N_R)$-bit integers \\
     & & $O(\codedim_2\cdot (D+1)^2)$ additions of $\log(\codedim \cdot N_R^2)$-bit integers \\
     & & $O(\codedim_2\cdot (D+1)^2)$ multiplications of $\log(\codedim\cdot N_R^2)$-bit integers \\
     & & $O((\codedim_1+\codedim_2)\cdot (D+1))$ reductions mod $q$ of $\log(N_R)$-bit integers \\
     & & $O((\codedim_1+\codedim_2)\cdot (D+1))\cdot \FF_q$ \\
     & Verifier & $O(D+1)$ reductions mod $\Char(R)$ of $\log((D+1)\cdot \codedim\cdot N_R^2)$-bit integers \\
     &  & $O(D+1)\cdot R$ \\
     & & $O((\codedim_1+\codedim_2)\cdot (D+1))$ reductions mod $q$ of $\log(N_R)$-bit integers \\
     & & $O((\codedim_1+\codedim_2)\cdot (D+1))\cdot \FF_q$ \\
     & Proof (bits) & $(2 D + 1)\cdot \log((D+1)\cdot \codedim\cdot N_R^2)$ \\
    \hline
    Round 2 & Prover & $O(\codedim)$ additions of $\log(q\cdot \codedim_1)$-bit integers \\
     & Verifier & $O(\codedim_2)$ reductions mod $q$ of $\log(q\cdot \codedim_1)$-bit integers \\
     & & $O(\codedim_2)\cdot \FF_q$ \\
     & Proof (bits) & $\codedim_2\cdot \log(q\cdot \codedim_1)$ \\
    \hline
    Phase 3 & Prover & $O((\codedim_1+\codedim_2)\cdot \comfieldexp/w)\cdot \comfield$ \\
     & Verifier & same as prover \\
     & Proof (bits) & -- \\
    \bottomrule
  \end{tabular}
  \caption{Costs of \cref{c:core_iop} when $k\leq 1$, in addition to those of $\Pi_\red$ and $\Pi_\iopp$. By $N\cdot \comfield$, $N\cdot \FF_q$, $N\cdot R$ we indicate $N$ operations in the respective ring. Rounds 0 and 1 are optional.}
  \label{t:core_iop_complexity}
\end{table}

\albert{Was AI generated and reviewed and edited by me after} We justify   the costs listed in Round 1 of \cref{t:core_iop_complexity}, which are arguably the most involved to derive. The first three Prover costs in Round 1 come from the computation of  $\mu'$. The prover can compute $\mu'$ by first building, for each $j\in [\codedim_2]$, the polynomial $h_j\defeq \sum_{i\in [\codedim_1]} \bff_{ij}\cdot \picanon^{-1}(\vv^{(1)}_i)$, which requires no multiplications since $\bff$ has bit entries. The coefficients of these polynomials are bounded by $\codedim_1\cdot N_R$. Hence the $h_j$ can be computed with $O(\codedim\cdot (D+1))$ additions of $\log(\codedim_1\cdot N_R)$-bit integers. Now, to obtain $\mu'$ the prover computes    $\sum_{j\in [\codedim_2]}\picanon^{-1}(\vv^{(2)}_j)\cdot h_j$. Each product in this sums costs at most $(D+1)^{2}$ multiplications of $\log(\codedim_1\cdot N_R)$-bit integers, and there are $\codedim_2$ of them, this yields the third cost in the prover's Round 1. The second cost correspond to adding together each of the terms in  $\sum_{j\in [\codedim_2]}\picanon^{-1}(\vv^{(2)}_j)\cdot h_j$.

The last two cost of the prover and verifier in Round 1  are the costs of computing $\pirand\circ \picanon^{-1}(\vv^{(i)})$ for $i=1,2$.  For both $i$, this is the cost of reducing $D+1$ integers in the range $\range{0}{N_R-1}$ modulo a  prime, and then computing a linear combination of the resulting elements. 




 The first two verifier costs in Round 1 correspond to computing $\picanon(\mu')$. For that, it first reduces the at most $2D+1$ coefficients of $\mu'$ (which are integers of at most $\log(T\cdot \codedim\cdot N_R^2)$ bits) modulo $\Char(R)$ and evaluates the resulting polynomial at $\eta_1$ in $R$ using Horner's rule, and then compares the result with $\mu$.

\albertimportant{How does the prover get the $g^{\mu_j}$ afterward though? Prover must prove an MLE evaluation of $(g^{\mu_j})_j$ } When $\codedim=\codedim_1\cdot \codedim_2$ is such that $\codedim_2$ is large, the elements $\mu_1,\ldots, \mu_{\codedim_2}\in \ZZ$ sent at Round 2 of \cref{c:core_iop} may start to dominate the proof size. For example, for $\codedim_2=2^{13}$ and $\mu_i$ of $128$ bits, the elements $\mu_1,\ldots, \mu_{\codedim_2}$ amount to $131$ KB.  To mitigate this cost,  one can have the prover commit to the vector $(\mu_1,\ldots, \mu_{\codedim_2})$ and then prove \eqref{e:tensor_decomposition_reconstruction}, i.e.\ 
%
$$
\langle \bff_{*j}, \pi_q^{-1}(\vv^{(1)})\rangle = \mu_j \qquad \text{for all } j\in [\codedim_2].
$$

using an IOP for constrained codes. Concretely, the prover commits to the vector $\bitstext(\mu)$ of bits forming the elements $(\mu_1, \ldots, \mu_{\codedim_2})$ using \ftwoz, and then proves \eqref{e:tensor_decomposition_reconstruction} using \cref{c:LinModFieldBin} from \cref{s: general_bitz}.   %At the end of \cref{c:core_iop}, the prover is left with an inner product claim for the vector $\pi_2(\bitstext(\mu))$.
