\begin{remark}[Succinct verifier and proofs]\label{r:succinct_verifier}
    By \cref{t:core_iop_complexity}, choosing $\codedim_1 =O(\sqrt{\codedim})$ and $\codedim_2 =O(\sqrt{\codedim})$, the verifier and the proof size of \cref{c:core_iop} have asymptotic cost $O(\sqrt{\codedim})$ (where $\codedim$ is the bit-length of the witness). %By using the approach \albertimportant{finish}
\end{remark}



\subsection{Soundness and completeness}\label{s:soundness_core_iop}


For readability, we temporarily denote $$\PESAT=\PESAT(\comfield, Q_{\mathbb{B}\text{it}\ZZ},\cC, \pack),\qquad \LIN = \LIN(\comfield, \cC, \pack).$$




\begin{theorem}[Completeness and round-by-round knowledge soundness of \cref{c:core_iop}]\label{t:thm_core_IOPP} 
   Let $\delta \in (0,1)$ be a proximity parameter within the unique decoding radius of $\cC$ (cf.\ \cref{s:prelims_codes}), and assume that Round 0 of \cref{c:core_iop} is skipped. We treat the general case in \cref{a:OOD}. Assume 
   %
   $$\max\cP < \fieldboundsizeexponent.$$ 
   %
   Assume $\Pi_{\mathsf{red}}$ is a perfectly complete $t_\red$-round IOR from $\PESAT$ to $\LIN$ with round-by-round knowledge soundness  with relaxation $(\PESAT^\delta,\LIN^{\delta})$, with errors  $(\ksred_1, \ldots, \ksred_{t_\red})$. Assume $\Pi_{\iopp}$ is a perfectly complete $t_{\iopp}$-round IOP  for $\LIN$ (i.e.\ an IOR from $\LIN$ to the trivial relation $\bot$) with round-by-round knowledge soundness with relaxation $(\LIN^{\delta}, \bot)$, and errors $$\ksiopp_1,\ldots, \ksiopp_{t_\iopp}.$$ 
%
    Then \cref{c:core_iop} is a perfectly complete $(1+t_\red+t_\iopp)$-round IOP for $\LinBitsDual$ with round-by-round knowledge soundness with relaxation $(\LinBitsDual^{\delta}, \bot)$, with errors $$ \kappa_0, (\ksred_i)_{i\in [t_\red]}, (\ksiopp_i)_{i\in [t_\iopp]},$$ where  %\albertimportant{Make sure the Instantiation section accounts for the OOD term}
    %
        $$
    \kappa_0 = \theta(\cP,\cM,\codedim)
    $$
    (see \cref{l:examples_morphisms}).
    If Round 1 is not executed, the error term $\kappa_0$ and its round are absent, and \cref{c:core_iop} has $t_\red+t_\iopp$ rounds. % The case where $\delta$ exceeds the unique decoding radius of $\cC$, so that Round 0 is executed, is treated in \cref{a:OOD} (\cref{t:thm_core_IOPP_OOD}) under additional hypotheses on $\Pi_\red$ and $\Pi_\iopp$; it adds one round and an error term $\kappa_{\mathsf{OOD}}$.
    %Recall that $q$ is either the characteristic of $R$ if $R$ is a finite field, or $q=\codedim_1 \cdot \lVert \vv\rVert_{\infty} +1$ if $R=\ZZ$.
%
%
    %If  $\evalfield$ is a prime field, then \cref{c:core_iop} is a $t_\red+t_\iopp$-round IOP with round-by-round knowledge soundness with relaxation $(\LinBitsDual^\delta, \bot)$, with errors $(\ksred_i)_{i\in [t_\red]}, (\ksiopp_i)_{i\in [t_\iopp]}$.
\end{theorem}




 To prove \cref{l:examples_morphisms} we will use the following result, which generalizes \albert{point to lemma} in \cite{zincplus}.
\begin{lemma}\label{l:reduction_lemma}
Let $h\in \ZZ[X_1, \ldots, X_m]$ be a nonzero polynomial of total degree $\leq d$ and with $\lVert h\rVert_\infty\leq M$ for some $M\geq 1$. Let $\cP$ be a set of primes, and let $q_{\min}$ be the smallest prime in $\cP$. Given a prime $q$ and $\bm{\alpha}\in \FF_q^m$, write $\pi_{q,\bm{\alpha}}(h)\defeq \pi_q(h)(\bm{\alpha})\in \FF_q$. Then 
%
$$
\Pr_{\substack{q\gets \cP,\\\bm{\alpha} \gets \FF_q^m}}[\pi_{q,\bm{\alpha}}(h)=0] \leq \frac{\log M}{\lfloor\log(q_{\min})\rfloor\cdot |\cP|} + \frac{d}{q_{\min}}.
$$
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Let $\cE_{\mathsf{prime}}$ be the event that all coefficients of $h$ are  multiples of $q$. Then, by the Schwartz--Zippel lemma, $$\Pr_{\substack{q\gets \cP,\\\bm{\alpha} \gets \FF_q^m}}[\pi_{q,\bm{\alpha}}(h)=0\mid \neg \cE_{\mathsf{prime}}] \leq  \frac{d}{q_{\min}}.$$
%
Now, every nonzero coefficient $c$ of $h$ is divisible by at most $\log M/\log q_{\min}\leq \log M/\lfloor\log(q_{\min})\rfloor$ primes from $\cP$, since the product of $m'$ distinct such primes divides $c$, so that $q_{\min}^{m'}\leq |c|\leq M$. Hence, the probability that $\cE_{\mathsf{prime}}$ occurs is at most $(\log M/\lfloor\log(q_{\min})\rfloor)/|\cP|$, and the lemma follows.
%
\end{proof}



\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{l:examples_morphisms}]
Let $h\in \ZZ[X_1,\ldots, X_{k}]$ be a polynomial of total degree at most $2\cdot D$, with coefficients in $\range{-2\cdot T\cdot \codedim\cdot N_R^2}{2\cdot T\cdot \codedim\cdot N_R^2}$, and with $\picanon^{R}(h)\neq 0$ (cf.\ the definition of $\theta(\cP,\cM,\codedim)$). In particular $h$ is nonzero, and $\lVert h\rVert_\infty\leq M\defeq 2\cdot T\cdot \codedim\cdot N_R^2$. Since $h$ is nonzero and has total degree at most $2\cdot D$, \cref{l:reduction_lemma} (applied with $m=k$ variables and total degree $2\cdot D$) yields $$\Pr_{\substack{q\gets \cP,\\ \bm{\alpha} \gets \FF_q^{k}}}[\pi_q(h)(\bm{\alpha})=0] \leq \frac{\log M}{\lfloor\log(q_{\min})\rfloor\cdot |\cP|} + \frac{2\cdot D}{q_{\min}},$$ as needed.
%
\end{proof}


%The following lemma bounds the probability, over the OOD challenge $\zeta$ of Round 0, that two distinct bit vectors close to the oracle share the same OOD evaluation. Throughout, for $\zeta\in \comfield$ we write, as in Round 0 of \cref{c:core_iop}, $$\bm{\zeta}= \left(\zeta^{2^0}, \zeta^{2^1}, \ldots, \zeta^{2^{\log(\codedim/\comfieldexp)-1}}\right)\in \comfield^{\log (\codedim/\comfieldexp)}$$ for the corresponding evaluation point.


\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{t:thm_core_IOPP}] 
%
The completeness of \cref{c:core_iop} is clear from its description \albert{Review}.  Recall that, by hypothesis, Round 0 (OOD sampling) is skipped; the case where Round 0 is executed is treated in \cref{a:OOD}. 

We decompose  \cref{c:core_iop} into three IORs $\Pi_1, \Pi_2, \Pi_3$.  The first one, $\Pi_1$, is an IOR from $\LinBitsDual$ to $\PESAT$, and it consists of applying Rounds 1 and 2 of \cref{c:core_iop}, and outputting $$\left(\inp'=(g^{\bm{\gamma}}, g^{\bm{\mu}}),\  \oracle'= f, \ \wit'=\picomfield(\bff)\right),$$ as described in Phase 3 of \cref{c:core_iop}. Formally, $\Pi_1$ is a $2$-round IOR whose second verifier message is empty: the prover first sends $\mu'$, the verifier replies with $(q,\pirand)$, and the prover then sends $\bm{\mu}=(\mu_1,\ldots, \mu_{\codedim_2})$. The second IOR, $\Pi_2$, is simply $\Pi_{\mathsf{red}}$, reducing $\PESAT$ to $\LIN$. Finally, the third IOR, $\Pi_3$, is simply the IOP $\Pi_{\iopp}$ for $\LIN$.


 Below we show that $\Pi_1$  has round-by-round knowledge soundness with relaxation $(\LinBitsDual^{\delta}, \PESAT^{\delta})$, with errors $(\kappa_0, 0)$, assuming Round 1 is not skipped. The second verifier message of $\Pi_1$ is empty, hence the last prover message of $\Pi_1$ can be sent together with the first prover message of $\Pi_2$, and thus  \cref{c:core_iop} has $1+t_\red+t_\iopp$ rounds. The theorem then follows from the composition of IORs result (cf.\ \cref{r:ior_compositoin}). 




 At the end of the proof we discuss the case when Round 1 is skipped. 



\paragraph{Completeness and soundness of $\Pi_1$}
We describe a knowledge state function $\kstate$. Let $\inp=(\mu,\vv)\in \evalfield \times \evalfield^{\codedim}$ and  $\oracle=f\in \comfield^{\codelength}$. Let $\tr$ be a partial interaction transcript for $\Pi_1$. We start by setting $\kstate$ so that the first  condition in \cref{def:knowledge-state-function} is met. Precisely, if   $\tr=\emptyset$, we set $\kstate(\inp,\oracle,\tr,\ww) = 1$ if and only if $(\inp, \oracle, \ww)\in \LinBitsDual^{\delta}$. Further,  if $\tr=(\mu')$ consists of a single prover message, we set $\kstate(\inp,\oracle,\mu',\ww)=\kstate(\inp,\oracle,\emptyset,\ww)$, so that the second condition in \cref{def:knowledge-state-function} holds for the first prover message round.

After the verifier's message, $\tr$ is as follows:
%
$$
\tr=(\mu', (q,\pirand)) \in \ZZ[X_1, \ldots, X_{k}] \times \left(\cP \times \cM_q\right).
$$
%
We define $\kstate(\inp,\oracle,\tr,\ww)=1$ if and only if  the verifier's degree and range checks on $\mu'$ in Round 1 pass,  $\ww=\bff\in \bits^{\codedim}\subseteq \ZZ^\codedim$,  $\bff=(\bff_{*j})_{j\in [\codedim_2]}\in \left(\bits^{\codedim_1}\right)^{\codedim_2}$, and
%
\begin{equation}\label{e:core_iop_proof_eq1}
    \begin{aligned}
&\picanon(\mu')=\mu,\\
&\langle \pirand\circ \picanon^{-1}(\vv^{(1)}) \otimes \pirand\circ \picanon^{-1}(\vv^{(2)}), \pi_q(\bff)\rangle = \pirand(\mu') \quad \text{ over } \FF_q,\\
&\hamming(f,\Enc_{\cC}\circ\pack\circ \pi_2(\bff))< \delta.
    \end{aligned}
\end{equation}
%
We continue with our definition of $\kstate$.  Let $\tr=(\mu', (q,\pirand), \bm{\mu})$ for a prover message $\bm{\mu}=(\mu_1,\ldots, \mu_{\codedim_2})\in \range{0}{(q-1)\cdot \codedim_1}^{\codedim_2}$, or let $\tr$ be the full transcript obtained by appending the empty second verifier message to $(\mu', (q,\pirand), \bm{\mu})$. We set $\kstate(\inp,\oracle,\tr,\ww)=1$ if and only if the verifier accepts on $\tr$ (i.e.\ its checks in Rounds 1 and 2 pass) and $(\inp',\oracle',\ww)\in \PESAT^{\delta}$, where $(\inp',\oracle')$ is the output of the verifier, namely
%
$$
\inp'=(g^{\bm{\gamma}}, g^{\bm{\mu}}), \qquad \oracle'= f, \qquad \text{with } \bm{\gamma} = \pi_{q}^{-1}\left(\pirand\circ \picanon^{-1}(\vv^{(1)})\right) \in \range{0}{q-1}^{\codedim_1}\subseteq \ZZ^{\codedim_1}.
$$
%
 Thus $\kstate$ satisfies the third condition in \cref{def:knowledge-state-function}.



We define an extractor as $\rbrextractor(\inp,\oracle,\tr,\ww)=\ww$. It remains to prove two statements: first, that
%
\[
  \Pr_{q,\pirand}\!\left[
  \exists\,\ww \ \left| \ \begin{aligned}
  &\kstate\bigl(\inp,\oracle,\mu',\rbrextractor(\inp,\oracle,(\mu', (q,\pirand)),\ww)\bigr)=0\\
  \wedge\ &\kstate(\inp,\oracle,(\mu', (q,\pirand)), \ww)=1
  \end{aligned}
  \right.\right]
  \ \le\ \kappa_0;
\]
and second, that $\kstate$ satisfies the second condition in \cref{def:knowledge-state-function} for transcripts where the prover is about to send $\bm{\mu}$ (its second message).

\bigskip

We start with the first statement. Assume $$\kstate(\inp,\oracle,(\mu', (q,\pirand)), \ww)=1 \quad \text{and} \quad \kstate(\inp,\oracle,\mu',  \ww)=\kstate(\inp,\oracle,\ww)=0.$$
The latter occurs if and only if  $(\inp,\oracle,\ww)\notin \LinBitsDual^{\delta}$. The former implies that $\ww=\bff=(\bff_{*j})_{j\in[\codedim_2]}\in \bits^\codedim$ satisfies \eqref{e:core_iop_proof_eq1}; in particular $\hamming(f,\Enc_{\cC}\circ\pack\circ \pi_2(\bff))< \delta$, and hence, since $(\inp,\oracle,\ww)\notin \LinBitsDual^{\delta}$, necessarily
%
\begin{equation}\label{e:break_soundness_2}
\langle \pi(\bff), \vv\rangle \neq \mu \qquad \text{over } R.
\end{equation}
%
Define the polynomial $$h\defeq  \langle \picanon^{-1}(\vv^{(1)}) \otimes \picanon^{-1}(\vv^{(2)}), \bff \rangle- \mu' \ \in \ \ZZ[X_1, \ldots, X_{k}].
$$
Since $\picanon$ is a ring homomorphism satisfying $\picanon\circ \picanon^{-1}=\mathsf{id}$ and restricts to $\pi$ on $\ZZ$, the first condition in \eqref{e:core_iop_proof_eq1} gives $$\picanon(h) = \langle \pi(\bff), \vv^{(1)} \otimes \vv^{(2)} \rangle- \picanon(\mu') = \langle \pi(\bff), \vv\rangle - \mu,$$ which is nonzero by \eqref{e:break_soundness_2}. In particular $h\neq 0$. On the other hand, since $\pirand$ is a ring homomorphism restricting to $\pi_q$ on $\ZZ$, the second condition in \eqref{e:core_iop_proof_eq1} states precisely that $\pirand(h)=0$. We claim that $h$ has total degree at most $2\cdot D$ and satisfies $$\lVert h \rVert_{\infty}\leq 2\cdot T\cdot \codedim\cdot N_R^2.$$ Indeed, the lifts $\picanon^{-1}(\vv^{(1)}_i)$ and $\picanon^{-1}(\vv^{(2)}_j)$ have total degree at most $D$ and coefficients of absolute value at most $N_R$, so each product $\picanon^{-1}(\vv^{(1)}_i)\cdot \picanon^{-1}(\vv^{(2)}_j)$ has total degree at most $2\cdot D$ and coefficients of absolute value at most $T\cdot N_R^2$ by the definition of $T$. Further, by the verifier's checks in Round 1, $$\lVert \mu'\rVert_{\infty} \leq T\cdot \codedim\cdot N_R^2$$ and the total degree of $\mu'$ is at most $2\cdot D$. The claim follows now from the fact that the entries of $\bff$ are bits and there are $\codedim$ of them.


Since additionally $\picanon(h)\neq 0$, the polynomial $h$ belongs to the class of polynomials over which $\theta(\cP,\cM,\codedim)$ is defined. Finally,  since $\delta$ is within the unique decoding radius of $\cC$, the code $\cC$ is $(\delta,1)$-list-decodable, and hence there is at most one $\ww$ such that \ $\hamming(f,\Enc_{\cC}\circ\pack\circ \pi_2(\ww))< \delta$.  Therefore  \albert{review} $$
\begin{aligned}
&\Pr_{q,\pirand}[\exists \ww  \text{ such that } (\inp,\oracle,\ww)\notin \LinBitsDual^\delta \text{ and } \kstate(\inp,\oracle,(\mu', (q,\pirand)), \ww)=1]\\ \leq &\Pr_{q\gets \cP,\ \pirand\gets \cM_q}[\pirand(h)=0] \leq \theta(\cP,\cM,\codedim) = \kappa_0,
\end{aligned}
$$
%
where the last inequality holds by the definition of $\theta(\cP,\cM,\codedim)$.

\bigskip

It remains to prove the second statement, namely: if $\kstate(\inp,\oracle,(\mu', (q,\pirand)),\ww)=0$, then $\kstate(\inp,\oracle,(\mu', (q,\pirand), \bm{\mu}),\ww)=0$ for every prover message $\bm{\mu}=(\mu_1,\ldots, \mu_{\codedim_2})\in \range{0}{(q-1)\cdot \codedim_1}^{\codedim_2}$. Suppose towards a contradiction that $\kstate(\inp,\oracle,(\mu', (q,\pirand), \bm{\mu}),\ww)=1$, i.e.\ that the verifier accepts and $(\inp',\oracle',\ww)\in \PESAT^{\delta}$. Then $\ww=\bff=(\bff_{*j})_{j\in [\codedim_2]}\in \bits^\codedim$, $\hamming( f,\Enc_{\cC}\circ \pack\circ \pi_2(\bff)) < \delta$, and, for all $j\in [\codedim_2]$, $$g^{\langle \bff_{*j}, \bm{\gamma}\rangle} = \prod_{i \in [\codedim_1]} \left(\left(g^{\bm{\gamma}_i}-1\right)\cdot \pi_2(\bff_{ij}) +1\right)= g^{\mu_j},$$ and hence $$\langle \bff_{*j}, \bm{\gamma}\rangle = \mu_j \mod \mathsf{ord}(g),$$ where $\mathsf{ord}(g)$ denotes the multiplicative order of $g$. Now, since $\bm{\gamma}_i\in \range{0}{q-1}$, $\mu_j\in \range{0}{(q-1)\cdot \codedim_1}$, and $\bff_{*j}$ contains bits, we have $|\langle \bff_{*j}, \bm{\gamma}\rangle - \mu_j| \leq \codedim_1 \cdot (q-1)$. Since $g$ is a generator of $\comfield^*$ and $q\leq \max\cP< \fieldboundsizeexponent$, we have $$\mathsf{ord}(g) = |\comfield|-1> \codedim_1\cdot \max\cP \geq \codedim_1\cdot q> \codedim_1 \cdot (q-1),$$ and hence
%
\begin{equation}\label{e:mu_j_integer_equalities}
\langle \bff_{*j}, \bm{\gamma}\rangle = \mu_j \qquad \text{over } \ZZ, \qquad \text{ for all } j\in [\codedim_2].
\end{equation}
%
Applying $\pi_q$ to \eqref{e:mu_j_integer_equalities}, and using that $\pi_q(\bm{\gamma})=\pirand\circ \picanon^{-1}(\vv^{(1)})$, we obtain $$\langle \pi_q(\bff_{*j}), \pirand\circ \picanon^{-1}(\vv^{(1)})\rangle = \pi_q(\mu_j) \quad \text{ over } \FF_q, \qquad \text{ for all } j\in [\codedim_2].$$ Since the verifier accepts, its check \eqref{e:tensor_decomposition_reconstruction} in Round 2 passes, i.e.\ $\sum_{j\in [\codedim_2]} \pi_q(\mu_j)\cdot \left(\pirand\circ \picanon^{-1}(\vv^{(2)})\right)_j = \pirand(\mu')$. Now
%
\begin{align*}
&\langle \pirand\circ \picanon^{-1}(\vv^{(1)}) \otimes \pirand\circ \picanon^{-1}(\vv^{(2)}), \pi_q(\bff)\rangle\\ =& \sum_{j\in [\codedim_2]} \langle \pi_q(\bff_{*j}), \pirand\circ \picanon^{-1}(\vv^{(1)})\rangle \cdot \left(\pirand\circ \picanon^{-1}(\vv^{(2)})\right)_j \\ =& \sum_{j\in [\codedim_2]} \pi_q(\mu_j)\cdot \left(\pirand\circ \picanon^{-1}(\vv^{(2)})\right)_j = \pirand(\mu').
\end{align*}
%
Hence the second condition in \eqref{e:core_iop_proof_eq1} holds. The first condition in \eqref{e:core_iop_proof_eq1}, i.e.\ $\picanon(\mu')=\mu$, holds because the verifier accepts, and the third one, i.e.\ $\hamming(f, \Enc_{\cC}\circ \pack \circ\pi_2(\bff))<\delta$, holds because $(\inp',\oracle',\ww)\in \PESAT^{\delta}$. Hence $\kstate(\inp,\oracle,(\mu', (q,\pirand)),\ww)=1$, a contradiction.




\paragraph{The case where Round 1 is skipped.} \albert{Review that I'm not missing any important argument} In this case, the messages $\mu'$ and $(q,\pirand)$ and the error $\kappa_0$ disappear, and essentially the arguments above apply after replacing $\pirand\circ \picanon^{-1}$ with the identity map on $\FF_q$ or on $\ZZ$. In the latter case,  $\pi_q$ is also replaced by the identity map, cf.\ \cref{r:skip_round_1_integers}.

\end{proof}








\subsection{The general LinBitsRings relation}\label{s:general_LinBitsDual}


\renewcommand{\modulemorphism}{\psi}
\renewcommand{\ringmorphism}{\psi}
\renewcommand{\moduleonebound}{{S_{\leq B}^{<D_S}}}

We now proceed to describe the full relation we seek to work with in this section.  Compared to $\LinBitsDual(\bits, \pi, R, \cC)$,   the witness $\bff$ will now have entries in an arbitrary finitely generated ring $S$,  instead of in $\bits\subseteq \ZZ$.  

The witness entries will be restricted to a bounded subset of $S$, so that each witness can be expressed as a pre-specified linear expression (over $S$) of a string of bits.


We set the following additional notation:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
 \item Let $S$ be a ring, possibly different than $R$, let $\psi:S \to R$ be a ring homomorphism. %Let $M$ be a $S$-module and  let $\psi: \moduleone \to R$ be a module homomorphism (with underlying ring homomorphism $\ringmorphism$ \albert{terminology in preliminaries}).
\item 
Let $D_S\geq 1$ be a degree bound, and let $\gamma_1=1, \gamma_2,\ldots, \gamma_\moduledegree\in S$ be the monomials of total degree less than $D_S$ in a fixed set of generators of $S$ (\cref{s:prelims_algebra}), so that $\moduledegree$ is the number of such monomials. Concretely $\moduledegree\leq \binom{D_S-1+k_S}{k_S}$, where $1,\eta_1,\ldots,\eta_{k_S}$ are the generators of $S$.
\item Recall from \cref{s:prelims_algebra} that $\moduleonebound$ denotes the set of elements of $S$ of the form $h=\sum_{i\in [\moduledegree]} h_i\cdot \gamma_i$ for $$h_i \in \range{0}{2^B-1}_S \subseteq  S,$$ and that for a vector of integers $\ww$, we write $\ww_S$ for its projection to $S$ via reduction modulo $\Char(S)$ (see \eqref{e:integers_subscript_proj}).
     \item Let $\codedim_0$ be a vector witness length, so that our witnesses are $\bff\in S^{\codedim_0}$.    
     \end{itemize}

     We will use the natural homomorphism $\pi_{\Char(S)} : \ZZ \to S$ consisting in reducing an element modulo the characteristic of $S$, see  \eqref{e:Z_to_ring} in \cref{s:prelims_algebra}. 

\begin{definition}[Bitification of the witness]\label{d:bitification}
Let $\bff \in (\moduleonebound)^{\codedim_0}$. Let $\codedim$ be a  vector length, called \emph{bitification  length}. Let $L\in S^{\codedim_0 \times \codedim}$ be a matrix, called  \emph{bitification matrix}. Let $\bfg\in \bits^\codedim\subseteq \ZZ^\codedim$ be a vector filled with integer bits. We say $\bfg$ is a
\emph{bitification} of $\bff$ (with respect to $L$)  if
%
$$
\bff = L \cdot \bfg_S.
$$
%
where for a vector $\ww$ of integer bits, $\ww_S$ denotes its natural projection to a vector with entries the elements $\bits_S \subseteq S$. 
%
Additionally, we require that\footnote{This condition is required to guarantee that extractors obtain witnesses in $\moduleonebound$. In some applications, proving that the prover knows a witness in $S$ may suffice, and in that case the condition can be dropped.} $$L\cdot \bfg_S\in (\moduleonebound)^{\codedim_0} \qquad \text{for all } \bfg\in \bits^{\codedim}.$$  This  condition holds for the standard bitification matrix below. 


The choice of $L$ and a method for building a bitification of $\bff$  can be customized. Below, we describe what we call the \emph{standard bitification} procedure (see below). This can be used for arbitrary finitely generated rings $S$ and bit bounds $B$. In some scenarios (see \cref{r: non_uniform_bit_decomposition}), one may choose different more convenient approaches.

For a fixed $L$ and $\bff$, bitifications of $\bff$ are not unique in general. Nevertheless, abusing the notation, we often denote them as $$\bitstext(\bff).$$ 
%
A \emph{standard bitification} of $\bff\in (\moduleonebound)^{\codedim_0}
$, denoted $\bitstext(\bff)$, is obtained as follows: write
%$\bff=(\bff_{*1}, \ldots, \bff_{*\moduledegree})$ 
$\bff=\sum_{j\in [\moduledegree]} \bff_{*j}\cdot \gamma_j$
for some $\bff_{*j}\in \left(\range{0}{2^B-1}_S\right)^{\codedim_0}$, $j\in [\moduledegree]$ (this expression is not unique).  Then write $$\bff_{*j}= \sum_{k=1}^{B}\  \bff_{*jk}\cdot \pi_{\Char(S)}(2^{k-1}) $$ for some vectors $$\bff_{*jk}\in \{0,1\}_S^{\codedim_0}\subseteq S^{\codedim_0},$$ for all $j,k$.  We  define   $$
\begin{aligned}
& \codedim\defeq \codedim_0\cdot \moduledegree\cdot B,\\
&\bitstext(\bff)\in \bits^{\codedim} \subseteq \ZZ^{\codedim}\end{aligned}$$ as the vector with integer bit entries obtained by concatenating the vectors $\bff_{*jk}$ and lifting and reordering their entries to $\bits \subseteq \ZZ$ in a natural way, i.e.\ \begin{equation}\label{e:bits_of_f}\bitstext(\bff)\defeq \left(\mathsf{lift}(\bff_{ijk})\right)_{i\in [\codedim_0], k\in [B], j\in [\moduledegree]}\in \bits^{\codedim} \subseteq \ZZ^{\codedim},\end{equation}
where $\mathsf{lift}$ is the identity function if $\Char(S)=0$,  and  $\mathsf{lift} = \pi_{\Char(S)}^{-1}$ if $\Char(S)>0$.  Defining 
$$L=\mathsf{Id}_{\codedim_0\times \codedim_0} \otimes (1, 2, \ldots, 2^{B-1})_S \otimes (\gamma_1, \ldots, \gamma_d) \in S^{\codedim_0\times \codedim},$$
we have
$$
\bff = L \cdot \bitstext(\bff)_S.
$$ 
\end{definition}


Assume $\cC$ is a linear code over $\comfield$ of dimension $\codedim/\comfieldexp$ and blocklength $\codelength$. 



\begin{definition} The \emph{general LinBitsRings}  relation is defined as \albert{Here we could allow for $\psi = \phi \circ \picanon^{S}{}^{-1}$ for $\phi: \ZZ[X_1, \ldots, X_k]\to R$ a morphism. This is what is actually happening and it allows us to build later full IOPs for constraints over arbitrary f.g.\ rings}
%
\begin{align*}
 &\LinModFieldBin(\moduleonebound,\modulemorphism,R,  \cC,L)\\ &\qquad\qquad\qquad \defeq \left\{ \left(\begin{aligned} &\inp=(\mu, \vv)\in R  \times R^{ \codedim_0}, \\
 &\oracle = f \in \comfield^{\codelength}, \\ &\wit= \bff\in (\moduleonebound)^{\codedim_0}\end{aligned}\right) \;\middle|\;  \begin{aligned}  
 &f = \Enc_{\cC}\circ \pack\circ\pi_2(\bitstext(\bff)),\\
 &\langle \modulemorphism(\bff), \vv \rangle = \mu  \qquad  \text{in } R,\\
 &  \bff = L \cdot (\bitstext(\bff))_S \ \text{for some } \bitstext(\bff)\in \bits^{\codedim}\subseteq\ZZ^\codedim
 \end{aligned} \right\}.
\end{align*}
\end{definition}
%
When there is no risk of confusion, we denote $\LinModFieldBin(\moduleonebound,\modulemorphism,\moduletwo,  \cC, L)$ simply by $\LinModFieldBin$. The Core LinBitsRings relation is precisely $\LinModFieldBin(\moduleonebound,\modulemorphism,\moduletwo,  \cC, L)$ after taking $S=\ZZ$, $B=1$, $\modulemorphism = \pi_{\Char(R)}$, and $L=\mathsf{Id}_{\codedim\times \codedim}$ (the standard bitification matrix in this case, so that $\codedim_0=\codedim$).

%\renewcommand{\modulemorphism}

For $\delta\in (0,1)$, we let $\LinModFieldBin^\delta$ be the relation $\LinModFieldBin$ after replacing the constraint $f=\Enc_{\cC}\circ \pack\circ\pi_2(\bitstext(\bff))$ by $\hamming(f,\Enc_{\cC}\circ \pack\circ\pi_2(\bitstext(\bff)))<\delta$, as in \cref{s:linmodfieldbin}.

\begin{remark}[Non-uniform bit-decomposition sizes]\label{r: non_uniform_bit_decomposition} In practical settings, it is possible that not all entries of $\bff$ have the same bit size. For example, some chunks of $\bff$ may be comprised of elements from $\{0,1\}_S$, while others may be comprised of generic ring elements. However, the standard bitification procedure from \cref{d:bitification} allocates $\moduledegree\cdot B$ bits for every entry of $\bff$, no matter its size. To avoid this, one can find alternative approaches to defining $L$ and $\bitstext(\bff)$. 

For efficiency purposes, one needs to make sure that $L$ has enough structure to later support  tensor decomposing certain vectors, as discussed in \cref{r:tensor_dec_u}. In any case, one can always use the matrix $L$ above, which in general does possess enough structure as argued in \cref{r:tensor_dec_u}.
%
\end{remark}


\subsection{The \ftwoz\  IOP for the general LinBitsRings relation}\label{s: general_bitz}



\renewcommand{\modulemorphism}{\psi}
\renewcommand{\ringmorphism}{\psi}
\renewcommand{\moduleonebound}{{S_{\leq B}^{<D_S}}}

Next we describe our IOP for  the general relation $\LinModFieldBin$.  We assume the notation of \cref{s:general_LinBitsDual}. Let  $L$ be a \emph{bitification} matrix as in \cref{d:bitification}, with $L\in S^{\codedim_0 \times \codedim}$ so that $\codedim$ is the vector length of the vector $\bitstext(\bff)$. For the standard bitification matrix   $L=\mathsf{Id}_{\codedim_0 \times \codedim_0} \otimes (1,\ldots, 2^{B-1})_S\otimes (\gamma_1, \ldots, \gamma_d)$, we have $\codedim=\codedim_0\cdot \moduledegree\cdot B$. Let further $\codedim_1,\codedim_2$ be tensor decomposition parameters (to be used in \cref{c:core_iop}) such that $\codedim=\codedim_1\cdot \codedim_2$. We assume that $\cC$ is a linear code over $\comfield$ of dimension $\codedim/\comfieldexp$. %, which is a $\codedim_2$-interleaving of a code $\cC_1$ over $\comfield$ of dimension $\codedim_1/2^{\comfieldexp}$.

We will need the following result.


\begin{lemma}\label{l:bitify}
    Let $\bff\in (\moduleonebound)^{\codedim_0}$,  let $\vv\in \moduletwo^{\codedim_0}$, and let $\bitstext(\bff)$ be a bitification of $\bff$ with respect to $L$. Then %\albertimportant{General comment: I need to discuss how the verifier handles these vectors $\vv$ and $\uu$. Reviewers may complain that verifier could be non-succinct.} \albertimportant{Answer: write the tables mentioned above}
       \begin{align*}
   & \langle \psi(\bff), \vv \rangle = \langle \bitstext(\bff)_R,\uu\rangle \in R \\ & 
    \uu\defeq \psi(L)^{\mathsf{T}}\cdot \vv \in R^{\codedim},
    \end{align*}
    %
    where $\mathsf{T}$ denotes transposition.  In particular, if $L=\mathsf{Id}_{\codedim_0 \times \codedim_0} \otimes (1,\ldots, 2^{B-1})_S\otimes (\gamma_1, \ldots, \gamma_d) \in S^{\codedim_0 \times (\codedim_0\cdot B\cdot \moduledegree)} = S^{\codedim_0 \times \codedim}$ (cf.\ \cref{d:bitification}), then 
    \begin{equation}\label{e:u_def}
    \uu =   \left(\vv \otimes \left(1, 2, \ldots, 2^{B-1}\right)_R \otimes \left(\modulemorphism(\gamma_1), \ldots, \modulemorphism(\gamma_{\moduledegree})\right)\right) \in R^{\codedim}
    \end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Rewriting $\langle \psi(\bff), \vv \rangle$, we obtain  by \cref{d:bitification,e:matrix_transpose_inner_product}
$$
\langle \psi(\bff), \vv\rangle = \langle \psi(L\cdot \bitstext(\bff)_S), \vv\rangle =  \langle \psi(\bitstext(\bff)_S), \psi(L)^{\mathsf{T}} \cdot \vv\rangle 
$$
%
Now $\psi(\bitstext(\bff)_S) = \bitstext(\bff)_R$ because every entry of $\bitstext(\bff)$ consists of bits and $\psi$ is a ring homomorphism, so it sends the $0$ and $1$ elements of $S$ to the $0$ and $1$ elements of $R$. Further, $$\psi(L) = \mathsf{Id}_{\codedim_0\times \codedim_0} \otimes \psi( (1, \ldots, 2^{B-1})_S) \otimes (\psi(\gamma_1),\ldots, \psi(\gamma_\moduledegree)),  $$ 
and $\psi \circ \pi_{\Char(S)}(2^i) = \pi_{\Char(R)}(2^i)$ because $\psi \circ \pi_{\Char(S)}$ is a ring homomorphism,  $\psi\circ \pi_{\Char(S)}(2^i) = 2^i \cdot (\psi\circ \pi_{\Char(S)}(1))$, hence $\psi\circ \pi_{\Char(S)}(2^i)$ is $2^i$ times the identity element of $R$, and that is precisely the definition of $\pi_{\Char(R)}(2^i)$. 

The specific form of $\uu$ in \eqref{e:u_def} is now obtained by direct calculation of $\psi(L)^{\mathsf{T}} \cdot \vv$. 
%
\begin{comment}
\begin{align}
    \langle \modulemorphism(\bff),\vv\rangle &= \langle \modulemorphism\left(\sum_{\substack{k\in [B],\\ j\in [\moduledegree]}} \bff_{*jk} \cdot 2^{k-1} \cdot \gamma_j\right), \vv \rangle\label{e:iop_linear_claim_1}\\ &= \langle \sum_{\substack{k\in [B],\\ j\in [\moduledegree]}} \ringmorphism(\bff_{*jk}) \cdot \ringmorphism(2^{k-1}) \cdot \modulemorphism(\gamma_j),\vv \rangle,\label{e:iop_linear_claim_2}\\
    &= \sum_{\substack{k\in [B],\\ j\in [\moduledegree]}} \ringmorphism(2^{k-1}) \cdot \modulemorphism(\gamma_j)\cdot \langle \ringmorphism(\bff_{*jk}), \vv \rangle \notag\\ &=   \sum_{\substack{k\in [B],\\ j\in [\moduledegree]}} \sum_{i\in [\codedim_0]} \ringmorphism(2^{k-1}) \cdot \modulemorphism(\gamma_j)\cdot  \langle \ringmorphism(\bff_{ijk}), \vv_i \rangle= \langle \ringmorphism(\bitstext(\bff)), \uu \rangle \notag,
\end{align}
as needed.
\end{comment}
\end{proof}

We assume that the input vector $\vv$ for $\LinModFieldBin(\moduleonebound, \psi, R,\cC,L)$, the matrix $L$, and the tensor decomposition dimensions are such that $\uu= \psi(L)^\mathsf{T}\cdot \vv$ admits a tensor decomposition $\uu=\uu^{(1)}\otimes \uu^{(2)}$ with $\uu^{(1)}\in R^{\codedim_1}$, $\uu^{(2)}\in R^{\codedim_2}$, see \cref{r:tensor_dec_u} for an explanation that this can be achieved easily in  applications, and what to do otherwise. 

\begin{construction}[IOP for $\LinModFieldBin$]\label{c:LinModFieldBin}

 \textbf{Parameters.} $(\codedim_0, \codedim, \codedim_1,\codedim_2, \Pi_\iopp, \Pi_\red, \cP, (\cM_q)_{q\in \cP})$ comprised of: a witness vector length $\codedim_0$; a \emph{bitified witness} vector length $\codedim$; tensor decomposition dimensions $\codedim_1, \codedim_2$ for \cref{c:core_iop} satisfying $\codedim_1\cdot \codedim_2 = \codedim$; IORs and IOPs $\Pi_\iopp$ and $\Pi_\red$ as in \cref{c:core_iop}; and sets of primes and homomorphisms $ \cP, (\cM_q)_{q\in \cP}$ again as in \cref{c:core_iop}.\\


\noindent \textbf{Inputs.} The prover and verifier receive $\inp=(\mu, \vv)\in \moduletwo\times R^{\codedim_0}$ and  $\oracle= f \in \comfield^\codelength$. The prover receives  $\wit= \bff\in (\moduleonebound)^{\codedim_0}$ such that $(\inp,\oracle, \wit) \in \LinModFieldBin(\moduleonebound,\modulemorphism, \moduletwo, \cC, L)$, i.e., 
%
$$
f = \Enc_{\cC}\circ \pack\circ\pi_2(\bitstext(\bff)),\qquad \langle \psi(\bff), \vv \rangle = \mu \quad \text{in } R.
$$
for some $\bitstext(\bff)$ such that $L\cdot \bitstext(\bff)_S =\bff$.
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
     \item By \cref{l:bitify}, the   claim $(\inp,\oracle, \wit) \in \LinModFieldBin(\moduleonebound,\modulemorphism, R,  \cC, L)$ is equivalent to the claim \begin{equation}\label{e:iop_final_linear_claim}
     \left(\inp'=(\mu, \uu), \oracle' = f=\Enc_{\cC}\circ \pack\circ\pi_2(\bitstext(\bff)), \wit' = \bitstext(\bff)\right) \in \LinBitsDual(\bits,\pi, R,\cC).
\end{equation}
where $\uu = \psi(L)^{\mathsf{T}}\cdot \vv$. 

By assumption, $\uu=\uu^{(1)}\otimes \uu^{(2)}$ with $\uu^{(1)}\in R^{\codedim_1}$, $\uu^{(2)}\in R^{\codedim_2}$. Then the prover and the verifier use \cref{c:core_iop} to prove \eqref{e:iop_final_linear_claim}. They use parameters $(\codedim,\codedim_1,\codedim_2,\Pi_\iopp, \Pi_\red, \cP, (\cM_q)_{q\in \cP})$.  See \cref{r:tensor_dec_u} for comments on how to obtain tensor decompositions of $\uu$ in \cref{c:core_iop} assuming $\vv$ is appropriately tensor-decomposable.
 %  \left(\vv \otimes \left(1, \ringmorphism(2), \ldots, \ringmorphism(2^{B-1})\right) \otimes \left(\modulemorphism(\gamma_1), \ldots, \modulemorphism(\gamma_{\moduledegree})\right)\right)$. 
 \end{enumerate}
\end{construction}


\begin{remark}[Tensor decompositions of $\uu$]\label{r:tensor_dec_u}
Recall that \cref{c:core_iop} considers a (possibly trivial) tensor decomposition of its public input vector $\uu$ (denoted $\vv$ in \cref{c:core_iop}). Moreover, as argued in \cref{s:to_low_entropy_gkr,s:gkr_low_entropy}, this tensor decomposition is crucial for performance of \ftwoz\ (concretely, we seek $\codedim^{0.5}\leq \codedim_1 \leq \codedim^{0.6}$, cf.\ \cref{s:gkr_low_entropy}).   

In many applications, the input vector $\vv$ for $\LinModFieldBin(\moduleonebound,\modulemorphism, \moduletwo,  \cC, L)$ is an ``eq'' vector, i.e.\ a vector of the form $(\eq(\bb;\rr))_{\bb\in\bits^{\log\codedim_0}}$ for some $\rr\in \moduletwo^{\log \codedim_0}$. In this case  $\vv=\vv^{(1)}\otimes \vv^{(2)}$ with the dimensions of $\vv^{(1)}$, $\vv^{(2)}$ being arbitrary powers of two as long as $|\vv^{(1)}|\cdot |\vv^{(2)}|=\codedim_0$. In that case, if \begin{equation}\label{e:canonical_bitification_matrix}L=\mathsf{Id}_{\codedim_0\times \codedim_0} \otimes (1,\ldots, 2^{B-1})_S\otimes (\gamma_1,\ldots, \gamma_\moduledegree)\end{equation} (cf.\ \cref{d:bitification}), then $\uu$ has the form \eqref{e:u_def}, i.e.\ $$ \uu =   \left(\vv \otimes \left(1, 2, \ldots, 2^{B-1}\right)_R \otimes \left(\modulemorphism(\gamma_1), \ldots, \modulemorphism(\gamma_{\moduledegree})\right)\right) $$ and we may use a tensor decomposition for $\uu$ in \cref{c:core_iop} of the form, say, $\uu=\uu^{(1)}\otimes \uu^{(2)}$, where
%
$$
\uu^{(1)} \defeq \vv^{(1)},\quad \uu^{(2)}\defeq \vv^{(2)}\otimes \left(1, 2, \ldots, 2^{B-1}\right)_R \otimes \left(\modulemorphism(\gamma_1), \ldots, \modulemorphism(\gamma_{\moduledegree})\right).
$$
Other decompositions apply. For example one of the tensor factors in $\uu^{(2)}$ above could be placed in $\uu^{(1)}$, and the vector  $\left(1, 2, \ldots, 2^{B-1}\right)_R$ can admit further tensor decomposition, and some of the factors can be placed in $\uu^{(1)}$.

%
For more general matrices $L$, one needs to make sure that $L$ has enough structure to allow for a favorable tensor decomposition of $\uu$. If that is not possible, one  can either: 
\begin{itemize}
[leftmargin=*,nosep]
\item Fall back to using the matrix \eqref{e:canonical_bitification_matrix}, which is always available for arbitrary finitely generated rings $S$ with bit-bound $B$, or
\item Alternatively, if the ring $R$ supports it (e.g.\ if $R$ is a finite field or a ring with a large exceptional set), one can run a sumcheck to reduce the claim $\langle \bitstext(\bff)_R, \uu\rangle = \mu$ to an MLE evaluation claim over $R$, thereby ``replacing'' the vector $\uu$ by an ``eq vector'' that, as such, admits tensor decompositions of arbitrary dimensions.
\end{itemize}
\end{remark}

\paragraph{Costs.} \albertimportant{Review. Computed with AI. Justify here and in the previous table why we use $k=1$?} We discuss the costs of \Cref{c:LinModFieldBin}. The rounds, oracles and queries of \cref{c:LinModFieldBin} are exactly those of \cref{c:core_iop} (see \cref{t:core_iop_complexity}), which is run on the bitified witness $\bitstext(\bff)$ of length $\codedim=\codedim_0\cdot \moduledegree\cdot B$ (if using the standard bitification matrix of \cref{d:bitification}), and with the weight vector $\uu=\uu^{(1)}\otimes \uu^{(2)}$ of \cref{r:tensor_dec_u}. For $R=\ZZ_n$ ($k=0$)  or $R=\ZZ_n[X]/(h(X))$ ($k=1$) for some $n\geq 0$ and polynomial $h(X)$, the costs of executing \cref{c:core_iop} are the same as those listed in \cref{t:core_iop_complexity}.   The only additional costs of \cref{c:LinModFieldBin} are those of computing the bitification of $\bff$ and of computing $\uu^{(1)}$ and $\uu^{(2)}$. Note that, when using the standard bitification matrix, the prover and verifier do not need to compute $\uu$ entirely: it suffices to compute $\uu^{(1)}, \uu^{(2)}$.  Hence the verifier's work outside $\Pi_\red$ and $\Pi_\iopp$ is $O(\codedim_1+\codedim_2)$ operations (cf.\ \cref{t:core_iop_complexity}).  In \Cref{t:LinModFieldBin_complexity} we summarize the costs of \cref{c:LinModFieldBin}. 


%For a general bitification matrix $L$, computing $\uu=\psi(L)^{\mathsf{T}}\cdot \vv$ costs $O(\mathsf{nnz}(L))$ operations in $R$, where $\mathsf{nnz}(L)$ denotes the number of nonzero entries of $L$.

\begin{table}[H]
  \centering
  \begin{tabular}{l|l|l|l}
    \toprule
     & Prover & Verifier & Proof (bits) \\
    \hline
    Bitification of $\bff$ (\cref{d:bitification}) & $O(\codedim)$ bit op. & -- & -- \\
    \hline
    Instance translation $(\mu,\vv)\mapsto (\mu, \uu^{(1)}, \uu^{(2)})$ & $O(\codedim_2)\cdot R$ & same as prover & -- \\
    \hline
    \Cref{c:core_iop} with length $\codedim=\codedim_0\cdot \moduledegree\cdot B$ & \cref{t:core_iop_complexity} & \cref{t:core_iop_complexity} & \cref{t:core_iop_complexity} \\
    \bottomrule
  \end{tabular}
  \caption{Costs of \cref{c:LinModFieldBin} for the standard bitification matrix and the tensor decomposition of \cref{r:tensor_dec_u}. By $N\cdot R$ we indicate $N$ operations in $R$.}
  \label{t:LinModFieldBin_complexity}
\end{table}

\begin{theorem}\label{t:thm_LinModFieldBits}
    Under the same hypotheses and notation as in \cref{t:thm_core_IOPP} for the parameters $(\codedim,\codedim_1,\codedim_2, \Pi_\iopp,\Pi_\red, \cP, (\cM_q)_{q\in \cP})$, the  ring $R$, the code $\cC$, and the binary field $\comfield$ with packing map $\pack$, \cref{c:LinModFieldBin} is a perfectly complete $(1+t_\red + t_\iopp)$-round IOP for $\LinModFieldBin(\moduleonebound,\modulemorphism, \moduletwo, \cC,L)$ with round-by-round knowledge soundness with relaxation $(\LinModFieldBin^{\delta},\bot)$ and errors $$\kappa_0, (\ksred_i)_{i\in [t_\red]}, (\ksiopp_i)_{i\in [t_\iopp]},$$ where $
    \kappa_0 = \theta(\cP,\cM,\codedim). %\frac{\degevalfield}{q_{\min}} + \frac{\log(\codedim_1 +1) + \log(q-1)}{|\cP|\cdot \lfloor\log(q_{\min})\rfloor}.
    $ (see \cref{t:thm_core_IOPP}; here $\theta(\cP,\cM,\codedim)$ is computed with respect to the tensor decomposition $\uu=\uu^{(1)}\otimes \uu^{(2)}$ of \cref{r:tensor_dec_u} used in \cref{c:core_iop}, i.e.\ the quantities $D$, $T$, $N_R$ are determined by the lifts of the entries of $\uu^{(1)}$ and $\uu^{(2)}$).
    
    The error term $\kappa_0$ and its round are absent if Round 1 of \cref{c:core_iop} is not executed. If Round 0 of \cref{c:core_iop} is executed, the same holds under the hypotheses of \cref{t:thm_core_IOPP_OOD}, with the additional round and the additional error term $\kappa_{\mathsf{OOD}}$ of that theorem.
    %If $\degevalfield=0$, i.e.\ $\evalfield$ is a prime field, then \cref{c:core_iop} is a $t_\red+t_\iopp$-round IOP with round-by-round knowledge soundness with relaxation $(\LinBitsDual^\delta, \bot)$, with errors $\kappa_0, (\ksred_i)_{i\in [t_\red]}, (\ksiopp_i)_{i\in [t_\iopp]}$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof} Deferred to \cref{a:LinModFieldBin}. In short, the result is a consequence of \cref{l:bitify}, which establishes an equivalence between  triples for $\LinModFieldBin(\moduleonebound,\modulemorphism, \moduletwo, \cC,L)$ and $\LinBitsDual(\bits,\pi, \moduletwo,\cC)$. \cref{l:bitify} further provides a method for efficiently computing one triple from the other. This allows us to build a round-by-round knowledge soundness state and an extractor for \cref{c:LinModFieldBin} from those of \cref{c:core_iop}.
\end{proof}



\subsection{Instantiation}\label{s:instantiation}
We instantiate \cref{c:core_iop,c:LinModFieldBin} by letting $\Pi_{\red}$ be an optimized GKR protocol for multiple grand product claims of certain form (cf.\ \cref{s:gkr_low_entropy}). The IOP $\Pi_\iopp$ is taken to be WHIR/Ligerito \cite{whir,ligerito} with the ring switching step from \cite{binius}. Our implementation of WHIR  is taken from Flock's \cite{flock} implementation. The inner product claims when $\Pi_\iopp$ is called are not MLE evaluation claims. Since  the ring-switch implementation in \cite{flock} does not support arbitrary inner product claims,   we run a sumcheck to reduce to those to MLE evaluation claims. Alternatively, one could avoid the sumcheck and use the approach from \cref{a:ring_switch_1}. We instantiate them solely with the following parameters:
\begin{table}[H]
    \centering
    \begin{tabular}{c|c}
        Parameter & Choices \\
        \hline\hline
        $R$ & A finite field (possibly non-prime)\\
        \hline
        $S$ &  Integers $\ZZ$\\
        \hline
        $\pi$ & Reduction mod $\Char(R)$\\
        \hline
        $\psi$ & Reduction mod $\Char(R)$\\
        \hline
        $\comfieldexp$ & $128$\\
        \hline
        $\cC$ & Reed-Solomon code over $\comfield$ of rate $1/2$ or $1/8$\\
        \hline
        Proximity parameter $\delta$ & Close to the Johnson bound\footnote{Except when targeting $>100$ bits of security, in which case we use the unique decoding radius}\\
        \hline
        $L$ & The standard bitification matrix (\cref{e:canonical_bitification_matrix})\\ 
         \hline
        $\cP$ & Set of primes. Discussed below\\
         \hline
        $\cM_q=\{\pi_q\}$ & Reduction modulo $q$\\
        \hline $\codedim_1,\codedim_2$ & Discussed below
    \end{tabular}
    \caption{Parameter choices in our deployment of \cref{c:core_iop,c:LinModFieldBin}}
    \label{tab:instantiation_params}
\end{table}

We next provide example  instantiations of \cref{c:core_iop,c:LinModFieldBin} so as to achieve  $\lambda=128$ bits of security, while keeping the protocols as efficient as possible, with the main challenge being to use $\comfield$ of size $2^{128}$ for efficiency purpose. We remark that in \cref{s:experiments} we provide experimental results for $100$ bits of security.   Achieving $\lambda=100$ bits of security is relatively straightforward and we do not discuss it. We use a careful selection of parameters in combination with grinding. In \cref{r:beyond_gridning} we discuss other instantiations and techniques that do not require grinding.

The contents of this section should be taken as examples of how to instantiate \cref{c:core_iop,c:LinModFieldBin}, not as strict rules. Other configurations may be perfectly plausible and practical. 

\bigskip


\noindent In terms of security, we need to ensure the following:
\begin{enumerate}
       \item The round-by-round knowledge soundness errors of \cref{c:core_iop} $\kappa_{\mathsf{OOD}}$ (if Round 0 is executed), $\kappa_0$ (if Round 1 is executed), $(\ksred_i)_{i}$ and $(\ksiopp_i)_{i}$ are all at most $2^{-\lambda}$.
\end{enumerate}
%


\noindent In terms of efficiency, the most important step of \cref{c:core_iop} to be aware of is Phase 3, where one uses an IOR $\Pi_{\red}$ to reduce the claim 
\begin{equation}\label{e: grand_products_again}
\prod_{i \in [\codedim_1]} \left(\left(g^{\bm{\gamma}_i}-1\right)\cdot \pi_2(\bff_{ij}) +1\right)= g^{\mu_j}  \quad  \in \comfield, \qquad \text{for all } j\in [\codedim_2],
\end{equation}
into a linear claim on $\pi(\bff)$. As mentioned, in our instantiation, $\Pi_{\red}$ is the GKR protocol for multiple grand product claims,
%
equipped with the optimizations discussed in \cref{s:gkr_low_entropy}. This is the most expensive prover part of \cref{c:core_iop,c:LinModFieldBin}, constituting between $45\%$ ($\codedim=2^{20}$) and $85\%$ ($\codedim=2^{30}$) of prover costs excluding the commitment, cf.\ \cref{tab:f2z-raw-performance}. Because of this, it is important for efficiency that operating in $\comfield$ is as efficient as possible. 

With this in mind, we target using $\comfield$ with $\comfieldexp\approx 128$. A natural candidate is the $2^{128}$ sized binary field in GHASH representation  $$\comfield= \FF_2[X]/(X^{128} + X^7 + X^2 + X + 1).$$ 
%
%
Before anything else, we discuss the constraint  $\max\cP < \fieldboundsizeexponent$. 
%
First of all, we set $\codedim_1, \codedim_2$ with $\codedim_1\cdot \codedim_2 = \codedim$ and 
%
\begin{equation}\label{e:codedim_1_proportion}
\codedim_1 \in [\codedim^{0.5}, \codedim^{0.6}].
\end{equation}
%
In general, choosing $\codedim_1 \approx \codedim^{0.6}$ can lead to better prover time and proof size for the GKR grand product step, as observed in our experiments. However choosing $\codedim_1\approx \codedim^{0.5}$ allows to use slightly smaller soundness errors, which reduces grinding times if targeting $128$ bits of security.  


%
%To ground our analysis, we two witness bounds $\codedim\leq2^{35}$ (so the witness consists of $\leq 2^{35}$ bits), say, and $\codedim_1 \leq 2^{21}$ following our rule of thumb.   


We now discuss how to make the errors $\kappa_{\mathsf{OOD}}, \kappa_0, (\ksred_i)_{i}, (\ksiopp_i)_{i}$ be all at most $2^{-\lambda}$.



\paragraph{The errors $\kappa_{i,\iopp}$.} The ring switching step adds an error term of the form $7/|\comfield|$.  If targeting 128 bits of security, we use grinding  (\cref{s:IORs})  of $3$ bits in this round. The sumcheck before the ring switch adds similarly small error terms, which are handled with a few bits of grinding. WHIR is configured at any desired $\lambda\leq 128$ bits of security using $|\comfield|=2^{128}$ following \cite{stir-repo,whir-repo}. We do not discuss this further since WHIR is a well-known IOP, and it  can achieve $128$ bits of security (in the Unique Decoding Radius) in $128$ bit fields when codewords have dimension approximately $2^{30}$ assuming an interleaved RS code (to reduce the correlated agreement error) and the use of grinding when needed.


\paragraph{The error $\kappa_{\mathsf{OOD}}$.} As mentioned, we instantiate $\Pi_\iopp$  as WHIR with the ring-switching techniques of \cite{binius} and \cref{a:ring_switch_1}. For $\lambda=100$ we use a proximity parameter $\delta$ up to the Johnson bound. For $\lambda=128$ we set $\delta$ up to the unique decoding radius, so that the Out Of Domain sampling step (Round 0) is skipped. % hence Round 0 of  \cref{c:core_iop} is executed and contributes the error $\kappa_{\mathsf{OOD}}\leq \binom{L_\delta}{2}\cdot (\codedim/\comfieldexp-1)/|\comfield|$ (\cref{t:thm_core_IOPP}). For a Reed--Solomon code of rate $\rho$ and $\delta=1-\sqrt{\rho}-\eta$, we have the list size bound \albert{review} $L_\delta \leq 1/(2\eta\sqrt{\rho})$. We select $\eta=??$, which leads to an error $\kappa_{\mathsf{OOD}}\leq **$, for which, if needed, we can attain 128 bits of security by grinding $??$ bits \albertimportant{fill in the ?? numbers}. 

\paragraph{The errors $\kappa_{i,\red}$.} As mentioned, our $\Pi_{\red}$ IOR is the GKR protocol for multiple grand product claims from \cref{e: grand_products_again}, see \cref{s:gkr_low_entropy}. The first round of \cref{s:gkr_low_entropy} has soundness error $\log\codedim_2 / |\comfield|$. The rest of rounds are either a round of   sumcheck for a polynomial of degree $3$, or a unification of two evaluation claims of the same multilinear polynomial.  Accordingly we have $\ksred_i\leq 3/|\comfield|$ for all $i$, except $\ksred_1 \leq \log \codedim_2/|\comfield|$. This immediately guarantees $\lambda=100$ bits of security. To achieve $\lambda=128$ bits of security, one can do a small number of bits of grinding (\cref{s:IORs}) per round. 

\paragraph{The error $\kappa_0$.}
 The error $\kappa_0$ is perhaps the most delicate, as it demands at the same time that  $|\cP|$ is large, while still satisfying $\max\cP < \fieldboundsizeexponent$.
  
 Recall that in many scenarios $\kappa_0$ does not exist in the IOP  (i.e.\ sometimes our IOP skips Round 1). Concretely, if our initial inner product claim is over $R=\FF_q$ a prime field of size $< \fieldboundsizeexponent$, then  $\kappa_0$  does not appear as an error term, and we do not need to do anything to adjust for it. The term $\kappa_0$ does appear in all other scenarios ($R=\ZZ$ with large or negative entries in $\vv^{(1)}$  \albert{negative?} (cf.\ \cref{r:skip_round_1_integers}), or $R$ a prime field of larger size, or $R$ a proper field extension). We divide our approaches by whether this first step is skipped or not. 




\bigskip

\noindent For the purposes of this discussion, we assume \ftwoz \ is used within a bigger proof system, as described in \cref{s:to_snark}, so that \ftwoz \ is used to prove an inner product claim for a committed witness, as the last phase of a bigger proving system where \albert{review what the technical overview exactly says} 1) first the initial constraint is projected to a finite field $\FF$, or, alternatively, this step is skipped if the constraint is  already over a finite field; and 2) a  PIOP is run over $\FF$ for the projected constraint, outputting an inner product claim on the projected witness. Now, this inner product claim is the input of \cref{c:core_iop}, and we aim to configure \cref{c:core_iop} at 128 bits of security for this setting. 


\paragraph{Strategy 1: the PIOP achieves $128$ bits of security through grinding and we avoid the error term $\kappa_0$.} If allowing, say, $18-19$ bits of grinding during each round of the PIOP (which adds $0.8-1.8$ms per round, cf.\ \cref{tab:grinding-cost}), then, say, a Spartan-like PIOP will achieve $128$ bits of security if run on a field $\FF$ of size $\approx 2^{112}$ to  $2^{113}$  (since each one of its rounds is either a sumcheck round for a degree $\leq 3$ polynomial, or a step that batches some claims with error $1/|\FF|$). Hence, if the initial constraint is over $\ZZ$ or $\ZZ[X]$ \albert{do we mention $\ZZ[X]$? Why not mention other rings here? Again, wait for the TO}, then we may project it to a $112$-$113$ bit random prime field $\FF$ and run a PIOP over $\FF$. We study this case next. Our analysis also applies if the original constraint was already expressed over a prime field of such size.  

Note that the projection of the constraint to $\FF$ also requires to be set up to 128 bits of security. We discuss this in \cref{r:first_projection_security}. 




%in this case we may assume $R$  is a finite field of size $|R| \approx 2^{112}- 2^{113}$.  In this latter case, we do need to make sure that this projection step achieves 128 bits of security, while still being able to project to a $112$-$113$ bit field. We discuss this below in this section \cref{?} \albertimportant{do. Also it's a bit weird we discuss the error term of this step, which is a step described later in \cref{s:iop_upesat}}.


Let $q=|\FF|$. Then if
%
$$
q < \fieldboundsizeexponent,
$$
then $\kappa_0$ is not present as an error term, and there is nothing to adjust. Since $|\comfield|=2^{128}$  and $q\approx 2^{113}, 2^{112}$, this occurs as long as $\codedim_1 \lessapprox 2^{15},2^{16}$, and using \eqref{e:codedim_1_proportion}, as long as 
%
$$
\codedim\lessapprox 2^{25}, 2^{26} \qquad \text{if } \codedim_1\approx\codedim^{0.6}
$$
 and
$$
\codedim\lessapprox 2^{30}, 2^{32} \qquad \text{if } \codedim_1\approx\codedim^{0.5}.
$$
%
The specific upper bounds depend on how much grinding one is doing at the PIOP level. 

%Belo only analyse the scenario where $R$ is a finite field, and thus do not consider the case $R=\ZZ$, which is unlikely to occur in practice, unless \ftwoz\ is used in isolation, and not as a step subsequent to a PIOP. 





\paragraph{Strategy 2: No or essentially no grinding during the PIOP, and $\kappa_0$ is present and  grinding is used during the first projection step (Round 1) of \cref{c:core_iop}.} We now assume $\FF$ is either a prime field with  $|\FF|\geq \fieldboundsizeexponent$,  or a proper extension field. In this case  \cref{c:core_iop} goes through an initial projection step with soundness error  $\kappa_0$, i.e.\ Round 1 of \cref{c:core_iop}.

Recall (\cref{t:thm_core_IOPP}) that $\kappa_0 = \theta(\cP,\cM,\codedim)$. We use $\picanon: \ZZ[X]  \to \FF$ as in \cref{e:presentations}, so that $\picanon(h(X))= \pi_{\Char(\FF)}(h)(x)$ where $\pi_{\Char(\FF)}(h)$ is the polynomial $h$ after reducing its coefficients modulo $\Char(F)$, and  $x$ is a root of an irreducible polynomial defining $\FF_{q^d}$ (if $d=1$ and so $\FF_{q^d}$ is a prime field, then we can use a morphism $\picanon:\ZZ\to \FF_q$). We have $D=d-1$, $T\leq d$, and $N_\FF\leq q-1$ (see \eqref{e:ring_theta_params} and the text below it for a definition of these quantities). We instantiate the morphism sets $\cM_q$ as in \cref{l:examples_morphisms}, i.e.\ $\pirand$ reduces coefficients modulo a sampled prime and evaluates $X_1$ at a random $\alpha\in \FF_q$. In this case, by \cref{l:examples_morphisms},
    $$
    \kappa_0 \leq \frac{2\cdot (d-1)}{q_{\min}} + \frac{\log\left(2\cdot d\cdot \codedim\cdot (q-1)^2\right)}{|\cP|\cdot \lfloor\log(q_{\min})\rfloor},
      $$
    where $q$ is  the characteristic of $\FF$.

%
%
    We select $\cP$ to be the set of all primes in a range $[2^a, 2^b]$. There are approximately  $$|\cP|\approx (2^b/b - 2^a/a)/\log_e(2)$$ such primes.  Using $b= 128 - \tau \cdot \log(\codedim)$,  $\codedim_1= 2^{\tau\cdot \log(\codedim)}$,  for a parameter $\tau \in \range{0.5}{0.6}$ and plugging these values into $\kappa_0$ we obtain
     %
     \begin{equation}\label{e:kappa_0_practical_upper_bound}
     \begin{aligned}
    \kappa_0&\lessapprox \frac{2\cdot (d-1)}{2^a} + \frac{\log\left(2\cdot d\cdot \codedim\cdot (q-1)^2\right)\cdot \log_e(2)}{\frac{a}{b}\cdot 2^{b} - 2^a},\\
    b&\defeq 128 - r_1, \qquad r_1\defeq \tau\cdot \log(\codedim).
    \end{aligned}
    \end{equation}
    %
    We calculate several minima for the right hand side of \eqref{e:kappa_0_practical_upper_bound} when $\FF$ is a  field of size $\approx 2^{128}$ (not necessarily a prime field), for varying values of $\codedim$ and $\tau \in \{0.5, 0.6\}$. We include the corresponding grinding times in order to achieve $128$ bits of security for this initial step of \cref{c:core_iop}.  

    %We also include minima of $f(a)$ for other configurations where $R$ is a finite field of size between $2^{128}$ to $2^{256}$, not necessarily a prime field.
    
   \begin{table}[H]
  \centering
 \begin{tabular}{rrrrrrrrr}
    \toprule
    $\log |\FF|$ & $d$ & $\log q$ & $\log \codedim$ & $\tau$ & $a^{*}$ & $\kappa_0\leq$ & Bit-deficit to $128$ & Grinding time (ms) \\
    \midrule
    128 & 1 & 128 & 24 & 0.5 & 110 & $2^{-108.3}$ & 20 & 3.4 \\

    128 & 1 & 128 & 28 & 0.5 & 108 & $2^{-106.3}$ & 22 & 11.9 \\
    128 & 1 & 128 & 28 & 0.6 & 105 & $2^{-103.5}$ & 25 & 98.1 \\

By choosing primes in the interval $[2^{a^*}, 2^{128-r_1}]$, we have that the first error term $\kappa_0$ is bounded by the quantity in the $\kappa_0\leq$ column. We include the bits left required to achieve  $\kappa_0 < 1/2^{128}$ and the cost of grinding such amount of bits in a MacBook Air M5 24\,GB, multi-threaded, using Blake3 as hash function.}
