\begin{remark}[128 bits of security for larger witness sizes $\codedim$, without  or with minimal grinding]\label{r:beyond_gridning}
    Grinding costs scale exponentially in the number of grinding bits. Because of this, we cannot resort to grinding for very large witness sizes $\codedim$. There are at least two ways to achieve $128$ bits of security (or more), in this case.

    \medskip 
    
    \noindent \textbf{Method 1}: Use a larger binary field $\comfield$, i.e.\ simply take $\comfield$ with $|\comfield|$ comfortably larger, rather than $|\comfield|=2^{128}$.
 
 \medskip
 
 \noindent \textbf{Method 2}: We generalize an idea from \cite{binius64}. In this setting we make the assumption that the entries in $\bm{\gamma}$ (cf.\ Phase 3 of \cref{c:core_iop}) are smaller than $|\comfield|-1$. This means we require $\max\cP < |\comfield|-1$ instead of $\max\cP< \fieldboundsizeexponent$ (and similarly for the conditions that would allow to skip Round 1 in \cref{c:core_iop}).  Hence now the bound on the primes in $\cP$ does not depend on the witness size. 
 
 Note that when we attempt to prove the integer equalities $\langle \bm{\gamma}, \bff_{*j}\rangle =\mu_j$ ($j\in [\codedim_2]$)  ``in the exponent'' during Phase 3 of \cref{c:core_iop}, what we are really doing is proving the equalities modulo $|\comfield|-1$.  So far, we have used $\comfield$ large enough that this distinction does not matter, in that the total possible magnitude of $\langle \bm{\gamma}, \bff_{*j}\rangle$ does not exceed $|\comfield|-2$.  

    If that is not the case, one can, in addition to proving  $\langle \bm{\gamma}, \bff_{*j}\rangle =\mu_j$  modulo $|\comfield|-1$, prove the same equality modulo $\codedim_1$. This works because, since  $\codedim_1$ and $|\comfield|-1$ are coprime (because $\codedim_1$ is a power of two), by the CRT, if both equalities are true, then  $\langle\bm{\gamma}, \bff_{*j}\rangle -\mu_j$ is an integer multiple of $\codedim_1\cdot (|\comfield|-1)$. However, because the entries of $\bff$ are bits and the entries of $\bm{\gamma}$ are at most $|\comfield|-2$, this means that   $\langle \bm{\gamma}, \bff_{*j}\rangle = \mu_j$ as integers. 


    
    To prove that  $\langle\bm{\gamma}, \bff_{*j}\rangle =\mu_j $ modulo $\codedim_1$, one can prove the integer equality  $\langle \bm{\gamma}', \bff_{*j}\rangle = \mu_j'  +  a_j\cdot \codedim_1$ for some $a_j\in \ZZ$ (provided by the prover), where $\bm{\gamma}'$ contains the entries of $\bm{\gamma}$ reduced modulo $\codedim_1$, and $\mu_j'$ is $\mu_j$ reduced modulo $\codedim_1$.  The verifier checks that $a_j$ is not too large, namely it checks that $|a_j| \cdot \codedim_1 \leq (\codedim_1-1)\cdot \codedim_1$, which is the maximum value $|a_j| \cdot \codedim_1$ can achieve when $\bff$ is filled with bits (which is guaranteed by our commitment via WHIR). Hence the value $|\langle \bm{\gamma}', \bff_{*j}\rangle - \mu_j'  - a_j\cdot \codedim_1|$ can be at most $2\cdot (\codedim_1+1)\cdot \codedim_1$.  As long as $2\cdot (\codedim_1+1)\cdot \codedim_1 < |\comfield|-1$, this equality can be proved ``in the exponent'' without causing overflow. 
    
    
    Note that if $\bm{\gamma}$ admits a tensor decomposition   $\bm{\gamma}=\bm{\gamma}^{(1)} \otimes \bm{\gamma}^{(2)}$, then we can take, instead of $\bm{\gamma}'$, the tensor-product $\bm{\gamma}^{(1)}{}' \otimes \bm{\gamma}^{(2)}{}'$, where  $\bm{\gamma}^{(i)}{}'$ denotes $\bm{\gamma}^{(i)}$ reduced modulo $\codedim_1$. This allows to use all of our ``low-entropy'' grand product optimizations from \cref{s:gkr_low_entropy}, though in this case the required bound becomes $2\cdot (\codedim_1^2+1)\cdot \codedim_1 < |\comfield|-1$.%\albert{Point here to some explanation saying that this is the best way to prove such inner product?}

    Overall, this method allows to use $\max\cP < |\comfield|-1$, meaning we can choose primes that are $\log(\codedim_1)$ bits larger than we otherwise would. This makes soundness errors be smaller by this same amount of bits, which leads to small grinding requirements.  The cost is an extra batch of low entropy grand products. 
\end{remark}


\begin{remark}[Projecting polynomial constraints to a $112$-$113$ bit prime field with $128$ bits of security]\label{r:first_projection_security}
    The error term of the projection step (also called fingerprinting) in \cite{zincplus,zinc,zaratan} is (for integer constraints) bounded by (cf.\ \cref{l:reduction_lemma} with $d=0$)
    %
    $$
    \eps_{\mathsf{PIOP-proj}}\leq \frac{\log(N)}{|\cP'| \cdot \lfloor\log(q_{\min})\rfloor}
    $$
    where $\cP'$ is a set of primes; $q_{\min}$ is the smallest prime in $\cP'$; $N$ is the largest  absolute value that an unsatisfying constraint can attain. More precisely, assume integer witness entries $\ww$ are guaranteed to have at most certain bit-size $B'$, and assume the constraints have the form $q_i(\xx,\ww)=0$ for all $i$, for $\xx$ a public input vector and $q_i$ constraint polynomials. Then $N$ is the maximum possible value of the form $|q_i(\xx,\ww)|$ among all $i$, all possible inputs $\xx$ and all  $\ww$ with entries having bit-size at most $B'$. 
    
    Using a similar analysis as in Strategy 2 above, where we let $\cP'$ be the set of primes between the interval $[2^{112}, 2^{113}]$,  we obtain that, as long as $\log(N)< 1.4\cdot 2^{6}\approx 90$ we have $   \eps_{\mathsf{PIOP-proj}}< 2^{-106}$. In that case we could attain $128$ bits of security during this projection step by performing $22$ bits of grinding ($12$ms in a MacBook Air M5).
\end{remark}


\section{A GKR protocol for low entropy batched grand products }\label{s:gkr_low_entropy}






The bulk of the work in \ftwoz \ (\cref{c:core_iop}) is to reduce the claim over the binary field $\comfield$
\begin{equation}\label{e:main_grand_product}
\prod_{i \in [\codedim_1]} \left(\left(g^{\bm{\gamma}_i}-1\right)\cdot \pi_2(\bff_{ij}) +1\right)= g^{\mu_j}  \quad  \in \comfield, \qquad \text{for all } j\in [\codedim_2]
\end{equation}
to an inner product claim  of the form $\langle \vv, \pi_2(\bff)\rangle =\mu$ over $\comfield$, for some $\vv\in \comfield^{\codedim}$, where here $\bff\in \bits^\codedim \subseteq \ZZ^\codedim$.  Recall that $$\codedim=\codedim_1\cdot \codedim_2.$$ To fix intuition,  in our instantiation (cf.\ \cref{s:instantiation}) we set $\codedim_1 \approx \codedim^{0.6}, \codedim_2=\codedim^{0.4}$. %For the purposes of this reduction, it is irrelevant that $\bff$ is defined over the integers: we will only consider the vector $\pi_2(\bff)$.


\renewcommand{\comfield}{\KK}


Cryptographically speaking, our protocol is simply a standard batched version of the GKR protocol applied to grand products from \cite{?}. However, our implementation uses lookup tables (i.e.\ preprocessed data) to optimize the prover time of such protocol. In particular, our techniques leverage the fact that the factors in \eqref{e:main_grand_product} have \emph{low entropy}, in the sense that for every $i\in [\codedim_1]$ and $j\in [\codedim_2]$, the $i$-th factor in the $j$-th grand product of \eqref{e:main_grand_product} can take only two values, depending on whether $\bff_{ij}=1$ or $\bff_{ij}=0$.  



 The optimizations we introduce apply to any (batched) grand product with low entropy factors, and not only to the grand product in \eqref{e:main_grand_product}. They also work on any field. Because of this, we  describe a general protocol IOR  that, given a finite field $\KK$;  a bivariate polynomial $\gkrpol\in \KK[Y,X]$; %a code $\cC$ and map $T$ as in \cref{?}\footnote{The code $\cC$ and map $T$ are  not used throughout the entirety of the IOR.}; 
 a small subset $S\subseteq \KK$; and dimensions $\codedim=\codedim_1\cdot \codedim_2$, reduces triples from 
%
\begin{align*}
&\PESAT(\comfield, \gkrpol)\\ &\qquad = \left\{ \left(\begin{aligned} &\inp=(\yy,\bm{\mu})\in \comfield^{\codedim_1}\times \comfield^{\codedim_2}, \\
&\oracle=\bot,\\
%&\oracle = f\in \KK^{\codelength},\\ 
&\wit=\bff= (\bff_{*j})_{j\in [\codedim_2]}\in S^{\codedim_1 \times \codedim_2} \subseteq \KK^{\codedim_1 \times \codedim_2}\end{aligned}\right) \;\middle|\;  \begin{aligned}   % &f =\Enc_{\cC}\circ T(\bff)\\ 
&\prod_{i\in[\codedim_1]} \gkrpol(\yy_i, \bff_{ij}) = \bm{\mu}_j \ \text{ for all } j\in [\codedim_2]\end{aligned}  \right\},
\end{align*}
%
to triples in 
%
$$
\LIN( \comfield)  = \left\{ \left(\begin{aligned} &\inp=(\uu,\mu)\in \comfield^\codedim \times \comfield, \\
&\oracle=\bot,\\
%&\oracle = f\in \comfield^{\codelength}, \\ 
&\wit= \bff\in S^{\codedim_1\times \codedim_2}\subseteq \KK^{\codedim_1 \times \codedim_2}\end{aligned}\right) \;\middle|\;  \begin{aligned} 
%&f=\Enc_{\cC}\circ T (\bff) \\   
&\langle \uu, \bff\rangle = \mu  \end{aligned}\right\}.
$$
%
One can additionally add an encoding $f=\Enc_\cC(\bff)$ as an oracle  in the above relations (matching the context in which we use the IOR), but our IOR does not make any use of it whatsoever, hence we omit it. 

In our instantiation, $\yy_i=g^{\bm{\gamma}_i}$ and $\bm{\mu}_j=g^{\mu_j}$, where $\bm{\gamma}_i$ and $\mu_j$ are the integers in \eqref{e:main_grand_product}.

We  describe $\Pi_{\red}$ now. We will describe our implementation-level optimizations afterwards. As we mentioned, cryptographically/protocol-wise, our protocol is a standard batched GKR for grand products \cite{thalerbook}.  We assume $\codedim_1,\codedim_2$ are powers of two:
%
$$
\codedim_1 = 2^{r_1}, \quad \codedim_2 = 2^{r_2}.
$$
We index $[\codedim_1],[\codedim_2]$ via elements of the hypercubes $\bits^{r_1}, \bits^{r_2}$, respectively.
%
\begin{construction}[An IOR $\Pi_\red$ from $\PESAT(\comfield, \gkrpol)$ to $\LIN( \comfield)$]\label{c:gkr_grand_product}
\noindent \textbf{Input.}   The prover and verifier receive $(\yy,\bm{\mu})\in \comfield^{\codedim_1} \times  \comfield^{\codedim_2}$. The prover receives also $\bff\in S^{\codedim_1\times \codedim_2}\subseteq\comfield^{\codedim_1\times \codedim_2}$.


Let $L_{r_1}$ be a  \emph{multilinear} polynomial over $\comfield$ on $r_1 + r_2$ variables such that $$L_{r_1}(\bb,\cc)= \gkrpol(\yy_{\bb}, \bff_{\bb,\cc}) \quad  \text{ for all } (\bb,\cc) \in \bits^{r_1} \times \bits^{r_2}.$$ (note that $\gkrpol$ may not be multilinear).  
     For $i=0,\ldots, r_1-1$, let $L_i$ be a multilinear polynomial on $r_2 + i$ variables  such that 
      $$
  L_{i}(\bb,\cc)  =  L_{i+1}(\bb,0,\cc) \cdot L_{i+1}(\bb,1, \cc)  \quad \text{for all } (\bb,\cc)\in \bits^{i} \times \bits^{r_2}.
    $$
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
    \item The verifier sends $\bm{\alpha}^{(0)}\in \comfield^{r_2}$. Both prover and verifier compute $C^{(0)}\defeq 
    L_0(\bm{\alpha}^{(0)})$ (note that $L_0$ is the multilinear extension of $\bm{\mu}$ over $\comfield$). 
    \item  For $i=0,\ldots, r_1-1$:
    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
        \item \textbf{$i$-th sumcheck} The prover and verifier run the sumcheck protocol for the following claim: %\john{FYI: The current implementation constructs two sumchecks. One for b variables. A subsequent one for the c variables. It might be good to explore the difference between the two} \albert{I think it's the same. Any sumcheck with at least two rounds is a composition of two smaller sumchecks, that's what's happening in the implementation essentially. Maybe we should modify the implementation to avoid confusion}
    %
    $$
    C^{(i)} =  \sum_{(\bb,\cc)\in \bits^i \times \bits^{r_2}}  L_{i+1}(\bb,0,\cc) \cdot L_{i+1}(\bb,1,\cc) \cdot \eq(\bb, \cc; \bm{\alpha}^{(i)}). 
    $$
    %
    At the end of the sumcheck protocol, the prover and verifier are left with a claim of the form
    %
    \begin{equation}\label{e:gkr_intermediate}
    L_{i+1}(\rr_\bb^{(i)},0,\rr_{\cc}^{(i)}) = a_{0}^{(i)}, \qquad L_{i+1}(\rr_\bb^{(i)},1,\rr_{\cc}^{(i)}) = a_{1}^{(i)}
    \end{equation}
    %
    for some $\rr_\bb^{(i)}\in \comfield^{i}$, $\rr_\cc^{(i)}\in \comfield^{r_2}$ and $a_{0}^{(i)},a_{1}^{(i)}\in \comfield$.
    %
    \item \textbf{$i$-th unification step} The prover and verifier unify the two claims from \eqref{e:gkr_intermediate} into a single one via the following standard procedure:
    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
        \item The verifier sends a random element $\beta^{(i)}\in\KK$.
        \item The claim \eqref{e:gkr_intermediate} is replaced by
        %
        $$
        L_{i+1}(\rr_\bb^{(i)},\beta^{(i)},\rr_{\cc}^{(i)}) = C^{(i+1)} \defeq a_0^{(i)} \cdot (1-\beta^{(i)}) + a_1^{(i)} \cdot \beta^{(i)}.
        $$
        Let 
        $$
        \bm{\alpha}^{(i+1)} \defeq (\rr_\bb^{(i)},\beta^{(i)},\rr_{\cc}^{(i)}).
        $$
    \end{enumerate}
    %
    \end{enumerate}
    \item At this stage, the prover and verifier are left with the claim
    %
    $$
    L_{r_1}(\bm{\alpha}^{(r_1)}) = C^{(r_1)}.
    $$
    This claim is equivalent to
    %
    \begin{equation}\label{e:gkr_leaf_claim}
    \sum_{(\bb,\cc)\in \bits^{r_1} \times \bits^{r_2}} \gkrpol(\yy_{\bb},\bff_{\bb,\cc}) \cdot \eq((\bb,\cc), \bm{\alpha}^{(r_1)}) = C^{(r_1)},
    \end{equation}
    If $\gkrpol(Y,X)$ is linear on $X$, then \eqref{e:gkr_leaf_claim} is a linear claim on $\bff$. Namely, it is equivalent to
    %
    $$
    \langle \uu, \bff \rangle = C^{(r_1)} + c
    $$
    for some $\uu\in \KK^{\codedim}, c\in \KK$. In this case, prover and verifier output 
    %
        $$
    (\inp'=(\uu, C^{(r_1)}+c),\ \bot, \ \wit' = \bff) \in \LIN(\comfield).
    $$
    %
    Otherwise, i.e.\ if $\gkrpol$ is not linear in $X$, prover and verifier run a sumcheck for the claim \eqref{e:gkr_leaf_claim}, which results in MLE claims for $\yy$ and $\bff$. The verifier checks the claim for $\yy$ itself, and prover and verifier output the MLE claim for $\bff$.

    Below we discuss what $\gkrpol$, $\uu$ and $C^{(r_1)}+c$ are specifically in our usage of $\Pi_\red$ as a subprotocol of \cref{c:core_iop,c:LinModFieldBin}, and how to keep the verifier succinct.
    %
\end{enumerate}
    
\end{construction}

\paragraph{Using $\Pi_\red$ in \ftwoz \ and succinctness of the verifier. }

If $\gkrpol(\yy_\bb, \bff_{\bb,\cc}) = (\yy_\bb-1)\cdot \bff_{\bb,\cc} +1$ as in \cref{c:core_iop,c:LinModFieldBin}, then $$\uu=((\yy_\bb - 1)\cdot \eq(\bb;\bm\alpha^{(r_1,1)}))_{\bb\in \bits^{r_1}} \otimes (\eq(\cc;\bm\alpha^{(r_1,2)}))_{\cc\in \bits^{r_2}}, \quad C^{(r_1)} + c =  C^{(r_1)} -1,$$  where $\bm{\alpha}^{(r_1)} = (\bm{\alpha}^{(r_1,1)}, \bm{\alpha}^{(r_1,2)})$.
    In this case, to avoid having the verifier pay a linear cost, the verifier constructs $\uu^{(1)}\defeq ((\yy_\bb - 1)\cdot\eq(\bb;\bm\alpha^{(r_1,1)}))_{\bb\in \bits^{r_1}} $ and $\uu^{(2)}\defeq (\eq(\cc;\bm\alpha^{(r_1,2)}))_{\cc\in \bits^{r_2}}$ separately, and outputs these two vectors instead of $\uu$.
    
     When used as a subprotocol of \cref{c:core_iop,c:LinModFieldBin} (concretely, in Phase 3 of \cref{c:core_iop}), after executing the GKR protocol above (\cref{c:gkr_grand_product}), we use an IOP $\Pi_\iopp$ to prove the linear claim $\langle \uu, \pi_2(\bff)\rangle= C^{(r_1)}-1$ (if using \cref{c:LinModFieldBin},  $\bff$ is replaced with $\bitstext(\bff)$), with the verifier having oracle access to $\Enc_\cC \circ \pack \circ \pi_2(\bff)$. As we mentioned (\cref{s:instantiation}), $\Pi_{\iopp}$ first runs a   sumcheck \albert{Remark somewhere that this sumcheck is avoidable}, reducing the claim $\langle \uu, \pi_2(\bff)\rangle= C^{(r_1)}-1$  to an MLE evaluation claim  of the vector $\pi_2(\bff)$ and of $\uu$. For the latter, we have, letting $\bm{\theta}=(\bm{\theta}^{(1)}, \bm{\theta}^{(2)})\in \KK^{r_1+r_2}$ be the evaluation point,  \albert{check} \begin{align*}\mle{\uu}(\bm{\theta})&=\mle{\uu^{(1)} \otimes \uu^{(2)}}(\bm{\theta}^{(1)}, \bm{\theta}^{(2)}) = \mle{\uu^{(1)}}(\bm{\theta}^{(1)}) \cdot \mle{\uu^{(2)}}(\bm{\theta}^{(2)}),\\  
     \mle{\uu^{(1)}}(\bm{\theta}^{(1)})&=     \sum_{\bb\in\bits^{r_1}} (\yy_\bb-1)\cdot\eq(\bb;\bm\alpha^{(r_1,1)})\cdot\eq(\bb;\bm{\theta}^{(1)}),\\
     \mle{\uu^{(2)}}(\bm{\theta}^{(2)})& =  \eq(\bm{\theta}^{(2)};\bm\alpha^{(r_1,2)}),
     \end{align*} and so the verifier can compute $\mle{\uu}(\bm{\theta})$ by itself in time $O(\codedim_1 +\codedim_2)$. For the MLE claim on $\pi_2(\bff)$, we first use ring-switching \cite{binius} (\cref{a:ring_switch_1}) to reduce the claim to a batch of linear claims for $\pack\circ \pi_2(\bff)$, and then prove these with WHIR/Ligerito.   


\paragraph{Costs.} In \cref{t:gkr_grand_product_complexity} we summarize the number of rounds, the proof size and the verifier work of \cref{c:gkr_grand_product} when used within \ftwoz \ as described above.  These are standard costs corresponding to a standard GKR construction. We discuss an optimized prover, and its costs, in the next subsection. Recall that in our instantiations $\codedim_1\approx \sqrt{\codedim}$ and $\codedim_2\approx\sqrt{\codedim}$, hence the verifier is succinct.

\begin{table}[H]
  \centering
  \small
  \begin{tabular}{l|l|l|l}
    \toprule
     & Rounds & Proof (elements of $\comfield$) & Verifier \\
    \hline
    Step 1 (batching) & $1$ & -- & $O(\codedim_2)\cdot \comfield$ \\
    \hline
    Step 2, $i$-th sumcheck & $i+r_2$ & $3\cdot (i+r_2)$ & $O(i+r_2)\cdot \comfield$ \\
    \hline
    Step 2, $i$-th  unification & $1$ & $2$ & $O(1)\cdot \comfield$ \\
    \hline
    Computing $\uu$ & --    & -- & $O(\codedim_1 + \codedim_2)\cdot \comfield$ (only $\uu^{(1)}, \uu^{(2)}$)\\
    \hline
    \textbf{Total} & $t_\red = 1 + r_1\cdot r_2 + \binom{r_1+1}{2}$ & $3\cdot t_\red - r_1 - 3$ & $O(\codedim_1 + \codedim_2 + \log^2 \codedim)\cdot \comfield$ \\
    \bottomrule
  \end{tabular}
  \caption{ Rounds, proof size and verifier work of \cref{c:gkr_grand_product} for the polynomial $\gkrpol(Y,X)=(Y-1)\cdot X +1$ in our deployment of \ftwoz.  By $N\cdot \comfield$ we indicate $N$ operations in $\comfield$. The proof size counts $3$ elements per sumcheck round. Using  \cite{gruen} we send two $2$ elements per sumcheck round in our implementation.}
  \label{t:gkr_grand_product_complexity}
\end{table}

The next lemma records the soundness errors of \cref{c:gkr_grand_product}. We omit its proof since \cref{c:gkr_grand_product} is a well-known variation of the GKR protocol, see \cite{thalerbook}.

\begin{lemma}[Soundness and completeness of \cref{c:gkr_grand_product}]\label{l:gkr_grand_product}
 Assume $\gkrpol(Y,X)$ is linear in $X$\footnote{We focus on this case since it is the one in our instantiation. A generalization to arbitrary polynomials $\gkrpol(Y,X)$ would  add rounds  and error terms corresponding to the sumcheck protocol mentioned at the end of \cref{c:gkr_grand_product}.}. Then \cref{c:gkr_grand_product} is a perfectly complete
$(1 + r_1 r_2 + r_1(r_1+1)/2)$-round IOR from $\PESAT(\comfield,\gkrpol)$ to $\LIN(\comfield)$, whose verifier makes no oracle queries and whose prover outputs the witness $\bff$ it received. Accordingly it also extends to an IOR from $\PESAT(\comfield,\gkrpol, \cC,\pack)$ to $\LIN(\comfield, \cC,\pack)$ in a natural way (for $S=\bits$; these are the same relations as $\PESAT(\comfield,\gkrpol)$ and $\LIN(\comfield)$, but with an additional oracle $\Enc_\cC\circ \pack(\bff)$). For every $\delta$ within the unique decoding radius of $\cC$, this latter IOR has round-by-round knowledge soundness with relaxation $(\PESAT(\comfield,\gkrpol,\cC,\pack)^\delta,\LIN(\comfield,\cC,\pack)^{\delta})$ with errors $r_2/|\comfield|$ in the
first round, $3/|\comfield|$ in every sumcheck round and $1/|\comfield|$ in every unification step.
%so given $\yy$ the verifier performs $O(\codedim_1+\codedim_2+r_1 r_2)$ field operations.
\end{lemma}

\subsection{Prover optimization via lookup tables} \albert{Mention somewhere that the optimization has something to do with the method of four russians?} We optimize \cref{c:gkr_grand_product} as follows.  For simplicity we use the subset
%
$$
S=\bits \subseteq \KK,
$$
but any small subset of $\KK$ is amenable to the techniques discussed here.

%\john{Typing is a bit inconsistent here for $c$ and $f_{b,c}$. Namely, we say c seems to be a boolean vector but $f_{b,c}$ is not } \albert{We mentioned above that we are indexing $[\codedim_1]$ and $[\codedim_2]$ with hypercube elements. Does this resolve the question?}
\paragraph{Setup} We start with the following observation: for every $(\bb,\cc)\in \bits^{r_1} \times \bits^{r_2}$, we have $\bff_{\bb,\cc}\in S=\bits$, and so 
%
$$L_{r_1}(\bb,\cc)=\gkrpol(\yy_{\bb}, \bff_{\bb,\cc})= 
\begin{cases}
    \yy_{\bb}^{(1)}\defeq \gkrpol(\yy_{\bb},1) & \text{if } \bff_{\bb,\cc}=1\\
     \yy_{\bb}^{(0)}\defeq \gkrpol(\yy_{\bb},0) & \text{if } \bff_{\bb,\cc}=0.
\end{cases}$$
%
The values $\yy_{\bb}^{(0)}, \yy_{\bb}^{(1)}$   do not depend on $\cc$. Hence the prover can set up a lookup table $T_{r_1}\in (\comfield\times \comfield)^{\bits^{r_1}}$ defined as
%
$$
T_{r_1}[\bb]= (\yy_{\bb}^{(0)}, \yy_{\bb}^{(1)}) \qquad \text{for all } \bb \in \bits^{r_1}
$$
at the beginning of the protocol, and, whenever required, use it to obtain the values $L_{r_1}(\bb,\cc)$. Concretely
%
$$
L_{r_1}(\bb,\cc)=T_{r_1}[\bb][\bff_{\bb,\cc}] \quad \text{for all } (\bb,\cc)\in \bits^{r_1}\times \bits^{r_2}
$$
%Since $\yy$ is part of the input, this is essentially a no-op 
In our instantiation, $\yy_{\bb}^{(1)}=g^{\bm{\gamma}_{\bb}}$ and $\yy_\bb^{(0)}=1$ (cf.\ \eqref{e:main_grand_product}). Hence additionally the prover precomputes the elements $g^{\bm{\gamma}_{\bb}}$ at the beginning of the protocol, before computing $T_{r_1}$.



As we will explain later, the prover will also precompute similar tables for the values $L_{r_1-j}(\bb,\cc)$ of the levels close to the leaves (i.e.\ for small $j$),
\begin{equation}\label{e:size_T_r1_i}T_{r_1-j}\in \left(\comfield^{2^{2^j}}\right)^{\bits^{r_1-j}},\end{equation}
as well as other tables relevant for the different sumcheck rounds of \cref{c:gkr_grand_product}, constructed from the tables $T_{r_1-j}$ themselves. The superindex $\bits^{r_1-j}$ in \eqref{e:size_T_r1_i} indicates that the table $T_{r_1-j}$ has one entry per element in the hypercube  $\bits^{r_1-j}$; and this entry is a vector of $2^{2^j}$ field elements. Concretely, for $\bb\in \bits^{r_1-j}$, $T_{r_1-j}[\bb]$ consists of all possible products of  $2^j$ consecutive factors of the grand product, namely the factors indexed by $(\bb, \bb')$ with $\bb$ fixed and $\bb'$ ranging over $\bits^{j}$.  Each factor can take two values, depending on whether $\bff_{\bb,\bb', \cc}$ is $1$ or $0$. Hence $T_{r_1-j}[\bb]$ consists of $2^{2^j}$ elements. Hence the size of $T_{r_1-j}$ in \eqref{e:size_T_r1_i}.

Because $|T_{r_1-j}|$   grows doubly-exponentially in $j$, these tables are viable only for the bottom few levels of the tree ($j=0,1,2$ in our implementation). In higher levels we use standard sumchecks.



%Indeed, a node $i$ levels above the leaves is the product of the $2^i$ leaves below it, so its value is one of $2^{2^i}$ position-indexed constants, selected by $2^i$ entries of $\bff_{*j}$ and independent of $j\in [\codedim_2]$. 

Importantly, all these tables have size independent of $\codedim_2$.  This (among others, cf.\ \cref{s:why_the_tensor_split}), is one of the reasons why we use the tensor decomposition structure, i.e.\ the split given by $\codedim=\codedim_1\cdot \codedim_2$, in \cref{c:core_iop}.

\begin{remark}
    All precomputed data in this section depends on the input vector $\yy$ of $\PESAT(\comfield,\gkrpol)$, and possibly on further verifier challenges. Since, in a wider context where \cref{c:gkr_grand_product} is a subprocess of a larger proof system, $\yy$ typically depends on verifier's challenges, these precomputations must be done at \emph{runtime}. 
\end{remark}





\paragraph{First prover message in the last sumcheck in Step 2 of \cref{c:gkr_grand_product}}
We start by analyzing Step 2 with $i=r_1-1$. In this step prover and verifier run a sumcheck for the claim
%
$$
C^{(r_1-1)}= \sum_{(\bb,\cc)\in \bits^{r_1-1}\times \bits^{r_2}} L_{r_1}(\bb,0,\cc) \cdot L_{r_1}(\bb,1,\cc) \cdot \eq(\bb,\cc;\bm{\alpha}^{(i)})
$$
%
This is the most expensive sumcheck of \cref{c:gkr_grand_product} for the prover (if done naively), since it is the sumcheck over the largest number of variables throughout \cref{c:gkr_grand_product}. 

For $1\leq j\leq r_1-1$, the $j$-th prover message in this sumcheck is, if $\bm{\rho}^{(j)}=(\rho_1, \ldots, \rho_{j-1})$ is the vector of verifier challenges so far in this sumcheck,
%
\begin{align*}
h_j(X)&\defeq \sum_{(\bb,\cc)\in \bits^{r_1-1-j}\times \bits^{r_2}} L_{r_1}(\bm{\rho}^{(j)}, X, \bb,0,\cc) \cdot L_{r_1}(\bm{\rho}^{(j)}, X, \bb,1,\cc)\cdot \eq(\bm{\rho}^{(j)}, X, \bb,\cc;\bm{\alpha}^{(i)})\\
&= \sum_{\cc\in \bits^{r_2}}\left( \underbrace{\sum_{\bb\in \bits^{r_1-1-j}}L_{r_1}(\bm{\rho}^{(j)}, X, \bb,0,\cc) \cdot L_{r_1}(\bm{\rho}^{(j)}, X, \bb,1,\cc) \cdot \eq(\bm{\rho}^{(j)}, \bb;\bm{\alpha}^{(i,1)})}_{\defeq h_{j,\cc}(X)}\right)\cdot   \eq(X,\cc;\bm{\alpha}^{(i,2)}) \\
&= \sum_{\cc\in \bits^{r_2}}h_{j,\cc}(X)\cdot   \eq(X,\cc;\bm{\alpha}^{(i,2)}),
\end{align*}
where $\bm{\alpha}^{(i)} = (\bm{\alpha}^{(i,1)}, \bm{\alpha}^{(i,2)})\in \KK^{r_1-2}\times \KK^{r_2+1}$.
%
We claim that
%
\begin{equation}\label{e:grand_product_Lr1}
L_{r_1}(\bm{\rho}^{(j)}, X, \bb,0,\cc) = \left(L_{r_1}(\bm{\rho}^{(j)}, 1, \bb,0,\cc) -  L_{r_1}(\bm{\rho}^{(j)}, 0, \bb,0,\cc)\right)\cdot X + L_{r_1}(\bm{\rho}^{(j)}, 0, \bb,0,\cc)
\end{equation}
Indeed, $L_{r_1}(\bm{\rho}^{(j)}, X, \bb,0,\cc)$ is a linear polynomial, and the right-hand side in the above expression is the line passing through the two evaluations of  $L_{r_1}(\bm{\rho}^{(j)}, X, \bb,0,\cc)$ at $X=0$ and $X=1$. An analogous expression holds for $L_{r_1}(\bm{\rho}^{(j)}, X, \bb, 1, \cc)$:
\begin{equation}\label{e:grand_product_Lr2}
L_{r_1}(\bm{\rho}^{(j)}, X, \bb,1,\cc) = \left(L_{r_1}(\bm{\rho}^{(j)}, 1, \bb,1,\cc) -  L_{r_1}(\bm{\rho}^{(j)}, 0, \bb,1,\cc)\right)\cdot X + L_{r_1}(\bm{\rho}^{(j)}, 0, \bb,1,\cc).
\end{equation}
%
Renaming
%
$$
E_{\eps_1\eps_2}^{(j)}[\bb,\cc] \defeq L_{r_1}(\bm{\rho}^{(j)}, \eps_1, \bb, \eps_2,\cc) \qquad \eps_1,\eps_2\in \bits, \qquad \omega[\bb]\defeq \eq(\bm{\rho}^{(j)}, \bb;\bm{\alpha}^{(i,1)}),
$$
we obtain
%
$$
h_{j,\cc}(X) =\sum_{\bb\in \bits^{r_1-1-j}} X^2 \cdot A_2^{(j)}[\bb,\cc] + X\cdot A_1^{(j)}[\bb,\cc] + A_0^{(j)}[\bb,\cc]
$$
 where,
$$
\begin{aligned}
A_0^{(j)}[\bb,\cc] &\defeq E_{00}^{(j)}[\bb,\cc]\cdot E_{01}^{(j)} [\bb,\cc]\cdot \omega[\bb],\\ 
A_1^{(j)}[\bb,\cc] &\defeq  \left((E_{10}^{(j)}[\bb,\cc]-E_{00}^{(j)}[\bb,\cc])\cdot E_{01}^{(j)}[\bb,\cc] + (E_{11}^{(j)}[\bb,\cc]-E_{01}^{(j)}[\bb,\cc])\cdot E_{00}^{(j)}[\bb,\cc]\right)\cdot \omega[\bb]\\
A_2^{(j)}[\bb,\cc] &\defeq (E_{10}^{(j)}[\bb,\cc]-E_{00}^{(j)}[\bb,\cc])\cdot (E_{11}^{(j)}[\bb,\cc]-E_{01}^{(j)}[\bb,\cc])\cdot \omega[\bb].
\end{aligned}
$$
%
Now, for $j=1$, we have $\bm{\rho}^{(j)}= \bot$, and hence each value $E_{\eps_1\eps_2}^{(1)}[\bb,\cc]$ can be obtained, whenever needed, by querying $T_{r_1}[\bb]$ and selecting $T_{r_1}[\bb][1]$ or $T_{r_1}[\bb][0]$  depending on whether $\bff_{\bb,\cc} = 1$ or $0$. A consequence  is that  the coefficients themselves $A_k^{(1)}[\bb,\cc]$ ($k\in [3]$) can be obtained through lookup tables, as follows:
%
 we have $$A_0^{(1)}[\bb,\cc] = 
    T_{r_1}[0,\bb,0][\bff_{(0,\bb,0),\cc}] \cdot    T_{r_1}[0,\bb, 1][\bff_{(0,\bb,1),\cc}] \cdot \omega[\bb]
$$
Hence
%
$$
A_0^{(1)}[\bb,\cc] = \begin{cases}
    \yy_{0,\bb,0}^{(1)} \cdot  \yy_{0,\bb,1}^{(1)} \cdot \omega[\bb]&\text{if } \bff_{(0,\bb,0),\cc}=1,\ \bff_{(0,\bb,1),\cc}=1 \\  
    \yy_{0,\bb,0}^{(1)} \cdot  \yy_{0,\bb,1}^{(0)}\cdot \omega[\bb]&\text{if } \bff_{(0,\bb,0),\cc}=1,\ \bff_{(0,\bb,1),\cc}=0\\
     \yy_{0,\bb,0}^{(0)} \cdot  \yy_{0,\bb,1}^{(1)}\cdot \omega[\bb]& \text{if } \bff_{(0,\bb,0),\cc}=0,\ \bff_{(0,\bb,1),\cc}=1 \\
    \yy_{0,\bb,0}^{(0)} \cdot  \yy_{0,\bb,1}^{(0)} \cdot  \omega[\bb] &\text{if } \bff_{(0,\bb,0),\cc}= 0,\  \bff_{(0,\bb,1),\cc}=0
\end{cases}
$$
%
Thus the prover can precompute the table $$T_{A_0} \in (\comfield^{4})^{\bits^{r_1-2}}=\comfield^{\codedim_1}$$ given by $$T_{A_0}[\bb]=(\yy_{0,\bb,0}^{(1)} \cdot  \yy_{0,\bb,1}^{(1)} \cdot \omega[\bb],  \ \
    \yy_{0,\bb,0}^{(1)} \cdot  \yy_{0,\bb,1}^{(0)}\cdot \omega[\bb],   \ \ \yy_{0,\bb,0}^{(0)} \cdot  \yy_{0,\bb,1}^{(1)}\cdot \omega[\bb],   \ \ \yy_{0,\bb,0}^{(0)} \cdot  \yy_{0,\bb,1}^{(0)} \cdot  \omega[\bb])$$
and use it to obtain any value $A_{0}^{(1)}[\bb,\cc]$ for $(\bb,\cc)\in \bits^{r_1-2}\times \bits^{r_2}$ via a single read into $T_{A_0}$. %\begin{equation}\label{e: A0_factor_T0}A_0[\bb,\cc] = T_{A_0}[\bb,\cc] \cdot \eq(\cc;\bm{\alpha}^{(i,2)}).\end{equation} 
%
The size of $T_{A_0}$, again, does not depend on $\codedim_2=2^{r_2}$. Building $T_{A_0}$ requires at most $8\cdot 2^{r_1-2} = 2^{r_1+1}$ multiplications and no additions. 

We now explain how to obtain the values $A_1^{(1)}[\bb,\cc]$ and $A_2^{(1)}[\bb,\cc]$, again via the construction of a lookup table. Note that these depend on the \emph{four} bits $\bff_{(\eps_1,\bb,\eps_2),\cc}$, $\eps_1,\eps_2\in \bits$, so our tables will need $2^4=16$ entries (as opposed to $4$ as in $T_{A_0})$ per  index $\bb$.
The prover  proceeds as follows:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
    \item It computes a table $T_{S_1}\in (\comfield^4)^{\bits^{r_1-2}}$ analogous to $T_{A_0}$, which allows to obtain the values 
    $$S_1^{(1)}[\bb,\cc] \defeq E_{10}^{(1)}[\bb,\cc]\cdot E_{11}^{(1)}[\bb,\cc]\cdot \omega[\bb],$$
    (observe that $T_{A_0}$ allows to obtain the values $E_{00}^{(1)}[\bb,\cc]\cdot E_{01}^{(1)}[\bb,\cc]\cdot \omega[\bb]$).
    \item It computes the table $T_{A_2}\in (\comfield^{16})^{\bits^{r_1-2}}$ given by
    \begin{equation}\label{e:TA2}T_{A_2}[\bb]=\Big( \big(\yy_{1,\bb,0}^{(x_1)}-\yy_{0,\bb,0}^{(x_0)}\big)\cdot \big(\yy_{1,\bb,1}^{(x_1')}-\yy_{0,\bb,1}^{(x_0')}\big)\cdot \omega[\bb] \Big)_{(x_0,x_1,x_0',x_1')\in \bits^4},\end{equation}
    so that $A_2^{(1)}[\bb,\cc]$ is the element  $$A_2^{(1)}[\bb,\cc]=T_{A_2}[\bb][\bff_{(0,\bb,0),\cc},\bff_{(1,\bb,0),\cc},\bff_{(0,\bb,1),\cc},\bff_{(1,\bb,1),\cc}].$$ Building $T_{A_2}$ takes at most $20$ multiplications and $8$ additions per index $\bb$\footnote{For the multiplications, there are $4$ multiplications to compute the $4$ elements $t_{x_0',x_1',\bb}\defeq \big(\yy_{1,\bb,1}^{(x_1')}-\yy_{0,\bb,1}^{(x_0')}\big)\cdot \omega[\bb]$, and then $16$ more multiplications to compute $\big(\yy_{1,\bb,0}^{(x_1)}-\yy_{0,\bb,0}^{(x_0)}\big)\cdot t_{x_0',x_1',\bb}$}. I.e.\ at most $5\cdot 2^{r_1}$ multiplications and $2^{r_1+1}$ additions in total. \albert{Discuss cost of other tables?}
    \item It obtains the values $A_1^{(1)}[\bb,\cc]$ via the identity $$S_1^{(1)}[\bb,\cc] = A_0^{(1)}[\bb,\cc]+A_1^{(1)}[\bb,\cc]+A_2^{(1)}[\bb,\cc] \qquad \text{for all } (\bb,\cc)\in \bits^{r_1-2}\times \bits^{r_2}.$$ %so the prover accumulates the three sums $\sum_\bb A_0^{(1)}, \sum_\bb S_1^{(1)}, \sum_\bb A_2^{(1)}$ over the slots and recovers $\sum_\bb A_1^{(1)}$ with two additions per $\cc$.
\end{itemize}
 Once the tables have been computed,  obtaining $h_{1,\cc}(X)$ for one $\cc\in \bits^{r_2}$ costs $3\cdot 2^{r_1-2}$ table reads (one read per table $T_{A_0}, T_{S_1}, T_{A_2}$ per index $\bb\in \bits^{r_1-2}$) and $3\cdot 2^{r_1-2} +2$ additions: this is the cost of adding together the values read at each table, providing $\sum_\bb A_0[\bb,\cc]$,  $\sum_\bb S_1[\bb,\cc]$,  $\sum_\bb A_2[\bb,\cc]$, and then doing two additions to obtain $\sum_\bb A_1[\bb,\cc] = \sum_\bb S_1[\bb,\cc] -\sum_\bb A_0[\bb,\cc] - \sum_\bb A_2[\bb,\cc]$). In total, this amounts to at most  
 $3\codedim/4$ table reads and $\approx 3\codedim/4 +2\cdot \codedim_2$ additions to compute $h_{1,\cc}$ for all $\cc$, and no multiplications (in our instantiation, additions in $\comfield$ are $128$-bit XORs). 
 
 Finally, the prover computes $h_1(X)$ from the polynomials $h_{1,\cc}(X)$ with at most $5\cdot 2^{r_2}$ additions and $3\cdot 2^{r_2}$ multiplications (plus $2^{r_2}$ multiplications to precompute the values $\eq(\cc;\bm{\alpha}^{(i,2)})$, shared by all rounds of this sumcheck, and $2^{r_1-2}$ multiplications for the weights $\omega[\bb]$).
 
\bigskip

In \cref{t:gkr_step1_costs} we record the total upper bound costs for computing $h_1(X)$. In our application we obtain lower numbers since $\yy_\bb^{(0)}=1$ and we work in characteristic two, and so in \cref{e:TA2} some elements collapse to $0$. 



 On the other hand, a standard generic approach to compute $h_{1}(X)$ would require $O(\codedim)=O(\codedim_1\cdot \codedim_2)$ multiplications and additions, even if using the few  sumcheck optimizations that apply in our settings (to our knowledge) \albert{which ones, exactly?}. Crucially, we note that our sumcheck features summands of full field bit size $\log|\KK|$, which voids some known powerful optimizations when the summands are small, e.g.\ the univariate skip \cite{gruen} and some optimizations from \cite{sumcheck_opt_1,sumcheck_opt_2}.  The prover message degree reduction through factoring the ``eq factor'' from \cite{gruen} does apply to our setting, and our implementation uses it: we send $2$ elements instead of the $3$ in each sumcheck round. 

 


  %On the other hand, a standard linear-time sumcheck prover \cite{thaler} computes $h_1(X)$ with $\Theta(\codedim)=\Theta(\codedim_1\cdot \codedim_2)$ multiplications in $\KK$, several per pair of summands, for the products $L_{r_1}(\cdot,0,\cdot)\cdot L_{r_1}(\cdot,1,\cdot)$ and their $\eq$ weights. The known techniques for lowering this cost do not apply here, because the polynomials being multiplied take arbitrary values in $\KK$: the values $L_{r_1}(\bb,\cc)\in\{y^{(0)}_\bb, y^{(1)}_\bb\}$ are full $\log|\KK|$-bit field elements (the elements $g^{\bm{\gamma}_\bb}$ in our instantiation), and the entries of every higher level of the tree are products of them. The univariate skip of \cite{gruen} gains by running the first rounds over a small base field, of which there is none here, and the small-value algorithms of \cite{DT24,BDT25} require the multilinear polynomials to take small values, which they do not. (The one technique that does carry over is the degree reduction of \cite{gruen} for the public factor $\eq(X;\bm{\alpha}^{(i)})$, which we use to shorten the round messages.) What is small in our setting is not the summands but their \emph{selectors}: each summand is one of a few position-dependent constants, chosen by the witness bits at that position, and 
  %....the tables $T_{A_0}, T_{S_1}, T_{A_2}$ are a Four-Russians-style precomputation \cite{ADKF70} of every such choice. This replaces the $\Theta(\codedim)$ multiplications by $\Theta(\codedim)$ table reads and additions (XORs in our instantiation) plus $O(\codedim_1+\codedim_2)$ multiplications, cf.\ \cref{t:gkr_step1_costs}.

  %We remark that the same multiplication count for $h_1(X)$ can be obtained by grouping the summands according to the pattern of witness bits they depend on and accumulating the $\eq$ weights of each group before multiplying, which is the transpose of our tables. We present the table form because it is the one that extends to the later rounds and to the higher levels of the tree, as we show next: the tables absorb the verifier's challenges, and the bottom levels of the tree never have to be written down.


\begin{table}[H]
    \centering
    \begin{tabular}{c|c|c}
         & Our approach & Standard/generic \\
         \hline
        Multiplications & $\leq   10\cdot \codedim_1 +  4\cdot \codedim_2 $ &$\Theta(\codedim_1\cdot \codedim_2)$\\
        \hline
        Additions & $\leq 3\cdot \codedim_1\cdot \codedim_2/4 + 2\cdot\codedim_1 +  5\cdot \codedim_2$  & $\Theta(\codedim_1\cdot \codedim_2)$\\
        \hline
        Table reads & $3\cdot \codedim_1\cdot \codedim_2/4$, into tables of $6\cdot\codedim_1$ field elements & --
    \end{tabular}
    \caption{The cost in field operations of the first sumcheck round of the last sumcheck in \cref{c:gkr_grand_product} (Step 2 with $i=r_1-1$), where $\codedim=\codedim_1\cdot \codedim_2 = 2^{r_1}\cdot 2^{r_2}$ is the witness size. In our instantiation $\codedim_1\approx \codedim^{0.6} = 2^{r_1}$ and $\codedim_2\approx \codedim^{0.4}=2^{r_2}$ (\cref{s:instantiation}). %The $\Theta(\codedim)$ additions and table reads are unavoidable --- every bit of the witness must be touched --- but additions in $\comfield$ are $128$-bit XORs; the gain over the generic prover is that only $O(\codedim_1+\codedim_2)$ \emph{multiplications} are performed instead of $\Theta(\codedim)$.
    }
\label{t:gkr_step1_costs}
\end{table}





\paragraph{Subsequent prover messages in the last sumcheck in Step 2 of \cref{c:gkr_grand_product}} Next we focus on computing $h_2(X)$. Substituting $X$ by the verifier's first sumcheck challenge $\rho_1$ in \cref{e:grand_product_Lr1,e:grand_product_Lr2}, we have, for every $\bb\in \bits^{r_1-1}, \cc\in \bits^{r_2}$, 
%
\begin{align*}
L_{r_1}(\rho_1,\bb, \cc) &   =\left(L_{r_1}( 1, \bb,\cc) -  L_{r_1}( 0, \bb,\cc)\right)\cdot \rho_1 + L_{r_1}( 0, \bb,\cc)\\ &= \begin{cases}
\yy_{\bb}^{(11)}\defeq \left(\yy_{1,\bb}^{(1)} - \yy_{0,\bb}^{(1)}\right)\cdot \rho_1 + \yy_{0,\bb}^{(1)} &\text{if } \bff_{(0,\bb),\cc}=1,\ \bff_{(1,\bb),\cc}=1 \\  
\yy_{\bb}^{(10)}\defeq \left(\yy_{1,\bb}^{(0)} - \yy_{0,\bb}^{(1)}\right)\cdot \rho_1 + \yy_{0,\bb}^{(1)}&\text{if } \bff_{(0,\bb),\cc}=1,\ \bff_{(1,\bb),\cc}=0\\
\yy_{\bb}^{(01)}\defeq 
\left(\yy_{1,\bb}^{(1)} - \yy_{0,\bb}^{(0)}\right)\cdot \rho_1 + \yy_{0,\bb}^{(0)} & \text{if } \bff_{(0,\bb),\cc}=0,\ \bff_{(1,\bb),\cc}=1 \\
    \yy_{\bb}^{(00)}\defeq \left(\yy_{1,\bb}^{(0)} - \yy_{0,\bb}^{(0)}\right)\cdot \rho_1 + \yy_{0,\bb}^{(0)} &\text{if } \bff_{(0,\bb),\cc}=0,\  \bff_{(1,\bb),\cc}=0
\end{cases}
\end{align*}
%®
The idea now is that the prover can compute $h_2(X)$ in essentially  the same way as  $h_1(X)$, but this time, instead of starting with the table $$T_{r_1}^{(1)}\defeq T_{r_1}\in (\comfield^2)^{\bits^{r_1}} \quad \text{such that } T_{r_1}[\bb]=(\yy_\bb^{(0)}, \yy_{\bb}^{(1)}) \text{ and } L_{r_1}(\bb,\cc) = T_{r_1}[\bb][\ff_{\bb,\cc}], \quad  (\bb,\cc)\in \bits^{r_1}\times \bits^{r_2},$$
it starts with the table
%
$$
T_{r_1}^{(2)} \in (\comfield^4)^{\bits^{r_1-1}} \quad \text{such that } T_{r_1}^{(2)}[\bb] = ( \yy_{\bb}^{(11)}, \yy_{\bb}^{(10)},  \yy_{\bb}^{(01)},\yy_{\bb}^{(00)}) \quad \text{for } \bb\in \bits^{r_1-1}
$$
for which we have, indexing the  entries of  $T_{r_1}^{(2)}[\bb]$ with the $4$ values $(1,1), (1,0), (0,1), (0,0)$,
%
$$
L_{r_1}(\rho_1, \bb,\cc) = T_{r_1}^{(2)}[\bb][(\bff_{(0,\bb),\cc},\bff_{(1,\bb),\cc})]
$$
This allows the prover, when needed, to obtain $L_{r_1}(\rho_1,\bb,\cc)$ for any $(\bb,\cc)\in \bits^{r_1-1}\times \bits^{r_2}$ with a simple read in $T_{r_1}^{(2)}$.  

We now have, for $(\bb,\cc)\in \bits^{r_1-3}\times \bits^{r_2}$, 
 \begin{align*}A_0^{(2)}[\bb,\cc] &= L_{r_1}(\rho_1,0,\bb,0,\cc)\cdot L_{r_1}(\rho_1,0,\bb,1,\cc)\cdot\omega[\bb]\\ &= 
    T_{r_1}^{(2)}[0,\bb,0][(\bff_{(0,0,\bb,0),\cc},\bff_{(1,0,\bb,0),\cc})] \cdot    T_{r_1}^{(2)}[0,\bb, 1][(\bff_{(0,0,\bb,1),\cc}, \bff_{(1,0,\bb,1),\cc})] \cdot \omega[\bb]
\end{align*}
%
%$$
%A_0^{(2)}[\bb,\cc] = \begin{cases}
    %\yy_{0,\bb,0}^{(1)} \cdot  \yy_{0,\bb,1}^{(1)} \cdot \omega[\bb]&\text{if } \bff_{(0,\bb,0),\cc}=1,\ \bff_{(0,\bb,1),\cc}=1 \\  
    %\yy_{0,\bb,0}^{(1)} \cdot  \yy_{0,\bb,1}^{(0)}\cdot \omega[\bb]&\text{if } \bff_{(0,\bb,0),\cc}=1,\ \bff_{(0,\bb,1),\cc}=0\\
     %\yy_{0,\bb,0}^{(0)} \cdot  \yy_{0,\bb,1}^{(1)}\cdot \omega[\bb]& \text{if } \bff_{(0,\bb,0),\cc}=0,\ \bff_{(0,\bb,1),\cc}=1 \\
    %\yy_{0,\bb,0}^{(0)} \cdot  \yy_{0,\bb,1}^{(0)} \cdot  \omega[\bb] &\text{if } \bff_{(0,\bb,0),\cc}= 0,\  \bff_{(0,\bb,1),\cc}=0
%\end{cases}
%$$
where $\omega[\bb]=\eq(\rho_1, \bb;\bm{\alpha}^{(i,1)})$ is the weight defined above for $j=2$.
%
Hence the elements $A_0^{(2)}[\bb,\cc]$ can be obtained by querying a table $$T_{A_0}^{(2)}\in (\KK^{16})^{\bits^{r_1-3}}$$ with
%
$$T_{A_0}^{(2)}[\bb]= (T_{r_1}^{(2)}[0,\bb,0][\xx]\cdot T_{r_1}^{(2)}[0,\bb,1][\xx']\cdot \omega[\bb])_{\xx,\xx'\in \bits^{2}}$$
%
A  table $T_{S_1}^{(2)}$ is computed analogously to $T_{S_1}$. The value $A_2^{(2)}[\bb,\cc]$, however, now depends on the value taken by \emph{eight} witness bits, this being because for $\eps\in \bits$, the value $E_{1\eps}^{(2)}-E_{0\eps}^{(2)}$ depends on  four bits. Hence computing a table for the values $A_2^{(2)}[\bb,\cc]$ analogous to $T_{A_2}$  would have $2^8=256$-sized vectors as entries. Instead of computing $T_{A_2}^{(2)}$, we compute two tables for the values $E_{1\eps}^{(2)}-E_{0\eps}^{(2)}$ (one table for each $\eps\in\bits$, with one of the tables also including multiplication by $\omega[\bb]$). Then the value $A_2^{(2)}[\bb,\cc]$ can be obtained by performing a lookup into each table, followed by  a single multiplication.

\bigskip

This can be iterated in further sumcheck rounds, for computing $h_3(X), h_4(X),$ etc. The limitation is that the entries in the tables are vectors whose dimension is squared at each round. More precisely, each further round squares the number of cases per index of every table, but it only halves the number of indices.
Hence, in practice we stop using lookup tables after a few rounds of each sumcheck and GKR, and after that we execute the sumcheck protocol normally. Concretely, in our implementation we never compute a table with more than $16$ elements per index. This is done so that the tables can be kept in cache, since we observe that, when this is not the case, table lookups become more and more expensive compared to  multiplications in $\FF_{2^{128}}$.     \cref{tab:gkr_tables} displays the different tables built or not built in our simplest implementation, the arithmetic cost of doing so, and their size at $\codedim_1=2^{r_1}=2^{16}$ (corresponding to a total witness size of $\approx 2^{26},2^{27}$ bits in our implementation). In our final implementation we do not explicitly compute all of the tables, similarly as done with the table $T_{A_2}^{(2)}$ above. See \cref{r:factored_tables} for details.

    \centering
    \small
    \begin{tabular}{c|c|c|c|l}

        \hline
        \hline
        $T_{r_1}^{(1)}$ & $(\KK^{2})^{2^{r_1}}$ & 0/0 & $2.1$ & yes \\
        \hline
        $T_{A_0}^{(1)}$ & $(\KK^{2^{2}})^{2^{r_1-2}}$  &  $0$ / $2^{r_1 +1}$ & $1.0$ & yes \\
        \hline
        $T_{S_1}^{(1)}$ & $(\KK^{2^{2}})^{2^{r_1-2}}$  &  $0$ / $2^{r_1 +1}$ & $1.0$ & yes \\
        \hline
        $T_{A_2}^{(1)}$ & $(\KK^{2^{4}})^{2^{r_1-2}}$  &  $2^{r_1+1}$ / $5\cdot 2^{r_1}$ & $4.2$ & yes \\
        \hline
        \hline
        $T_{r_1}^{(2)}$ & $(\KK^{2^2})^{2^{r_1-1}}$ & $2^{r_1+2}$ / $2^{r_1+1}$ & $2.1$ & yes \\
        \hline
        $T_{A_0}^{(2)}$ & $(\KK^{2^{4}})^{2^{r_1-3}}$  &  $0$ / $2^{r_1 +2}$ & $2.1$ & yes \\
        \hline
        $T_{S_1}^{(2)}$ & $(\KK^{2^{4}})^{2^{r_1-3}}$  &  $0$ / $2^{r_1 +2}$ & $2.1$ & yes \\
        \hline
        $T_{A_2}^{(2)}$ & $(\KK^{2^{8}})^{2^{r_1-3}}$  &  --- & $33.6$ & no  \\
        \hline
        tables for $E^{(2)}_{1\eps}-E^{(2)}_{0\eps}$, $\eps\in\bits$ & $(\KK^{2^{4}})^{2^{r_1-3}}$ each  &  $2^{r_1+2}$ / $2^{r_1+1}$ & $2.1$ each & yes \\
        \hline
        \hline
        $T_{r_1}^{(3)}$ & $(\KK^{2^{4}})^{2^{r_1-2}}$  & $2^{r_1+3}$ / $2^{r_1+2}$ & $4.2$ & yes \\
        \hline
        $T_{A_0}^{(3)}$ & $(\KK^{2^{8}})^{2^{r_1-4}}$  &  --- & $16.8$ & no \\
        \hline
        $T_{S_1}^{(3)}$ & $(\KK^{2^{8}})^{2^{r_1-4}}$  &  --- & $16.8$ & no  \\
        \hline
        $T_{A_2}^{(3)}$ & $(\KK^{2^{16}})^{2^{r_1-4}}$  &  --- & $4295$ & no
    \end{tabular}
    \caption{Dimensions, build costs, and sizes of the tables for computing $h_1(X), h_2(X), h_3(X)$ in the last sumcheck of \cref{c:gkr_grand_product}, with $\log|\KK|\approx 128$. We use the example size $\codedim_1=2^{r_1}=2^{16}$, which, following our instantiation ($\codedim_1\approx \codedim^{0.6}$, \cref{s:instantiation}), corresponds to a total witness size $\codedim  \approx 2^{26},2^{27}$. See \cref{r:factored_tables} for the tables that our implementation does not build.}
\label{tab:gkr_tables}
\end{table}




\paragraph{The rest of sumchecks in Step 2 of \cref{c:gkr_grand_product}} So far we have discussed the last sumcheck from Step 2 of \cref{c:gkr_grand_product}, corresponding to the lower layer of the binary tree of products of our GKR circuit for the grand products. 

The sumchecks run for $i=r_1-2, r_1-3,\ldots$ can be optimized in the exact same way as we did for the last sumcheck $(i=r_1-1)$. In this case, instead of starting with the table $T_{r_1}^{(1)}$, which at each index contains the two values $(\yy_\bb^{(0)}, \yy_\bb^{(1)})$, one starts with the table
%
$$
T_{r_1-j} \in (\KK^{2^{2^{j}}})^{\bits^{r_1-j}}
$$
described in \cref{e:size_T_r1_i}. Since $T_{r_1-j}$ has, per index, the square of the number of entries of $T_{r_1-j+1}$, all subsequent tables also have, per index, the square of the number of entries of the analogous tables from the $(i+1)$-th sumcheck, and they require correspondingly more operations to compute.


In our implementation we want to keep all tables in cache (otherwise we observe that multiplication in $\FF_{2^{128}}$ is cheaper than a table lookup). Because of this, the smaller $i$ is (i.e. the further we get from the last sumcheck in the GKR protocol), the fewer number of sumcheck rounds we use tables in. Concretely, for $i=r_1-2$ ($j=1$) we build and use the table $T_{r_1-1}^{(1)}$, the analogues of $T_{A_0}^{(1)}$ and $T_{S_1}^{(1)}$ (now with $16$ entries per index), and $T_{r_1-1}^{(2)}$. Similarly as done for $A_2^{(2)}$, the analogue of $T_{A_2}^{(1)}$ (which would have $2^8$ entries per index) is replaced by two tables of $16$ entries per index and one multiplication per read. For $i=r_1-3$ $(j=2)$ we only use the table $T_{r_1-2}^{(1)}$.

\begin{remark}[Factored tables]\label{r:factored_tables}
The tables and operation counts in \cref{t:gkr_step1_costs,tab:gkr_tables} describe the algorithm as presented above. In our implementation, the four $16$-entry tables of the second round of the last sumcheck (namely $T_{A_0}^{(2)}$, $T_{S_1}^{(2)}$ and the two tables for the values $E^{(2)}_{1\eps}-E^{(2)}_{0\eps}$), their analogues in the first round of the sumcheck for $i=r_1-2$, and the table $T_{r_1-2}^{(1)}$ are not built explicitly. Instead, the prover builds smaller $4$-entry tables and recomputes each required value from two of their entries with one multiplication.%: the prover keeps only the $4$-entry tables they are products of, and recomputes the required values from their entries with one multiplication per read. This costs $\Theta(\codedim)$ additional multiplications (between $\codedim/4$ and $\codedim/2$ per sumcheck round concerned), and in exchange it saves the $O(\codedim_1)$ multiplications needed to build the tables and reduces their footprint by a factor between $2$ and $8$. As observed above, this is a net win at the sizes we target ($\codedim\geq 2^{28}$), since the prover is memory-bound and a multiplication in $\KK$ is cheaper than a data-dependent load from a table that does not fit in the L2 cache. The proofs are unaffected. We omit the details for brevity.
\end{remark}




%\paragraph{Using $4$ factor multiplication (instead of $2$) in the last step of the GKR grand product}

%\albertimportant{Actually autoresearch ended up undoing this and went back to all $2$ factors}

%In Step 2 we reduce the number of sumchecks by $1$ (so now there are $r_1-2$ sumchecks) by making the last sumcheck certify the claim
%$$
%C^{(r_1-2)}= \sum_{(\bb,\cc)\in \bits^{r_1-2}\times \bits^{r_2}} L_{r_1}(\bb,0,0,\cc) \cdot L_{r_1}(\bb,0,1,\cc)\cdot L_{r_1}(\bb,1,0,\cc)\cdot  L_{r_1}(\bb,1,1,\cc) \cdot \eq(\bb,\cc;\bm{\alpha}^{(i)})
%$$
%I.e.\ 


\begin{remark}[Lookup tables in Round 2 of \cref{c:core_iop}]
In Round 2 of \cref{c:core_iop}, the prover computes the elements $\mu_1,\ldots,\mu_{\codedim_2}$ of \eqref{e:tensored_equality} again by means of a lookup table $T$, which is indexed over  $i\in [\codedim_1/4]$ and contains the $16$-length vectors $$T[i]= (\sum_{k\in [4]} \eps_k \cdot \pi_q^{-1}(\vv^{(1)})_{4(i-1) + k})_{\eps_1,\ldots, \eps_4 \in \bits} \in \ZZ^{16}.$$
\end{remark}

\subsection{Instantiation}




\begin{remark}[The intermediate IOR $\Pi_{\KK}$ as a batched sumcheck]
\albertimportant{Scratch. Maybe remove} In many reasonable instances of $\PIMSAT$ (e.g.\ R1CS, CCS, or AIR-like instantiations), we  have that $$\pp_\bb^{(i)}(Y_1, \ldots, Y_{\inplen+\codedim}) = \pp'{}^{(i)}(\bb,Y_1, \ldots, Y_{\inplen+\codedim}) \qquad \text{for all } i\in [\iota],$$
for some polynomials $\pp'{}^{(i)}$ on $n'+\inplen+\codedim$ \albert{$\codedim_0$?} variables. 


In this case, one can take the IOR $\Pi_{\KK}$  from $\PESAT(\KK,\hat{\pp})$ to $\LIN(\KK)$ from at Step 3 of \cref{c:iop_pimsat} to consist simply in applying the batched sumcheck protocol over $\KK$ for the claims 
$$
\sum_{\bb\in \bits^{n'}}\pi(\pp'{}^{(i)})(\bb,\pi(\xx),\pi(\bff)) \cdot \eq(\bb, \rr) = \pi(u_i) \in \KK \qquad \text{for all } i\in [\iota].
$$
%
At the end of  batched sumcheck, they are left with a claim of the form
%
\begin{equation}\label{e:iop_projected_claims}
\pi(\pp'{}^{(i)})(\rr^{(1)}, \pi(\xx),\pi(\ff)) = u_i' \in \KK \qquad \text{for all } i \in [\iota]
\end{equation}
for some $\rr^{(1)} \in \KK^\nu, u_i' \in \KK$. 
%

....

...


The claim \eqref{e:iop_projected_claims} forms a valid instance-witness for the PESAT relation (cf.\ \cref{d:pesat}) over $\KK$ with polynomial constraints given by $\pi(\pp'{}^{(i)})(\rr^{(1)}, Y_1, \ldots, Y_{\inplen+\codedim})-u_i'$, i.e.\ for such constraints and corresponding parameters
%
$$
(\pi(\xx), \bot, \pi(\bff))\in \PESAT.
$$
%
 Accordingly, prover and verifier use the IOR $\Pi_{\KK}$  to reduce \eqref{e:iop_projected_claims} to 
 a claim of the form
\begin{equation}
\mle{\pi(\bff)}(\rr^{(2)}) = \mu
\end{equation}
%
for some $\rr^{(2)} \in \KK^{\log \codedim}$  and some $\mu\in \KK$. In other words, $((\rr^{(2)},\mu),\bot,\pi(\bff)) \in \Relation_{\mathsf{mle}}$ over $\KK$.
\end{remark}

\end{comment}



We implement the \ftwoz\ PCS (\cref{c:core_iop,c:LinModFieldBin}) following the guideline in \cref{s:instantiation}. We then implement an end-to-end roof system, called \ftwoz-SNARK, for integer R1CS constraints as described in \cref{s:to_snark} \albert{technical overview} (note that \ftwoz \ can be used to prove much more general constraints, such as the ones in Zinc+ \cite{zincplus}, cf.\ \cref{s:to_general_bitz} \albert{some remark in TO}). 

Following the lead of \cite{flock} and other usages of AI \cref{zkgolf} to optimize against verified specs, we  used AI to optimize the prover while keeping the verifier implementation untouched.  As such, we consider our prover fairly optimal, while  our verifier's implementation is pending significant optimization \albert{review}.



Concretely, our \ftwoz\ is instantiated with Reed-Solomon codes over $\FF_{2^{128}}$ of rate $1/2$ or $1/8$ (unless stated otherwise). As  backend of \ftwoz \ we use the Ligerito implementation from \cite{flock} with folding arity $4$. When targeting $100$ bits of security, we sample primes in the range $[2^{b-1}, 2^b-1]$ with $b=$ \albertimportant{fill in}. 



\subsection{Raw performance of the \ftwoz \ PCS for the core LinBitsRings relation}

We measure the performance of \cref{c:core_iop} for proving the Core LinBitsRing relation $\LinBitsDual(\bits, \pi, \FF_q,\cC)$ \albertimportant{Say in simple terms what the relation is} following the instantiation in \cref{s:instantiation}. Concretely, $\comfield=\FF_{2^{128}}$ and $\cC$ is a Reed-Solomon code. We set $|\FF_q|  < (2^{128}-1)/\codedim_1$ and we use the unique decoding radius as proximity parameter, so that Round 0 (OOD sampling) and Round 1 of \cref{c:core_iop} do not trigger.  Our goal for now is to measure the performance of the grand product and the WHIR/Ligerito commit and opening. In later experiments, Round 0 and Round 1 do trigger.

\subsection{RSA accumulation and Poseidon hashing}

We compare Limber \cite{limber}, Zinc+\cite{zincplus} and our \ftwoz-SNARK \ on proving the same circuit as in the Limber paper. This consists in verifying four  exponentiations modulo a $2048$-bit RSA modulus, with $352$-bit exponents, plus a Poseidon-based hash-to-prime evaluation  (see Section 7.1 of \cite{limber}). We take the corresponding R1CS constraint over the integers  from the Limber repository. See \cref{s:related_work} for further details on Limber, Zinc+, etc. 

We set \ftwoz \ at $114$ bits of security, matching Limber\footnote{Limber states in the paper that it achieves $128$ bits of security, but its fingerprint step only achieves $114$ bits of security in the implementation, because the random prime sampled to perform the fingerprint is of $128$ bits, and no other measures (like, say, grinding) are taken. \albertimportant{Double check and let the authors know.}} For the \ftwoz\ opening we use Ligerito in the unique-decoding regime (as opposed to the other benchmarks where we use the Johnson bound) at rate $1/8$ with fold arity $4$ \albertimportant{mention this elsewhere}, with grinding to achieve $114$ bits of security.

\subsection{SHA-256 hashing followed by ECDSA signature verification}

We experiment with proving the SHA-256 hash of a $4$KB message (a chain of $2^6$ SHA compressions) followed by an ECDSA signature verification on the message digest. This is a common application of proof systems in anonymous credentials. We compare \ftwoz-SNARK with Binius64 \cite{binius64}, Zinc+ \cite{zincplus}, \albertimportant{What else? Longfellow? Plonky3? Vega? Check the ethproofs dashboard. }


For \ftwoz, we  arithmetize the computation as an integer R1CS constraint with $\FF_2$-virtualization (\cref{s:to_f2_virtual}). 
The  SHA-256  constraints in particular are  an instance of the $\mathsf{CM}$ relation from \cref{e:CM_rel}, obtained using the methods from \cref{r:CM_is_NP_complete}. In \cref{tab:sha_arithmetization} we display the circuit and witness sizes achieved by our SHA-256 arithmetization, against that of binary-field native systems like Binius \cite{binius64} and Flock \cite{flock}.  

We emphasize that, as done in the next experiment \cref{s:experiments_sha_modmul}, when seeking to prove a large amount of hashes, it is preferable to arithmetize the hash over a binary field and follow the approach described in \cref{s:experiments_sha_modmul}

\subsection{Mod $2^{32}$ multiplication and SHA-256 hashing}\label{s:experiments_sha_modmul}

We prove simulataneously a large chain of SHA-256 compressions, together with a large amount of multiplications modulo $2^{32}$. In this experiment, contrary to the previous one, SHA-256 is arithmetized over a binary field, using the R1CS constraint from Binius64 \cite{binius64}. The modular multiplications are arithmetized as an R1CS constrain over the integers, where each modular multiplication  $x\cdot y = z \mod 2^{32}$ is expressed as an integer constraint of the form $x\cdot y = z + 2^{32}\cdot u$, where $u$ is a new integer witness.  

Our end-to-end \ftwoz-SNARK is built as described in \cref{s:to_snark}. We compare our results with Binius64. To arithmetize modular multiplication in Binius64 we use a gadget in the repository built for that purpose \albertimportant{double check}.

\subsection{SHA-256 hashing}\label{s:experiments_sha}


We compare Binius64 \cite{binius64}, Flock \cite{flock},  and our \ftwoz-SNARK on proving SHA-256 hashing.  


The main goal of this experiment is not to show that \ftwoz-SNARK outperforms \cite{binius64,flock} in SHA proving (it does not if the amount of hashing is very large, see below), but to show that \ftwoz-SNARK is competitive (and sometimes better) while using a natural arithmetization which only performs XOR and integer addition. As mentioned in \cref{??} \albertimportant{mention in intro}, we envision \ftwoz-SNARK being used as a proving backend for a general-purpose zk-DSL \cite{?} that is able to compile most computations of interest into a small circuit.

Our experimental results show that \ftwoz-SNARK is more performant when hashing smaller messages (up to a few KB), and becomes worse as the size of the messages grows beyond that. The size of the witness in \ftwoz\ is smaller than in \cite{binius64,flock}, .... \albertimportant{
    Continue comparison when we have the numbers. Don't forget memory consumption}


    Ultimately, for a proof system that requires proving a very large number of SHA (or similar) compressions, together with other type of arithmetic operations, e.g.\ integer or modulo $2^n$, we recommend using a hybrid arithmetization and for constraints over $\ZZ$ and binary fields (in the style of Zinc+ \cref{zincplus}), with a witness $(\bff_\ZZ, \bff_{2})$ split into a part $\bff_\ZZ$ that is used in the integer constraints, and another part $\bff_{2}$ used in the binary field constraints. Then the overall constraint is proved as: \albertimportant{Some of this could go to the intro?}
    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
        \item  Run a PIOP that is composed of a PIOP for integer constraints (as in \cref{?}) and a PIOP for binary field constraints (such as Binius64). This results in two MLE claims, one for (a projection of) $\ff_\ZZ$ over a finite field, and another for $\bff_2$ over a binary field.
        \item Use \ftwoz\ to reduce the resulting MLE evaluation claim for (a projection of) $\bff_\ZZ$ to an MLE evaluation claim over a binary field;
        \item Use WHIR or another PCS over binary fields to prove the above claim, together with (in a batched manner) the MLE evaluation claim for $\bff_2$.
    \end{enumerate}

\subsection{Integer multiplication}

\albertimportant{Add Limber to all tables. Zinc+, Plonk3. Something else?}

We measure the cost of proving $N$ integer multiplications of the form $x\cdot  y = z$, for $x,y$ random $t$-bit integers, and $z$ a $2\cdot t$-bit integer, for $t\in \{32,64,128\}$. As we mentioned, this is proved via our implemented end-to-end proof system, called \ftwoz-SNARK for R1CS over the integers, with \ftwoz\ as its PCS, as described in \cref{s:to_snark}.




\input{u32-mul-table}




We compare the performance of \ftwoz-SNARK with Binius64 \cite{binius64}, Plonky3 \cite{plonky}, Zinc+ \cite{zincplus}, and Limber \cite{limber} on the same task. Both Binius64 and Plonky3 implement gadgets for proving integer multiplication constraints. We use those in our benchmarks \albertimportant{Confirm}.

\subsection{Comparison with Fields-witch}


\subsubsection{Comparison with the field switch of \cite{lev}}\label{s:exp_fields_witch}

%\albertimportant{Review.} Soukhanov's implementation of \cite{lev}, \texttt{fields-witch}, proves the same kind of claim as \cref{c:core_iop}: a multilinear evaluation, over the prime field $\FF_{2^{127}-1}$, of data committed in $\FF_{2^{128}}$. \cref{tab:fields-witch} compares the two at equal commitment size (\texttt{fields-witch} commits $2^{m-7}$ entries of $127$ bits, \ftwoz\ commits $2^{m}$ bits) with both configured for $100$ bits of security. The cost of \texttt{fields-witch} is per $127$-bit entry regardless of the width of the data, since every intermediate fold is a full $\FF_{2^{127}-1}$ element, whereas the cost of \ftwoz\ is per committed bit; on $2^{20}$ entries of $32$-bit words, for instance, \texttt{fields-witch} still takes the $2^{27}$ row of \cref{tab:fields-witch}, while \ftwoz\ commits $2^{25}$ bits and takes $61$\,ms at $W = 32$ (with the prime width capped at $b = 83$ by the one-chunk rule $b \le 127 - \log \codedim_1 - W$; keeping $b \ge 100$ would double the grand-product cost). The proof-size gap comes from \texttt{fields-witch} committing every intermediate fold and opening three polynomials, where \ftwoz\ opens one. Its verifier is polylogarithmic, whereas ours reads the $\codedim_2$ integers $\mu_j$ and evaluates the lifted weights at $\codedim_1$ points; the two meet at $2^{29}$ bits on a single thread (not shown).


%----

We compare \ftwoz\ with Fields-Witch, the PCS over the prime field $\FF_{2^{127}-1}$, which can be found in \url{?} \albertimportant{fill in}. This PCS, similarly as \ftwoz, commits the witness over a binary field, and proves its linear claim statements in the exponent of $g\in \comfield^*$. Contrary to the flexibility of \ftwoz, Fields-Witch is restricted to proving linear claims specifically over the  prime field $\FF_{2^{127}-1}$. The method for proving the linear claims in the exponent is, as well, completely different to \ftwoz's, see \cref{?} for a brief discussion of how Fields-Witch works. 

\input{fields-witch-table}

\albertimportant{Review} Fields-witch uses the unique-decoding radius and the same ring-switch and Ligerito scheme over $\comfield$ as \ftwoz \albertimportant{confirm}. Fields-witch uses the unique decoding radius, while we use the Johnson bound \albertimportant{Why? Make them use the same?}, both schemes use rate $1/2$.   \ftwoz\ executes precisely \cref{c:core_iop} with $R=\FF_{2^{127}-1}$ \albertimportant{Make sure we include the first steps and not just the forest and opening}.
